and the book records what failed as a failure of the instrument and no entry is struck. **In the middle of it Adrian posts the held asset**: the lease on the building and the yard, the standing salvage, and a two-season named repair-crew term of two names and a mark, at **1,536 / 1,481 / 1,580**, held by the sitting steward under a seven-day renewable term, forfeit to the remedy on a breach of any charter the board has recorded, **not returnable by anybody including himself**, **entered as neither a payment nor a security because a reader cannot post security**, with the sign over the door excluded for the second time in public. His reason is given out loud and is not heroism: *I have run out of ways to hurt people by accident and this is the last one that is mine to give away.* Mara then reads the whole book in one sitting, from the eighteenth of last month to the twelfth of this month, three hours and ten minutes, six hundred people staying. Adrian reads the open page and gets `ONE USE IN THE RECORD. NOT PERFORMED. / THE TERMS ARE ENTERED. THE OBJECT WAS NOT ASKED`, entered as the first reading of the new month, and Mara says it is correct and that **the object was not asked** is the first clause in this book she did not get from anybody. The book is now the first thing in Alder Reach a stranger could use to argue both that a public route is possible and that the public man is not to be trusted, and it holds both in the same hand, and neither of them says a word about the fact that they are further apart in law and closer in practice than they have ever been.

## Rolling handoff, after Chapter 0040

Adrian is **still a Stage 1 Reader**. He cannot split, bind, transfer, renew or post security, and the held asset of the twelfth is **not** a security and **not** a stage; the entry says so in the same line as the figure. Five readings are entered in the month of the crossing and one in the new month, and Nessa Pell is the clerk who counts them. The public road is suspended, no fifth activation is on the record, and the token is behind a locked door on a seven-day term renewed four times in public, on the twenty-first, the twenty-ninth, the fifth and the twelfth. The terms-closing of the sixth is suspended; its review is called for the fifteenth. Selik's own order is under review by his own motion, on the day of the paper-levy vote, alongside a permanent-office proposal that was tabled and not adopted. A man of a protected household in the service-cut yards is in a room in the lower terrace with an ankle; the far-side mark-man on the goods-shed floor has still not decided what the job is; a woman at the far receiving ramp has not been in a household since she was nineteen and asked that the only thing in the book entered because she said nothing be that she said nothing. **A mark was given two jobs and Nessa Pell's index is the only thing standing between this city and a second version of the same failure.** The ward market funds an inspection and a public reader out of a paper levy that has been charged for eleven years for paper that does not exist. Hask Vell is exposed by a rent receipt and is not broken and is still selling honest fast claims to people who cannot read. **Teo's question is open and unanswered: who tells four thousand households, and for how long, if the crossing never opens.**

---

Batch 0005, Chapters 41-50, complete. **Volume 01 is complete.** These are compact continuity records, not substitutes for the chapter text. A full beat-by-beat account is in `state/batch-0005-summary.md`.

## Chapter 0041 - The Day the Ward Market Votes

Ivo Stenn, the ward market's counting room and the terraces, the thirteenth. The rate is put to a room in columns: **forty-one coppers a quarter** out of a four-copper levy that has produced no printed matter for eleven years - **nineteen to the warden's inspection, eleven to a public reader at the counter, six to the boards and the appointed clerk's board, two to oil, and three unassigned with the reason entered**, because a rate that pays for a thing nobody can name is the rate the market has been charging all along. **Mrs Halder and Stenn find the ledger in the same week and tell each other on the eleventh, at half past four, like two men who had opened the same box from two sides.** **Oveta Kraus moves the ward market's own motion**: the money is committed by the day, and the market pays toward no office that has no day on it. **A trader asks what happens to a held asset if the charter is never breached** - the answer is that it comes back, and *in this district nobody has ever put anything back* - and Stenn writes *no answer offered* on the slate. Stenn is asked by eleven trades to fund **nine coppers more for a standing crew at St. Orra** and **refuses in public with figures**: the crossing has no day on it, a rate paid for a thing with no day is this market's own paper levy with a different word on it, **and we may be wrong, and if we are wrong on a day we did not plan for, four people will stand at St. Orra with a lamp and tell somebody they cannot help them, and that will be our doing and not the Warden's.** The vote is by trades, each carrying its own count, read out: **the inspection carried on eleven trades and four thousand and one households, the reader carried with one abstention entered because there is no trade for the people the reader is for, six coppers carried on a nod with no count taken and that entered, the three unassigned not carried, and the nine refused** with the reason entered and entered as not a statement that the yard is not wanted. **Selik's review of his own order sits the same afternoon in the same room**, examined by the market that is paying for it, and the finding - written by the traders - is **the order is correct and the district does not want it.** He says both halves out loud in nine minutes and says he hopes he is not the one who is wrong and cannot say it. Stenn's discovery: the market can say yes to a **room** and not to a road. **The chapter ends with the market's second clerk reading a paragraph of the held-asset entry aloud, twice, to nine people at a counter**, because *not returnable to Mr. Vale by anybody* is the first news this city has had in three weeks.

## Chapter 0042 - What the Job Is

Fen Ambrose, the far receiving ramp, the fourteenth. Three hours down the river stairs and three back. **Eleven people and a dog on a goods-shed floor.** She tells the man at the chain what the job is, in words, at length: he stands at the ramp for the whole time the vehicle is on the far side; **every person who comes off it is somebody's and not a stranger's**; a decline goes in the count as a decline; **and he does not go and look for anybody, and if the bus does not come back he does not go and find out why.** It is a job where the whole of the work is standing still and counting and the whole of the danger is in a man who cannot stand still. He says **yes**, on two conditions and neither is about himself: the count is published **with the four in it**, and **nobody asks the woman by the kettle again, ever.** The count is taken in public as it is made: **seven in books, two of the four answered, one declined and the decline entered, one entered as not asked - eleven.** The cost is named aloud in front of eleven people: **the count becomes a number that cannot be checked by asking**, and a man with a lamp counting heads is the only way anybody in this city can check it, and **it is the only count here with no way to be found wrong aloud.** The **mark is retired** - the cross filed off the pin in six minutes, the ring kept, the pin put back with nothing on it, because a mark is a refusal and a refusal is permanent and what came off it was a job - and **Nessa Pell reads the index entry aloud in the yard on the fifteenth, twice, and a third time at Bett Ruhl's request.** The discovery: **a rotation of three on foot from the far ramp to the near apron does nothing**, and the charter must say that a party cut off is held, counted and not sent anywhere, and the crew of three is a count and not a rescue. The far-side responsible party goes up the stair unsolved, with Fen's name on it.

## Chapter 0043 - The Sale

Adrian Vale, Lot 17, the fifteenth, about six hundred people. **Nessa reads the mark's retirement aloud; the board sits its called review and sustains the seven items and the eighth line, refuses the fourth entry, and does not sustain item three's last sentence** - a term about a place - on the ground that a rule it cannot write is a rule it will still publish. Then **the eighth line meets the clinic and Adrian puts four ways of doing it on the crate and refuses to choose**, saying nobody in the yard should let him reinterpret his own clause. **Marda Osk names the four as three failures and one solution; Nessa Pell produces the rule** - the toll has always been charged to a person and never to a thing, and a woman with a bag is not a carriage; **the tenth hand names the cost.** The reading is entered with the cost at the front: **six of eight clinic hands each carry one cold box; a person carrying a thing cannot be the remembering passenger, cannot be the far-side responsible party, cannot stop to write, cannot be handed the box, and if they put it down it is theirs; the six are chosen by the man with the clock; and the six-hour clock starts the moment a box comes off a cart, and the boxes come off the cart in front of four hundred people.** The lot is described - one route, twelve minutes, one bearer, a toll read twice in the travellers' own words, **and no bidding, because there is nothing to bid for**. The refusal window opens and **stays open all night**; at about five o'clock **Teo says the word** and eleven minutes later **the girl of eleven who was never told the toll on the nineteenth says it again**, and **twenty-six offered, twenty-four travelling, and the sale does not stop** - the first time in this book a refusal has not stopped a room.

## Chapter 0044 - The Question Nobody Asked

Mara Quill, Lot 17, the sixteenth, about four hundred people. A charter's terms are agreed by people and the anchor is not a party, and in eight years she has never written a clause that says *and the object was asked*. **Orren Pike brings eleven lines and says the eleventh is the only one that matters.** There is no procedure and the reason there is not one is Fen's: **asking the object is not asking a person, and a record kept of the object is a record nobody in the room is protected from.** The yard builds the instrument in public and **writes the limit first**: one question, asked once, the object not asked to agree to anything, no second question until a new closing. The witnesses are **Rutt** - who stopped reading aloud on the sixth - and **Mrs Halder**, who asks what happens if the answer is a lie. Eight candidate questions are refused on the record, including the standing order with no expiry, which Mara refuses in one sentence. **The question is Teo's**, in a boy's words, unaltered. Adrian puts it, says in advance that he expects nothing and states what he wants out of it, and the answer's third line is `A REPLY GIVEN WITHOUT A QUESTION IS NOT A PROMISE. SIX WERE GIVEN TO THIS LOT. NONE OF THEM WAS A PROMISE.` The cost is nineteen minutes and an hour of not being able to tell whether a thing is nearer or further than it was. **The board refuses to let it be a revelation** - a reply given without a question was never a promise and nobody signed anything on one - **and enters that the defence is good and thin**; Sabin Tull supplies the other half, that a forged second page is not a promise either and about two hundred people have bought something on it. **Mara enters her own fear in her own hand and signs a record she cannot certify in advance.**

## Chapter 0045 - The Staging Line

Tarin Kest, the St. Orra staging yard, the seventeenth. Four hundred yards of chalked line, four hundred people, and a boy of five asleep on a coat, because **a road is a thing people bring children to see** - the best public notice anybody has written in five weeks, written by a liar on a stair. **The tenth hand brings his page and finds the first number in five weeks he cannot put on it**: twenty-six with two noes in it is not the same instrument as a number somebody said out loud. **Tarin extends Selik's far-side condition to everyone on the route who is not driving it**, and **Selik accepts and adds three terms of his own** - a line-holder named in the book, a duty to say the ballast is not ground, and **nobody past the second rail**, with **the twelfth of this month entered as the day that rule was argued with and held, by the tenth hand, and did not move.** The yard fails on a word (*urgent* eleven, *scheduled* seven, *priority* four, all entered, none adopted) and **Renn Fisk declines the word and gives a clock: six hours and forty minutes, in chalk, on six lids.** At ten past seven, to three hundred people on a line rather than nine hundred in a crowd, **Tarin says the real cost: the charter is to be performed with a person at the far end of it who has not been asked, and the only reason they are entitled to is a sheet of paper.** The line is walked. **The count is read at the near end - twenty-six offered, twenty-four travelling, two declines - and the number that has never been said aloud in a yard in this city is twenty-six, because it has a no in it.** The window stays open all day and all night.

## Chapter 0046 - The Auction

Adrian Vale, Lot 17, the eighteenth, about nine hundred people. **The release condition is read at the opening of the case by Nessa Pell, in capitals**: a returned object is not a returned vehicle, not a rescue, not evidence that anybody is alive. **Halloway Reed sells on the tram arch, in daylight, in front of everyone, and does not come into the yard** - a claim on the *next* closing, eleven coppers, toll on page one, fifteen-day labour term on page three, and the sentence *your public road is going to fail and on the morning it fails the only thing anybody can buy is speed, and I sell speed with the toll printed on the front*. He is better, he is right, **forty-one places sell**, and Adrian reads the sentence about a thing that does not exist and admits he has no answer to the rest of it. **The remedy is called, and calling it opens the sealed page, and what is behind it is a man and not a cause**; the board performs the closing **with the condition unmet**, in four reasons, the third of which is that a condition is a condition and not a consent. The witnesses are the tenth hand and **Ferrin Usk**, who accepts as a witness and not as a believer. Adrian reads the token - an hour, the third reading this month - and the object says that if it opens twice the route may be stranded and nothing on the page says it cannot. **The vehicle is the bus that came out of a service yard on its own, and the two facts are entered in two lines, on the tenth hand's demand, and the book contains no document in which they are joined.** One charter, one bearer, one activation, one term with an end, closed at twenty to five. **Out at five minutes past five with twenty-four people; the far-side holder writes twenty-four on a wall in chalk.**

## Chapter 0047 - The Term

Adrian Vale, the tram apron, the eighteenth, five minutes past five to twenty to seven. He stands on the apron from ten past five until twenty minutes to six with nothing to do for the whole of it, which is the job. **The clock is now closed and binding: the route opened at about ten past five, the term of twelve minutes expired at about twenty-two minutes past five and not before it, the vehicle was on the stamped plate at about twenty past five, the seven came off at about half past five, and the object came back on its own at about twenty minutes to six — after the limit, at the closing, which is what the release condition says.** **Rosalind Hesk, whose daughter of eight is on the far bank and not on the return party, asks him to keep it open four minutes**, because somebody told her in this yard at two o'clock that if he held it the bus could go up and take more. She did not know that holding it past the term closes the case; he tells her in four minutes, in front of nine hundred people, and is sorry for the man who cost her the hope without finding him. **The sentence the chapter rests on: the limit is the only thing in this city that anybody can check.** **Amos Treft states publicly that his cart of a hundred and seventy pounds of coal and eleven sacks of seed is not going over, and that he would have made a very good thing out of four minutes and is not calling anybody a robber** - 212 / 186 / 240, and the price entered as uncomputed and not nil. At a quarter past five **the object reports on the open page of the book**: it is failing before the term ends, the plate is parted at the rim, and it does not know whether the route will close at twelve minutes or before it. The bus comes down onto the stamped plate. **Bett Ruhl reads the release condition at the closing and cannot read and says so first.** The count: **twenty-four outward and twenty-four tolls, seven returning and seven tolls, thirty-one tolls in all, seventeen left on the far bank** - and the seventeen is the school roll's own number, entered as a coincidence. **Hester Vane comes off with a bag of seed and says she knows the road and cannot say why she got on the bus, and does not want to be kinded about.** Then **the token arrives on the crate on its own, without the bus, the wax broken again: two hundred and eleven grains before, two hundred and four after, and a chip out of the reverse the width of a thumbnail.** And at twenty to seven **Selik Marne says the batch's last hard thing: the limit was checked by one man, a limit checked by one man is a man and not a limit, Adrian should be replaced in about a year by a room, and if he cannot let them then everything this district has read for five weeks is a note from a man.** Adrian agrees it is fair, refuses to say which of his two reasons was the true one, and does not defend himself.

## Chapter 0048 - What Is Left of the Hearth Line

Tarin Kest, Lot 17, the nineteenth, six hundred people. A holding, not a hearing. **Three people try to treat a crossing that has been performed as a right, inside an hour** - a trader reselling a place, a broker's clerk offering to broker a season, and a man who got on the bus and came back and wants it written down that he did it - **and the fifth of the five things stops all three in four minutes each.** Then **the river stairs' own arithmetic**, because the footbridge's numbers belong to a plank in the service cut and Tarin strikes them off the slate before he will use one: **one at a time, three hours down and three up, in daylight; seventeen is about two and a half days; the last person on the stair is not accounted for until the morning of the third.** **The crew of three is a count and not a rescue.** There is no marshal, because a Warden's man with a lamp on a stair is a manifest; **the light is held by Nessa Pell, a clerk, who can see nine feet and cannot see the turn**, and the count is published in the morning whether it closes or not. **Joss Amery is paid** - 268 / 241 / 310, three days committed, the barrow-load uncomputed and not nil, the altered coal board entered as a fact joined to nothing - **and the reason the payment can be made at all is that he can be found, which is the only qualification he has.** Tarin finds the timetable has got a committee and says he does not know whether that is better. At ten past three, six hundred people build in nine minutes the answer to *can it be sold*: **the thing can be used and cannot be owned; the use can be sold and cannot be kept; the end can be checked and cannot be argued with.** And then **the custody term has expired at midnight and nobody noticed for twelve hours** - the lock was Nessa's and the authority was nobody's - the board renews it in public in four seconds, and **she asks for a rotation the board has no power to create, and the minute says so, and the request stands.**

## Chapter 0049 - The Damaged Token

Mara Quill, Lot 17, the twenty-first, four hundred people, and about two hundred who came because a notice said the case would be open. **Ossie Prent stands up at eleven and makes the best objection anybody has made to anything in this book, and he is right**: a case is where a thing is, a sheet travels, **the crack is the product, and a shape is the easiest thing in this city to print.** Mara agrees and answers with one thing: **a damaged object in a public case is not evidence, it is a souvenir with a lamp on it** - evidence requires a stated claim, and Lot 17's claims are unstated and uncomputed, and a child is dead with no name in any book. So the object is entered as **a lot with a condition and not a relic**, and the condition is the whole of the entry: the crack through the plate, the chip out of the reverse, **two hundred and eleven grains before and two hundred and four after**, five openings, and *it is not known whether it will open again*. **Rell Bode's objection is about the drawer**, not the remedy, and she says the answer is not enough. The board publishes a result: no expiry and no end in the entry, a seven-day term renewed in public with the hour published, inspection on stated days by anybody, a rotation it has been asked for and cannot create, and **the ward market's reader describing the object to anybody who turns up, at eleven coppers a quarter, which is what the market voted and not what a day of it is worth, and that difference is the lie in her own line.** **Mara signs the custody page with the hand that is in the spill and says so before anybody asks, and about four hundred people do not look at her hand**, and the book records that this is the fifth time and the first time it has cost anybody anything.

## Chapter 0050 - The Book, Left Open

Adrian Vale, and the room, the twenty-sixth. **The last read-back**, in a kitchen under the Ward Nine tram line, with two people who are not clerks, two hours and six minutes, the eighteenth read twice, the toll read three times at Bett Ruhl's request, and **Lina booking the autumn.** Then the yard and the key. **The board offers the key to Adrian first and he refuses it in eleven seconds**, because on the twelfth he put a thousand five hundred and thirty-six coppers into a steward's hands and if he takes the key back he took it back. **The first holder of the rotating key is Hester Vane** - a cooper's wife with a bag of seed she cannot account for - and the trader who objects is answered by her in thirty seconds: *because the last time there was a key in this lot it was in the hands of one person for five weeks, and on the nineteenth that person's term expired at midnight and nobody noticed for twelve hours.* **Selik Marne enters that he does not accept it** and will keep saying so in a public yard on every published day. **A girl of six asks what the token is worth and Adrian does not answer with a number**: who is listening, who bears it, and who may refuse - and about four hundred people go home after the child, not after him. Rutt reads the entry of the crossing at the counter and in the yard. **Mara enters the foot of the page**: the crossing and the tolls, the seventeen, the sealed page opened and the man **asked on the second of this month, told what the job was on the fourteenth, and not gone for on the day**, Hester's key and the third of the new month, **the reading ledger closed** at five in the month of the crossing and four in this month, **the counterfeit still winning on speed**, the span still shut, and a fifth line in chalk on a kitchen wall: ***THE ANSWER IS NO. AND IT IS SAID OUT LOUD. AND NO REASON IS OWED.*** And four words. **AND THIS BOOK IS OPEN.**

## Rolling handoff, after Chapter 0050 — Volume 01 complete

Adrian is **still a Stage 1 Reader** and the volume did not promote him; the entry of the twelfth still says so in the same line as the figure, and the reading of the eighteenth still cost an hour each time. The **fifth activation happened on the eighteenth of this month** and it is the only one: one bearer, one term of twelve minutes, twenty-four travellers, twenty-six offered, thirty-one tolls, seventeen left on the far bank, and the token cracked through and parted at the rim, **two hundred and four grains, seven grains gone, and nobody can say what seven grains of brass is.** The token is in a public case on a short renewable term, entered as **a lot with a condition**, inspectable on stated days, describable by a reader at a counter, and the key is held by **Hester Vane until the third of the new month**, named before she held it. **Lot 17's public lot book is open, has been read back in full twice, and is the first thing in Alder Reach a stranger can take cold and use.** The counterfeit is still winning on speed and forty-one places were sold on an honest claim in an hour. Selik's span is shut, his order is reviewed and found correct, his permanent-office proposal is tabled and alive, **his fourth term of the nineteenth is the standing safety rule of the Hearth Line, and the standing order with no expiry has not been read by anybody and is not named.** Teo's question is answered once, in six lines, by a piece of brass that was asked for the first time in its life, and the answer is that **a reply given without a question was never a promise** and that what is owed is owed to the people who paid it. A mark was retired. A count with a no in it is in the book for the first time. And the last line of the volume is four words long and it is not finished.

---

# Volume 02 — The Borrowed Road

## Chapter 0051 - The Third of the Month
Nessa Pell, Lot 17, the first and the second. The month turns and nobody notices. **Hester Vane's term is six days from the twenty-sixth of February and expires at midnight on the third, and the standing instruction says the holder on that day is to be named in the book before the key is held or it is not held - and nobody has named anybody.** The board sits at eleven on the first and lists its five powers off the wall (and **publish a result is the second**), and Sabin Tull says the board is not refusing: it is sitting there with five powers and the one it needs is a sixth, *ask a person whether they will do a job*, and if it had that power every board in this city would be a hiring office. Hester comes at half past three with the key in a coat and nine pounds of somebody else's seed still at her husband's, and **refuses in a yard of about sixty to name whoever takes it off her**: *if I am allowed to hand this key to a friend of mine in a doorway on Tuesday then this book will be a book with a drawer in it and the drawer is me.* She gives Tarin's sentence back - **a key is a smaller road** - and says what happens if nobody is named: nobody holds it, the case stays shut, the object sits on a lapsed term, **that is not a theft and it is a lapse, and it is published, and there is no second part.** The turn comes from Bett Ruhl on the step of the exchange at half past six in the morning: **the book has to have a name in it, a mark cannot carry a job, and the woman who should hold it has never been asked whether she has a name she wants in one.** **Wren Ostler**, six years at the ward market counter and in no book because nobody ever offered to enter one, is asked at a quarter to seven and gives her name with three conditions and one disclaimer - a name is a second way of being found and the second way is the one that travels; **a name in a book is not a promise to turn up and if I am late it is entered that I was late and not that I failed**; and it is not the road, not the last fare, not the bus, not any of it. **The term is shortened from seven days to four** with the reason entered out loud: *because we kept it once; a rule that has been kept once is a rule and a rule that has never been kept is a habit.* Wren's name goes in at ten past eleven and the key is handed over at ten past twelve, and the minute says the two hours and the reason, **which is that a name written in a room is not a name anybody has agreed to.** Selik argues a term checked by one board is a board and not a term; Nessa answers that what is being protected is the right of anybody to look at a thing without a schedule. The chapter closes with **Orren Pike, seventeen, asking whether the public lot book has ever had a place in it** - not a person, not a lot, not a man at a chain, **a place** - and Nessa entering it as asked and not answered and sleeving the paper at **411**, which is a page number and not the number of the trains, and he looks at it and does not correct her.

## Chapter 0052 - A Place on the Stair
Fen Ambrose and the river stairs, the third, fourth and fifth. **Two hundred and sixty-one steps, the turn at a hundred and forty, a kink at a hundred and ninety, a wall nine feet high at the turn and four at the top where the lamp-hook is, fog at about eight feet, two lamps** - and the standing failure entered in the book on the nineteenth of the month before this one: the person holding the light at the top can see nine feet up a stair and cannot see the turn. **Oswin Tace, forty-one, holds the lamps on the Ward Nine line, has never taken a count of people, only of lamps, and asks that it be said there as well as here**, and asks for one thing added: **the second lamp is the whole of the difference, there are two of them because I asked, and nobody has costed the second one.** The plan's asking step reads *anybody who does not wish to be counted will be asked* and has **no hour on it**, and that is the step nobody did. The count was moved off the far side because **Bett Ruhl said a number taken in a room where some people cannot speak is not a smaller number, it is a different number, and it is the number of the room.** The finding: **a person appears out of fog at about eight feet and the person is visible for eight feet, so the answer was not a lamp - the answer was a person at the bottom saying the name aloud while the person is still out of sight, so that the count is taken on a voice and not on a figure.** Seventeen came up, seventeen voices, seventeen figures, they matched, **and the count was right in number and wrong in identity**, because one of the seventeen lines on the roll stood for a house with two people in it and the other one of them stood at the top of a ramp in the dark two hundred and sixty-one steps below with a blanket over her shoulders. **Seventeen was published in the morning whether it closed or not, and it was correct when it was published and wrong within four hours, and the reason is underneath it and the reason is not a mistake.** On the fifth the entry is added to and the number becomes **eighteen**, on the authority of a plan with an hour in it: **a step in a plan that asks somebody a question is not a step until it has an hour and a person, and a step with an hour and a person in it is the only thing in this district that has ever worked.** Bett Ruhl reads it in a yard and stops twice, and says privately, to a cook, *That is me.* The money is **44 / NO SHEET / 58**, the middle column empty because the two blocks west of the depot are in no ward's book at all, and **a person who lives at the top of a stair that is the only way out of their own two blocks is a person the rate has never met.** The second lamp is **eleven coppers a week, unpaid and not as nil**, and Bett Ruhl distinguishes an instrument from a service in a paragraph the book prints whole. At about four in the afternoon on the fourth, on a goods-shed floor on the far bank, **a woman who has been there since the thirteenth of the month before last and has not asked anybody for three weeks** tells Fen where the water comes from: **nine miles west, a column at Sarrow Water, a siding, a hut, about thirty of us, metered to a road house in Alder Reach for as long as anybody can remember** - and the margin of that sheet is in the book with the number on the sleeve and the number on the sheet, **and it is the first time in five weeks that a question went out of this book and landed somewhere else.**

## Chapter 0053 - The Room With a Door On It
Selik Marne, the water office, the fifth. He goes up the staircase four minutes early with a book, **having arranged his whole professional life for nine years not to go into a room he knew about**, and takes three people, two of them the wrong ones, **and adds Adrian Vale in his own hand with the time and enters that the addition was his.** Four drafts: third year, fifth, eighth, and a fair copy in the tenth **that none of them knew existed**; the office *is to lapse and is to be renewed by no body and is to be continued until the crossing it administers ceases to be a crossing*; the fair copy's *for the duration of the need* with the need undefined anywhere in nineteen pages; **the closing clause that the office may close the crossing without a ground and that a closure without a ground is a safety measure and a safety measure is not subject to appeal - which is Selik's own March sentence written out as a permanent power by four men without him**; and the last page, which **cannot be ended by any body existing at the date of it and creates the body that may revoke it, consisting of the administrator and two persons named by him who hold no office, and may be dissolved only by the body which may revoke it.** The only page worth keeping is the eleventh: **a schedule of eight places with a duty against each**, written in the fifth year, the only document in nine years that is about a river, **and neither instrument the district has built can hold a river.** The name **CROSS-DISTRICT PASSAGE** appears four times as a pre-Fracture public office and is in no register anybody can reach. Selik asks the room what happens to a duty when the thing it is duty for is shut, and **Nessa Pell answers without moving, standing by a table with her hands at her sides: because nobody has written the sentence, and a duty with no end is the only thing in this basin that has never been in a book with a date on it, and the entire difficulty of that bundle of paper is that nobody wrote down what happens on Thursday.** He withdraws it - **and is refused his own instruction by his own clerk in one sentence, Ilse Markham: *then it is a paper a man took back, and in four years the district will believe a thing was never written*** - and switches to publication. On the second landing the second fitter, who suspended her own certificate and is not a Warden, tells him in fifty seconds what she thinks of the fourth page, and it becomes the front of the order: **I do not think you did it, and I do not think you would do it, and it is in a bundle, and I am going to have to look at it for the rest of my life.** **Nobody in Alder Reach ever learns who wrote the front page.** Adrian refuses to read the draft for him and says why in a yard.

## Chapter 0054 - The Hut at Sarrow Water
Sef Anyard, the ninth place, the seventh. A brick box nine feet by eleven on the cess at the top of a bank eleven feet above the river, **the ninth of twelve places on the near section.** **She has kept the hut for eleven years, she is fifty-one, and she is the second person anybody in Alder Reach has ever met who could be trusted with a pair.** The tenth hand walked the nine miles counting places and said nothing for two hours; Orren Pike came too, at seventeen, because he wanted to see a hut. Four things in a hut, and a fifth that is the water, **and the water belongs to the road house and she has a key to the column and has never once in eleven years opened it for herself, because a key to a column is a key to somebody's bill.** **A stall is her word: where the ground has stopped agreeing with itself. A train can stand in one perfectly well; what will not do is the question of where it is.** Nineteen reversals since the water came, the first two alone, **eleven of which left a stall**; the first man out from Alder Reach showed her on a page and his writing is very small. **What a reversal costs, in her words: the train comes back and knows the way and not the place, and then the wanting to know goes, because wanting is a thing you have and the thing you do is wanting.** The eleventh reversal produced a sentence from a young man she has never been able to unhear: **I know it was us. I could not have told you it was us from here. Those are two different things and I have been the second one all week.** The Tongue came out of Harrow in the fourth year when the low end was under about two feet; it is under nine feet now; **nobody has ever wanted it, and it has been in eleven huts and three sheds and a box in a wagon for eight years and in this hut for five.** The hut book has **sixty-one movements** since the water came, four of them wrong in a different ink because she found them wrong and would not rub out her own. The column headed **ORIGIN HELD** that she has kept for eleven years for no use at all, **and about four of the entries in it are not places**, one of them spelled out in full rather than contracted, which nobody does on that line except a keeper copying out of a book: **THE STANDING OFFICE OF CROSS-DISTRICT PASSAGE, ESTABLISHED, AND NOT AT THIS PLACE.** **Orren's four and hers are not the same four**, so there is a fifth in a column of sixty-one the line book has not got. The train has been on the siding **twenty-four days** and the water has been drawn off the column for **nine years** against a meter in a book in Alder Reach that nobody has ever asked to see. **The siding is not a place a train is kept; it is a place the line stops.** The chapter's last four minutes: he has been in her hut since half past nine asking about the lever, and the stall, and the book, and the column, and the train, **and has not asked her one single question about her**, and she stops answering. He asks, and she gives the number first: **twelve**, not eleven, because there is one in her hut, **it is the floor she is standing on, and she has been certain of it for eight years and has never put it in her own book, because a stall she puts in her own book is a stall she has to be asked about, and she has been afraid for eight years of being asked about it.** **The number that was said first was twelve, and the reader's book did not have it, and both are in the book and neither is struck.**

## Chapter 0055 - Nine Miles of Line
Tarin Kest, the ninth, nine miles west and back. He tells them about the lease on his own ballast at half past nine, before they are two miles out, **and neither of them says anything for another two miles, and that silence is the worst two miles of his year.** **A nine-year lease signed in the ninth year in about eleven minutes when he was thirty-four and had nineteen vehicles and nowhere to put them; it runs out in the fourth month of next year; he has not renewed it, has not told the other side, and has not told the woman on the column** - and his reason is not conscience: *the day I renew it is the day a village becomes a thing in a road house's business, and the day I stop is the day a train on a siding is a thing on a piece of somebody's ground.* The stall between the fourth and fifth place is three hundred yards long and everybody knows it is there and **nobody in the district has ever walked to the end of it.** The tenth hand walks both ends of the same one and finds **the line has got ten on it and the eleventh entry is one stall entered twice, and the woman who lives in a hut nine miles on says twelve and he says eleven and they are counting the same ground.** Tarin's arithmetic, done with a stick in the ballast in eleven minutes: **a line is an order and not a length** - you cannot ask how long it is, the answer changes with what is standing on it, the only true question is *in what order*, and you answer it from the east. **The train cannot come east, because to turn it the signal has to take the origin, and the origin of that train is the siding it is standing on.** And the part that frightens him: **every time somebody turns a train on this line the line gets a bit less, and nobody has ever written down that it gets less, because the book counts movements and a movement is not a hole.** He opens a second column on his page called *rolling stock standing* and refuses to put a train on the page of people, **because if he did, a stranger taking the page cold would think a train is a thing this district counts, and it is the only thing on his page he knows is not a place a train could be.** Selik's standing rule comes off the ballast at half past eleven and **does not survive the first curve**: the second rail on a straight is four feet from a man and on a curve it is the inside rail, a foot and a half, with the ballast up to his chest - **so the rule transfers and the distance does not, and he is not going to pretend a version is a version.** The tenth hand, who is the man allowed to argue it because he argued it once, writes the finding: **a rule that transfers is not a rule, it is a habit with a new address.** An eastbound empty goes through the stall thirty feet from a man's boot in about twenty-eight seconds. **It was three hundred and ten yards on Friday. It is three hundred and forty-four now.** And the whole of it: **the line is not a list of places, the line is an order and it is getting worse as it is used, and every instrument in this district is a count of what there is and none of them is a count of what is left.** The driver, Osgood Trill, thirty, white, four lines in his own words: **I came through it and I came out of it and I know what I came out of, which is a place with a lamp on it, and I cannot tell you what I came out of. Not the lamp. The stretch.** And: **I would like it noted that I did not stop.** At the ninth place the woman at the column is about fifty, **and tells Tarin he is the fourth man up that bank this year and the first three did not come up it, and it looks like a lorry to a river, and she agrees that it does.** She asks him the only question she has: **is it going to be moved in the night?** He says he does not know, says what he does know, **and says the harder half: that in every one of the nineteen takings this place was not asked, and that he signed the paper, and that he has been carrying it for eight years.** Nobody accuses him. He is asked by Orren whether the Road House would put a man on a reopened span, and says **no, in advance, and would like it in the book that he said it before a train had been late.**

## Chapter 0056 - The Tongue of Harrow
Adrian Vale, the eleventh and twelfth. He has not read since the eighteenth of last month, and the reason he had not was not a rule and not a limit: **on the eighteenth he read a piece of brass twice, an hour each time, and the second hour left him unable to tell whether a thing was nearer or further than it had been, and he sat on a crate afterwards and worked his way back into the ordinary world by counting the boards, and the counting took nine minutes and he never wrote the nine minutes down, and that was the private part of it and the private part was the reason.** He goes out to the hut with a satchel and tells Sef Anyard on the doorstep what he is going to do, **which is the practice now and is not a courtesy, and is the whole of the change in him that five weeks has made**: an hour, on her table, her book shut, and afterwards he will not be able to do some ordinary things, will not be able to hold a pair, is not holding one, is not driving, is not a witness on anything. She says do it at my table and I will bring you back and I will not make a thing of it. The reading is entered at Lot 17 on the twelfth **on page eleven, from a sheet made at a kitchen table nine miles west, by two people and not by him**, with the sleeve number on the sheet: **USE, send one vehicle back along the section it came from, one movement, one way, the use is not given back. TOLL: THE ORIGIN. STANDING: A PERSON WHO CAN HOLD A SIGNAL.** And the finding: **the object does not hold places; the toll is not stated in a form that can be read at a person, a household, a town or a water; the object does not say what the origin is; asked for more it repeats itself.** And: **eleven uses are on this line and the object does not count them and does not know them and has never once been asked.** The limit entered with it: **not to read again before the eighteenth**, and the reason in his own words, that the last reading cost two hours and he has not found out whether this one cost him something he has not noticed. In a yard of about six hundred he gives **three** readings of one word and refuses to choose: the place the vehicle is standing; the first place it was entered into the line's book; **the settlement whose hut knows about it - which is his, and which he wants struck, and which cannot be, so he says it and lets the room decide.** A fourth comes off the floor from **a carter of about fifty** - the origin of a thing is the place you can trace it back to, and a thing that cannot be traced back has not got one - **and Adrian says it is not one of his and it is worse than all three of his, and the yard makes a sound, and he does not fill the silence.** The standing eighth line is found to have been written about a bus: **the line says the only carriage is a person carried as a person, and this is not a carriage, it is a set of points in a loop with a lever, and what it moves is wagons, and there is nobody in the use at all.** Then the tenth hand asks the only question in nine seconds - **if a person is carried as a person, what does a person pay when a train is turned round?** - and the answer is nothing, **and the finding of the twelfth is that the passengers are free and the place is charged, and we have no instrument in this district that can be pointed at a place.** The satchel is refused three times in six minutes: there is no custody rule for moving a used object with a working standing; **the tongue is the only thing at that hut that throws the points, so that siding is not a train that is parked, it is the west end of the line**; and **it is in daily use by a person who is in no book, and there is no custody of it because there is no gap.** Mara Quill finds the shape of it: **a lot in daily use by a person who lives with it is a custody that cannot be inspected, cannot be described at a counter, and cannot expire, and a custody that cannot expire is a hiding place with a form on it.** And the new category, named on the spot by somebody who was not the man who asked for it: **A READING WITH NO LOT** - entered with the sheet, the sleeve number, the reader, the hour, the duration and two people who compared the hand, and **not evidence, not a relic, not a charter, and the first instrument in this district's history that is about a thing and not about anybody.** The echo: for about fifty minutes he could not say which way the river went, and Sef Anyard tells him he has got about forty minutes and it comes back in the order it went, and then tells him she has stood where he is standing and could not tell either, **and did not have to tell him, and did not want to be asked, and wants one line on a page somewhere that says the keeper cannot tell which way the river went either.** He walks the nine miles back in the dark with Orren, four hours and ten minutes, **and the tenth hand goes as far as the fourth place and turns back, because a count with a man in it who cannot see is not a smaller count, it is a different number.** At the door he asks the question he should have asked on the seventh: **did you want to talk about it?** No. **I wanted somebody else to come.** Come on the fifteenth, and bring somebody who is not a reader, and she gives the reason: **a district that can only hear about a village from a man who puts numbers on things is not a district that has heard about a village.**

## Chapter 0057 - The Word for a Train
Ivo Stenn, the ward market counting room, the thirteenth. **The nine coppers he refused in the third week of last month come back to his own fourth column by themselves, because a date has arrived**, and he has spent a week doing the arithmetic a man does when he has already decided. The rate goes from forty-one to **fifty coppers a quarter over four thousand and one households, in five columns**, and the fifth is nine coppers for a lamp and a bushel of chalk at the ninth place. **A rate buys a service and does not buy a seat.** Mrs Krell objects that she has four thousand and one households behind her and not one of them walks nine miles to look at a signal; Stenn's answer is that **this is the first entry in this market's book that is not a service for anybody's street, and he has put it to her anyway, and the reason goes in before anybody votes.** The hauliers say yes in four minutes, **because the ninth place is where they park their stock, and the lease that caused the problem was signed by a man who is not in the room** - and the entry reads *the trade that caused the problem ought to be the first to put its hand up and not the last*. One trade abstained, and the abstention is entered because there is no trade for the people the reader is for. **Five words for scarcity are proposed and none is adopted** - *first come*, *by need*, *by standing*, *next out*, *traffic* - and the reason none works is now the entry: **it is not that we have not got a word, it is that we have got one word and there are two different things being scarce, and they are shaped nothing like each other.** A medicine run's scarce thing is a place on a vehicle and a man with a clock can order it; a railway's scarce thing is a **direction**, and the scarcer direction on the Needle is the one going west, **and there is nobody at the west end of it because the west end is under nine feet of water** - and the finding is that **the hardest scarcity in this district is a direction nobody wants, and the district spent a month failing to name a queue for a hole.** What worked instead is a working page with no heading whose first line is **A TRAIN THAT CANNOT BE MOVED IS NOT WAITING. A TRAIN THAT CANNOT BE MOVED IS THE END OF THE LINE.** Halloway Reed's timetable goes on the table with the toll in its own column **so a woman who cannot read a sentence can compare two**, printed on the ward market's own blank claim stock, honest on its face, and Stenn's verdict is entered in full. Rutt's counter breaks in public: **she can read a claim and she cannot read an absence**, and a woman of sixty with a stick asks her what is true. Stenn takes on the rate sheet for ground nobody has claimed, **says he will have to walk onto it and count households, and does not like it.**

## Chapter 0058 - The Village That Is Not a Stop
Teo, and Rutt, the fifteenth and sixteenth. Rutt's shoulder has been bad since the flood year and it is the reason she is a reader at a counter instead of a clerk at the signal cabin, **a job she has wanted for eleven years**, and a reader's term is four days a week at eleven coppers a quarter **and the term does not have a clause for a shoulder.** She goes because the keeper asked for somebody who is not a reader, and because a woman at the counter said: **then send the boy who asked the question in a school gym, he is the only person in this district who has ever asked a question out loud and got an answer he did not like.** They leave at twenty past five with a bag of the ward market's own blank claim stock, **because a sheet of paper with nothing on it is a thing you can hand to a woman.** At the fourth place the lamp is not lit, and Rutt's reason is a keeper's: **a ground signal is a lamp you leave burning for something that is coming, and there is nothing coming from the west end, and a man who kept that lamp burning for eleven years would be burning oil to tell a train that does not exist that the line is clear.** Rutt reads the origin column aloud on a hut step to fourteen people, and by the end there are thirty-one and a dog, **and the dog is not a figure and is not in the count and she says so aloud.** **The book is indexed to the nine hundred and sixth movement since the flood year, and one hundred and forty-one of them name this place as the origin held.** And the finding she finds by accident because she read the column out loud where the people could hear her: **the line has nine places with platforms and in nine years this place has had no passenger stop on it, and it is named more times than any place on the line except Alder Reach and the fourth place, and the reason is not a mystery** - the west end is under water and the Alder Reach yards are full, **so almost everything that comes and goes on this line is standing on this siding, and the line book counts movements and not passengers, and a movement is not a place, and a place is not a victim, and nothing in the column asked the question.** Teo asks *where do the trains come from*, and then the real one, and asks it eleven times because the first is a question about a railway and the second is a question about people. **Asked of thirty-one: answered by twenty-three, not asked by eight, not asked is in the count and is not a hole and is neither a yes nor a no. The count of yeses is one**, and the one is a woman of about sixty-one who walked to Alder Reach in the flood year on the footbridge with a child and did not go by train, **and is entered as a departure because she left.** The twenty-three reasons are printed at length, and one of them is the only answer in twenty-three that contains a person who left and cannot be accounted for. **Four people gave the answer that is the answer: I HAVE A BROTHER AND I CANNOT FEEL THAT HE WENT.** The count is taken out loud on the bank and it is **four households and a chapel, because that is what the form asks for**, and the woman of sixty-one says the sentence the volume is built out of: **we have never once been a household, we are a place, and you cannot lose what was never entered, and that is not a mercy, that is a hole with a shape of our own making.** A boy of nine asks Sef Anyard what Harrow was like before, **and the answer is a fella with a barrow who said good afternoon, and it is the only local knowledge in the book.** Then the boy says the word, a third time, about himself, and nobody asks him for a reason. **She tells him exactly what it cost** - a boy of fifteen who cannot be asked to do a thing for a year, in a village of thirty-one people, on a line with no timetable, and the next time that bank moves at night the only people who will be asked are the woman of forty-five and the man of twenty-six and her - **and it is a job you have made unavailable, and I would like you to be fifteen about it for one more day and then be as angry as you like, because there is a train on my bank and I am not sentimental.** He says: **it's mine to pay.** He comes back nineteen miles in the wet with both bags, nobody thanks him, he has said he will not expect it, and Bett Ruhl has said she would have been insulted if anybody tried. His page carries nine words and a request - **the sentence to go in as the person's own words and not mine** - and the second half of his old question, which nobody asked for. **Nessa Pell will not put a question in the book. She puts the nine words in, and the count in, and the twenty-three reasons in at length, because that is the only part anybody will be able to check.**

## Chapter 0059 - A Destination Can Be a Victim
Adrian Vale, the eighteenth, in a yard of about five hundred people, with three sheets on the wall that have not all been on the same wall a fortnight ago. A trader asks what happens **today.** Adrian says no, and then says the harder no: **I cannot tell you a date and I have not read anything since the eleventh of this month, and I am not going to read anything today** - and gives the reason in his own words: **six days is not long enough for me to have learned anything and it is long enough for me to have stopped; every time I put my hand on that piece of iron I get five lines that say the toll is the origin and not a word about whose; if I read it again I will get better five lines, and better five lines will make me feel as though I know something, and I do not know something, and I have been selling the feeling for five weeks and I am not going to do it in front of a village.** **Nineteen minutes** goes in the ledger, and the entry reads *the reader's limit, entered as the eighteenth; he did not read; no cost; no time; the entry is of a non-event and is entered as one.* Then he puts every honest option on the crate and refuses to choose between them, and **all three fail correctly.** The proposal schedule is for a use somebody is proposing, and **nothing at the ninth place is about to happen; it has already happened nineteen times, and the schedule has nothing to point at.** The no-name remedy is a remedy for a breach, and **there is no charter, therefore no breach, therefore nothing to remedy, and every one of the nineteen takings was the correct operation of an object that nobody has ever bought** - and Sarrow Water has refused nothing, because **a person who was never asked has not refused, and a remedy for people who say no has nothing to say about a place nobody offered to say yes to.** And the third, the one that cost him the most: **the count rule is ours, all four lines are mine, I wrote them at a kitchen table in the second week of last month with a candle on it and a yard of about six hundred agreed to them in the rain, and I voted for them** - and **the district's central institutional gain is the reason a village is invisible, and the woman who found it out is the one who is in the form, and she was not in the room when I wrote them.** Then the sentence, and it does not go up the way a sentence goes up in a yard: **A DESTINATION CAN BE A VICTIM TOO.** Every instrument this district has built points at a person or a lot, a toll on that iron is charged to **a place**, and there is no column for a place anywhere in eleven boards and nine copies. **And I have been standing in this yard for five weeks saying that the charge is the person, and it is a rule about roads I built and not a law. I did not know what else to charge. That is the whole of it. I did not know what else to charge and I did not ask.** He makes a payment and not a security: **1,190 / NO SHEET / 1,430**, and the middle column says *no sheet* for two reasons, both entered, and **the shortfall is uncomputed and not as nil, because a nil would be a number the payer chose**, and the market is told before the money moves. Then a trader of about forty stands up in the fourth row and says the word on behalf of Sarrow Water four times, and about four hundred people agree with him out loud, and Adrian stops him - **not because he is wrong, but because the only act this district has made holy is the act that puts nine vehicles on top of the wall behind that column, where a man of seventy looks at it every evening and has seen a bank go before** - and the finding is the fourth instrument the district does not have: **saying no has never once been free, and it has never been free because nobody ever costed where the thing goes next.** Bett Ruhl claims the line for her kitchen wall. **Selik Marne says the west end should be closed**, and nobody argues with him about whether it is safe, and closing it strands nine vehicles on a village's bank for ever, **and he says that being right is the thing he has never once in nine years been able to explain to anybody**, and that **he will not be the man who does it, because it is a Warden's order and this is a public lot, and if it is done it will be done by a room of four seats with no owner and not by me.** The route-keeper remedy, written in Volume 01 and never once called, **is attempted for the first time and works completely in eleven minutes for the nine vehicles**, because a train is the easiest thing in the world to point at - **and is not discharged for thirty-one people, and the reason is not the protected category: there is no charter, therefore no breach, therefore nothing to remedy.** Then the board **publishes a result** - the second of the five things it can do - stopping the lot: **no charter, no terms-closing, no payment and no reading may be attached to the Harrow Tongue until the place its toll names has been asked, and the lot is therefore stopped by a room of four seats with no owner and not by the reader, and the reader asked for it to be stopped by the board and was refused the first time and accepted the second time in the same minute.** In the afternoon, alone, the tenth hand's margin gets the last line: **he did not read. Six days.** And the yard empties, and **the wall has three sheets on it and a hole where a fourth would go, and the twelfth stall is a floor a woman stands on every day of her life, and it was measured on the seventh and has not been measured since, and there is a date in this book on which somebody has to go and measure it, and there is not one yet, and that is the first thing in five weeks this district has not been able to write down.**

## Chapter 0060 - A Result, Published
Mara Quill, the twenty-first and the twenty-second. Two hours and fifty minutes, all four seats named aloud, **eleven parts, the registrar's own numbering, and she has said that in four years somebody will find the numbering and think it was always there.** It is read at a ward market counter on the morning of the twenty-second in words people did not bring with them, and again in a yard in the afternoon to about four hundred people in a warm rain. Part one, the lot. Part two, the toll, and **the object does not hold places and will not name one, and that is entered as a finding and not as a conclusion.** Part three, the standing, and the one person found, asked, and recorded with the hour. Part four, the count: **entered as four households and a chapel, because the form asks for a household, and the form is wrong, and the form is not struck.** Part five, the reasons, printed at length, **because ordinary reasons are the ones a stranger will not believe.** Part six, the stalls, **twelve on the keeper's count and eleven in the reader's book, both in the book and neither struck**, and the fourth-place stall at 344 and 361 with nobody able to say which is wrong. Part seven, the remedy, discharged for the train and not for the village. Part eight, the standing order of the ninth in four versions, **which cannot be ended by any body and which creates the body that may end it** - asked of the ward market by the twenty-second, **not answered in this record and not pretended to be answered.** Part nine is the only part anybody who was not a clerk asked for, and it is the name: **it appears in two documents in this district, it is the head of a draft in a room that had a door on it cited four times in nineteen pages, and it is in a column headed ORIGIN HELD in a keeper's own handbook four times, and it is spelled out in full both times, and it is the name of an office and not of a place, and it is not a guild** - and the two are kept in two lines at the tenth hand's asking, with the reason entered. Then the thing that took the other fifty minutes and is **not in the eleven parts, because the board cannot do it, and it is the first real gap in this district's constitution**: Nessa stops the morning and says publish a result about an object nobody can look at, or build the thing that lets a public lot hold a thing that is in use; and Mara says **we cannot**, and then names it: **a lot in daily use by a person who lives with it is a custody that cannot expire, and a custody that cannot expire is a hiding place with a form on it** - a sentence she wrote in Volume 01 about a different object and did not see for a month, **because it was in a case, and a case looks like custody, and a bench in a hut does not.** Part ten is the **remote custody**, worse on purpose: the keeper named and not an officer and not a seat, asked and said yes with the hour recorded, **asked again on the twenty-ninth with the answer recorded whatever it is, including no**; the condition published in full and measurable by anybody who walks out; **no case, no counter, no key**; the term the keeper's and not the board's; and the whole entry saying that **this cannot be checked without walking nine miles and the nine miles is the point**, with Rell Bode's objection entered twice again without an answer. Part eleven is the only page nobody argued about: **what this district has no instrument for** - a column for a place, a rate sheet for ground nobody has claimed, a way of saying what is not going to happen, a cost for a refusal, **and a way of saying that a toll lands on a place, which is the fifth, which nobody has a name for, and which is the one that matters.** Then the date goes up on the wall: **the west end is to be walked on the twenty-ninth, from the fourth place to the ninth, on foot, on the ballast, in daylight, nobody past the second rail on a straight and nobody inside the four-foot on a curve, four people - T. Kest, the tenth hand, I. Stenn, and a person of the ninth place who has asked to go and has not been asked yet what she is going for.** The stall at the ninth place is to be measured, the one at the fourth measured again, the counts taken going and coming - and at the bottom, in the same type, on the face of it: **THIS IS A DATE A PERSON COULD BE WRONG ABOUT. NOBODY HAS ASKED FOR IT AND IT HAS NOT BEEN ASKED FOR. IT IS THE FIRST DATE THIS DISTRICT HAS GIVEN ANYBODY.** Bett Ruhl reads it twice to about four hundred people, and Adrian at the back says it is the answer to a boy's question in a school gym and a bad answer and the only one available, **and then says the other half, which is that he is not on the walk, because the sheet says the stall is to be measured by somebody who is not the reader, and a man who has been that man once does not get to be the counter on the second occasion** - and Mara, who heard him, says nothing at all. Then the yard does the one thing it has never done on a published day: **it goes and finds out who the fourth person is and asks her, in front of about four hundred people, what she was going for** - and she dictates her own clause: **the person who says the word at the ninth place is a person, and the word is a word, and it is not a form and it is not a signature, and whoever holds the thing that day has to stop and wait, and that is all.** Nobody asked her name and the entry says she was not asked and not asked is in the count. Halloway sells about two hundred and six places on the walk at four coppers, honestly, before the yard empties, and Stenn puts the fact that the counterfeit got there first with a price into a column. The key turns, and the four days nobody held it at all are entered in the same breath as the rotation, **as a lapse and not as a theft**; **Mrs Halder** is named on the fifteenth, holds from the seventeenth, and will not hold it alone, and nobody has the power to give her a second person, and that is entered as a standing failure. Selik says **that is not enough** from the rail. And the last entry of the day: **the twelfth stall has not been measured since the seventh, the train is on the bank, the toll is the origin, the origin is a place, the place has been asked one question by one woman and has said it would say no, nobody has been paid anything, the rate sheet does not exist, the standing order is on the wall and has not been answered, the name is in two lines and not joined, and the book has a hole in it, and the hole is the shape of a place, and the hole is dated, and the first thing the district has ever done with a hole is give it a day.**

## Rolling handoff, after Chapter 0060 — Volume 02 Batch 0001 complete

Adrian is **still a Stage 1 Reader** and the volume has not promoted him. He read the Harrow Tongue **once**, on the eleventh of March, in about an hour, at a kitchen table nine miles west, from a sheet that went into a sleeve and was entered at Lot 17 on the twelfth by two people comparing the hand. **The month closes with one reading in it.** The echo of an origin toll is the loss of the sense of which way a river went, for about fifty minutes, and a keeper who has stood where he stood and could not tell either. **On the eighteenth, the day his own limit permitted a reading, he did not read, in front of about five hundred people, and the interval since the eleventh is six days** - and a refusal to read is entered, with no cost and no time, and is neither cowardice nor discipline. He admitted in public that the rule he spent five weeks teaching, that the charge is the person, **is a rule about roads he built and not a law, and that he did not know what else to charge and did not ask.** The lot is **stopped by a room of four seats with no owner until the place its toll names has been asked.** A village of thirty-one people has been asked one question by one woman and has said it would say no. The key rotated, lapsed for four days with nobody holding it, and is now with Mrs Halder, who will not open the case alone and cannot get a second person. The rate is fifty coppers with a fifth column for a lamp and a bushel of chalk nine miles west. **Nobody has been paid anything.** The standing order is on the wall in four versions and is unanswered. The office's name is in two documents in two rooms in two lines and is not joined. **The first date this district has ever given anybody is the twenty-ninth**, on a sheet that says on its face that it is a date a person could be wrong about, and four people are going, and one of them is a woman nobody has asked the name of. And the book has a hole in it and the hole is the shape of a place.

## Chapter 0061 - The Six-Foot
The tenth hand of the signal crew, the twenty-second, the first rehearsal anybody in this district has ever held for a date it gave itself. **It opens on a man being told *don't* while he chalks a rail, and the four minutes are the whole chapter in embryo.** The walk as written **cannot be done**: the sheet says on the ballast, and the ballast on a curve is the four-foot, and the fourth place is a curve, and the sheet also says nobody inside the four-foot on a curve - so the sheet forbids the walk at the first step. **He put the word in and read it out in a yard in the rain and did not see it.** Tarin Kest gives the answer: **the six-foot, the shoulder, and the cess where the six-foot is gone**, and it climbs on a curve and is stepped in places with old setts. **Four changeovers between the fourth place and the ninth** - standing water, a brick culvert with eleven inches of silt, **a length of green pipe lying across the cess at the seventh with one end in the ballast and one end in the air**, and a step down of two feet six inches into a foot of standing water at the eighth with a nine-foot drop off the shoulder, where the woman of forty-five says the sentence that is printed in her words: **nobody at the eighth goes down there, and I walk facing the drop, and a man of forty-three in good boots says the same thing and a woman of forty-five in walking shoes needs it printed.** **A boundary post is found on the embankment between the eighth and the ninth: octagonal, a foot and a half out of the concrete, a mile and three quarters cut into the top, which is the line book's own figure for the ninth place, and nobody has ever compared a post with a book.** It is a mark. **A mark may not carry a job, holding a boundary is a job, so the post cannot hold it - and it has been holding it for forty-odd years and nobody noticed, because the only job it was holding was a number and a number is not a job.** At the sixth place they stand in four inches of brown water coming out of a drain off the top of the bank, **which is the water that has made the ground behind the column the best ground for two miles and has been going onto the ballast since before the Fracture, and a signal clerk who has walked past it eleven times has never looked down because there is nothing on a signal to see.** The fourth-place stall measures **three hundred and sixty-one yards again**, the same as the eighteenth, and the tenth hand's finding is that **a number that repeats is a number anybody can check** and he says it twice out loud before he writes it. Stenn discovers he does not know how to ask a man how many people are in his house without insulting both of them, and is told: **ask us what we are.** **Forty-six days** on the siding, counted off a chapel calendar by the woman of forty-five, **the only number in that place anybody has got right in nine years.** The second version of the sheet goes up, **the first version is not struck**, and the entry says the first version contradicted itself in two places on the same morning and was written by somebody who had never stood on a curve, **and that is the first entry in the book that is about a person, which is me.** Closes on the **second lapse of the custody key in a month** and nine words: **a rotation is only real when it turns; a board that asks at the end of a term has not asked in time.**

## Chapter 0062 - The Meter Book
Ivo Stenn, the twenty-third and twenty-fourth, and a document nobody expected. **He asks for nine books, is refused the third by the person entitled to refuse it, and gets one - the Road House meter book.** No ward roll exists for the two blocks west of the depot because the surveyor got to the bottom of the stair and could not turn round: *no cart road. did not ascend.* No household book exists for the ninth place, **and there is not one now, and the absence is the sheet's fault and not the village's.** A third book is **refused by the woman of sixty-one** - *you want a list of who lives where, and I can give you that, and if you take it away from me in a new shape then somebody is going to lose a door they know* - and Stenn enters the refusal and then asks whether the reason holds, **and it does.** The fourth is the **Needle Road House meter book**, handed over unasked by Tarin Kest in one sentence: **it is thirty-six coppers for nine years and I have never known what it was worth because it was on an invoice.** The two blocks counted by hand: **twenty-two people, eleven rooms, six occupied.** At the fourth house the woman of sixty-one tells a freight clerk what to put in a rate sheet: **put the place.** **The first line of the sheet: a rate sheet is a document that says a place is a place, and the lines under it are about money and the first line is not.** The columns: the ninth place four households at **two hundred coppers a quarter**, the two blocks six at **three hundred**, the whole of the ground nobody has claimed **five hundred**. **The finding, and it is the reason nobody ever asked anybody at Sarrow Water anything: the ninth place is worth two hundred a quarter and the cost of having been asked twice is already at two hundred and four, and a place that cannot pay for its own instruments cannot be put on a sheet without making the sheet wrong, and this is not prejudice - prejudice would have been cheaper.** The meter: **four coppers a quarter, thirty-six readings, thirty-six coppers in nine years, and this market has charged the same four coppers for eleven years for a sheet it never printed.** The chandler demands the other half and gets it: **the figure is small because nobody has ever said what the place is for.** The line book is copied onto the sheet and **Orren Pike's four non-place entries and Sef Anyard's four go on in two lines and are not joined, and Stenn says he will use the boy's reason and cite him, and that citing him will have to be paid for.** Published on the twenty-eighth. **Five hundred coppers is entered and not collected, because the quarter began on the first and a market cannot go back, and nobody has been paid anything again.** Closes with the tenth hand's margin under the two entries nineteen days apart that both say it: **I have got a page of my own for numbers I cannot put anywhere. This is a pair of them and I have not got a box for a pair.**

## Chapter 0063 - The Answer, and the Figure
Mara Quill, the ward market's counting room, the twenty-fifth, and it opens on a haulier standing in a doorway for eleven minutes because **the market's rule gives a trade four days to answer a question and today is the fourth day.** The market is **three days late, and the lateness is entered, and the entry says that a rule which gives a market four days to answer a question about the safety of a crossing has been used for the first time on a question about the safety of a crossing**, and the clerk did not find it funny and the minute does not say so. **Seven to three, one abstaining, and the count each trade carries is not read out, because the market has never read it out on a question that was not a rate - and that omission is entered, because the district's own answer to this document is four batches away and the count will be read then.** **The figure: the eighth page of the standing order is a schedule of eight places with a duty against each, and at eleven coppers a quarter a standing post that is eighty-eight coppers a quarter against a take of two hundred thousand and fifty, so the market could pay that office more than two thousand times over and is voting against it, and the figure is entered because a figure that goes against a decision is the only thing that keeps a decision from becoming a feeling.** **The market's reason, in the secretary's own hand: a rate buys a service and does not buy a seat;
