an office is a seat with a duty against it; and a seat that may be filled and cannot be emptied is a condition, and this market has spent eleven years paying for a condition and calling it a service and it is not doing it twice on purpose with a river.** What it will fund instead: **six coppers a quarter for printed matter**, three out of the boards and three out of the oil. **A cooper off the lower terrace makes the only technical objection anybody in Alder Reach has made to that document in nine years: the eighth of the eight places on page eleven is Harrow, and Harrow is under nine feet of water, and the market cannot amend it, because a body that amends a document which may be dissolved only by itself is the body that revokes it, and the moment the room writes a word on it the order is void and the seven other pages go with it.** **Both halves of the answer are correct and cannot both be acted on, and that is why it is a loss and not a refusal.** **Mara Quill enters a market's decision into the Lot 17 book for the first time in the eight years she has held that lot and has to write two reasons** - the market's, which is good, **and hers, which is that a market cannot be the body that answers a standing order, because a market is a claim that everybody is responsible, and this district has spent five weeks learning that the difference between that and a lot is the difference between a lot and a queue** - and she prints her own worse reason in public because she is a registrar and does not have opinions and has got one. **On the twenty-fifth the reader was permitted to read and did not**, with no cost and no time, **and the month's ledger closes at one for the second time in the same month.** Stenn tells him a no gets more expensive every time anybody sees it and that somebody is going to call it a practice, and Adrian says **he does not know what it is and thinks it is a man looking for a door**, and notes that he has never once said the same reason twice; Stenn says he has been right four times this month and does not know what he is for, and that is the first time in six years it has happened to him. **A woman of sixty-one tells the keeper what a number on a village feels like: it feels like being counted. Nobody has ever counted us. We have been a thing that people have had opinions about for nine years, and a thing is not a thing that gets counted.** Selik is right twice in the afternoon - **the office is a better paper than the timetable and only one of the two can go on a rate sheet and it is not his** - and says from the rail that that is not enough, and entered that he does not accept the answer.

## Chapter 0064 - The Fare Is Complete
Rutt at the ward market counter, the twenty-sixth and twenty-seventh, and **it opens on an hour and it is the batch's conversation chapter.** At ten past six a printer arrives at a counter he has never come to in six years and asks for it entered that he came. She reads his sheet out, twice, all four columns and the bottom. **The bottom: there is no such timetable, this is a claim upon a closing that has not been called, you pay four coppers and that is the whole of what you pay.** He is honest and knows it: **a lie only works while it is untidy, and the moment somebody writes your lie down properly it stops working on anybody who can read and starts working on everybody else, which is worse.** His argument, in full: **a man who buys a question knows he is buying a question and keeps it; a man who buys a complete fare takes it home and tells his household the thing is settled, and a household that has been told a thing is settled stops asking, and a household that stops asking is the only reason anybody ever gets anything done in this basin.** The finding, arrived at by a signal clerk at a counter, **and it is the one specific thing he is wrong about: his fare is complete because the tenth hand's finding of the twelfth is that the passengers pay nothing and the standing eighth line says the only carriage is a person carried as a person, and he printed it correctly and honestly out of the public book - and the price of that ticket is not a toll, it is a place losing the memory of its own departures, and no column in this district can print it.** Stenn's figure: **two hundred and six places at four coppers in about forty minutes, eight hundred and twenty-four coppers, against a whole year's line budget of sixty, the figure as it stood on the twenty-sixth at nine plus six a quarter**, and a column of his own headed *what the counterfeit got there first with*. **And the district's position, entered: we can prove he is wrong in one sentence and cannot prove it in a book, and that is the worst possible place for the district to be.** A woman of sixty with a stick asks Rutt for the whole of the price and Rutt says she cannot read an absence, and the woman says she will wait, and Rutt enters that she is not angry and has walked nine miles to be told no by somebody paid eleven coppers a quarter to say yes. **On the twenty-seventh the market spends its six coppers before the light goes and finds out what a sheet costs: nine hundred and six coppers for a run of five hundred, quoted the fourteenth, payable before the run, four for stock and two for setting, and the gap of eight hundred and ninety-four carried on a trade for the second time by the chandlers, whose stall name is at the bottom in type the size of the date.** The first thing this market has printed since the flood year goes up on eleven boards with *this is a date a person could be wrong about* at the bottom. Halloway prints a second sheet at eleven, four hours before the market's run comes off the press, and the printer's boy gives the finding: **he has been asked for four hundred of his and none of ours, and both of those facts are about me and neither of them is about the walk.**

## Chapter 0065 - A Mark May Not Carry a Job
The twenty-eighth, opened on a boy of seventeen and a fair copy of **the index of marks** on a kitchen table - the only piece of administration anybody in this district has ever asked for - **and the finding is that the district's own rules have never once been tested against the district, and they are tested against it here, and a boy nobody pays finds the whole of it in a minute and a half because somebody finally asked him.** **The fifth rule, a mark may not carry a job, blocks two things.** One: the boundary, because the section post is a mark and holding a line is a job. Two: **the fourth person's own clause**, because it says whoever holds the thing that day has to stop and wait, **and that half is the lever, and a mark may not carry an object either, and nobody had asked her to carry it.** The resolution is the narrowest instrument this district has built in six weeks and the tenth hand says so at the time: **a job is something somebody gives you; you can be given a job and you can refuse one; and a person who says no to a train has not been given the train - so the fifth rule stops a mark from being made into an officer and does not stop a person from saying no, and the clause was always performable.** And: **the boundary is not solved, and he says so, and it is a job, and he has been walking the line for a week trying to find a way round that is not a mark.** Four people go up the bank with two questions and no forms. **Sef Anyard says yes in four seconds** - she has held the only pair for eleven years and nobody has ever asked whether she would hold a second thing, **and the answer has been yes the whole time and nobody knew because nobody ever asked** - and makes them ask what the job is before whether she wants it, and answers: **the line is not a fence and does not stop anything; it is the first honest limit anybody has drawn on that line; and I will use it about nine times a year with something to point at.** **Then the finding nobody had thought of: a line at the end of the siding takes the water with it, because the only way up that bank is through the end of the siding and the pail is behind the pail, and four men a week have filled there for nine years and the whole price of it is thirty-six coppers and the column is the only water for two miles - so the line is put sixty yards further west, past the column, where the ground stops being good ground.** The woman of fifty says yes in nine seconds and **names the cost before she says the yes: that column is metred to a road house and a man comes up twice a week and has never asked her a question about anything except the water, and if her name goes in a book then in four years there will be a man up here who has read it, and inside a year he will be asking about the column - and she has decided to be in a book, because she has been fifty for two months and has never once been findable on purpose, and would rather be findable and have it done to her than not findable and have it done to her anyway.** **The woman of forty-five is asked last and says no, and her reason is the sentence the volume is built out of: a name with nothing behind it is a name a man can use, it is the cheapest thing she owns, and it is not a handle for you to find her with, it is a door for her to send him to - and a name with a boy of nine behind it is a door that works.** She will say the word on the ballast and she will not hold the line, and those are two different things and it took her three hours and a half. **Oswin Tace, forty-one, a lamp-man who has never counted anything but lamps and has asked twice that it be said there as well as here, takes the key:** he wants the case open at the hours he is up, and **if nobody comes he will write down that nobody came, with the date on it, and he will not say the case was open or that anybody was careless**, and his reason is that **his mother was on that stair when a bridge went and about nine people stood on a ramp for a night because nobody came, and it is not a lamp.** The term is **four days from the twenty-eighth, expiring at midnight on the first of the new month**, and the board entered that it had not noticed that the thirtieth is the day after four people walk five miles of railway and the day a mechanic gives up a lease.

## Chapter 0066 - One Hour and Eleven Minutes
Sef Anyard, the ninth place, the twenty-ninth, morning to dusk, and it opens on a woman hearing four people coming and having eleven minutes, which she spends the way she has spent every morning for eleven years. **Selik Marne is at the fourth place at ten to seven with forty people behind him and does not stop the walk**, and **uses his own rule on a railway for the first time** - *the second rail on a straight is four feet off a man and on a curve it is a foot and a half and the ballast is up to his chest, and you have gone round the whole difficulty by walking on the outside of everything, and that is the correct answer* - and then says the other half: **four people on a line with no keeper at two of the twelve places and no lamp lit at either, and if something happens this afternoon then nobody with a whistle is within three miles and nobody with a book is within nine, and he is not going to be the man who stops four people walking, because stopping people walking is a Warden's order and this is a public lot.** Tarin Kest says the thank you, and it is the first one anybody has heard him say to Selik about anything. The walk is four and a half hours on an ordinary morning and took six and a half on the twenty-ninth, of which an hour and eleven minutes was standing still in a drain. **At ten to eleven the fourth person stops the walk, and the clause turns out to have a hole in it: there is no train and no lever and no thing held, and the clause is about a train, and she has found a hole in her own instrument in about four minutes and has used it, and it has worked because a train cannot be on that line at all.** They stand in four inches of drain water for **one hour and eleven minutes** - **the first price this district has ever put on a refusal**, in a column headed **what the thing goes on to next**, which is Bett Ruhl's name for it and is now the name of a column in the public lot book - and the entry says the figures are **the cost of the room and not of the thing**, and Bett Ruhl says she does not know how to write the other half and would like somebody to do it for her. The other half, at half past four: **then it should not have a train on it either, and it has had nine vehicles on it since the eleventh of last month.** The man of seventy says *you have come about the water* and is told there is water in it. **The twelfth stall is measured and it is a floor: eleven inches out of level at the left-hand end of the bench, a crack in the lime putty about four feet long, a folded blanket under the low leg since the winter before the water came** - a straightedge off the mantel shelf and a carpenter's level, about twenty minutes, and the mark is not scraped, **and it is the only stall in this district ever measured in feet and inches and it has been under a bench leg in a hut nine miles from the nearest public book for eight years.** Sef says the reason she was afraid for eight years: **a stall she puts in her own book is a stall she has to be asked about, and there was not one person in nine miles who could have asked her about it and not made her into something.** **She is asked the custody question from the sheet, on the twenty-ninth, in front of about twenty people, and says yes**, and attaches not a condition but a request: **when the reader comes to read, she wants to be asked whether she wishes to speak before he starts, not after** - and says he asked her that at ten to five on the eleventh, two hours and a half after he sat at her table, **and it is the only sentence anybody in this district has said to her in eleven years that was about her, and she has not told anybody she noticed it, and the yard is hearing it now.** The fourth person says at ten past three that the word has worked for the first time in her life and that what happened was four men standing still for an hour and a quarter, and that none of them is to write down that she was the fourth person.

## Chapter 0067 - Two Names, and a Line on the Ground
The twenty-ninth, ten past four to half past six, and **the chapter is a mechanic watching two people who do not own his ground mark a line on it.** **Tarin Kest refuses to mark the line, and says why in forty seconds, and it is not a confession: because he signed the lease, and the line would make the ground have a leaseholder and a boundary and a man who marks it, and all three would be him, and the man who marks it is the man who signed the paper that put the thing past it there - and you are all going to say that is fine because he has never done it, and I have never done it because I have not had the nerve, and that is not a reason.** Then the four attempts, in order, and all four fail. **A mark**, refused by the fifth rule, and **not argued with, because the person who may argue a rule is the tenth hand and he has argued one rule in his life and it was Selik Marne's.** **The board**, which cannot name a person and is nine miles away and whose five powers are all about a thing in its own building. **The leaseholder**, refused in front of witnesses. **A road house**, refused in advance on the ninth. And the fourth attempt, which is smaller than all of them: **two named people, a stated term, and a published entry** - the term **from the twenty-ninth of this month to the twenty-ninth of the next month, renewable only by asking both of them again, in a room, with the asking and the hour and the answer entered, whatever the answer is** - the reason for two and not one, and **the reason the published entry is not a courtesy: a private arrangement is the thing this district spent five weeks learning about and it does not survive a stranger.** The woman of fifty dictates her own half: **a name in a book is a door, and a door is for sending a man away, and I want it in that shape and not in any other shape, and if anybody writes my name on a line at a place they have to write what I am, and I am the water** - and: **nobody stops coming up that bank; a haulier comes up on the Tuesday; and if he is on the line when the word is said he waits on the bank until it is over, and I want it entered that a man waited on a bank once and not that we kept him off the road.** **Bev Tollard says the sentence that costs them the most: in a year there will be a book in that yard that says two people held a boundary at Sarrow Water, and a stranger will read it and think this place is a station, and it is not, and I want it in the book that the two people are not a station and did not become one, and that a line on the ground is not a platform, and that nothing that happened today made this place a place a train is meant to stop at, and if anybody reads it in four years and thinks otherwise that is on the sheet and not on us.** The word is said once at four o'clock in nine words and it is not a form and not a signature and nobody holds it. Tarin chalks the line, and **it cannot be seen from the west end of the siding at all, and the tenth hand enters that as the standing failure of the instrument, and the fixing of it is one bushel of white paint and about a hundred and forty yards of it.** The board publishes it as the **twelfth part**, with the things it is not, **and the finding printed inside it is that it is not the fifth thing, and the board has power to publish a result and no power to publish a charge on a place, and both of those are entered as the same gap and it is the gap that matters.** Then Selik says that is not enough for the eleventh time that week, **and Tarin Kest asks him for the thing nobody has asked him for in nine years**, and he gives three faults: **the line is invisible from the end it is for and will be killed by a man who walks up that bank in the dark and not by a train, and a fence on a shoulder above a river is a fence somebody will be paid for and the payment will be the end of it; and it is held by two people's goodwill, and in about a month one of the two will be tired of it, and the line does not know it has stopped working because a line is not a person and there is nobody standing there to find out** - and then the choice, **a line two women hold, or an office eight men sit in that nobody can end, which lost by seven to three** - and the tenth hand enters that the only reason he said any of it is that somebody asked him, **and that in nine years of the district's public life nobody has.**

## Chapter 0068 - Held by Nobody
Nessa Pell, the thirtieth. **The counts came east on a two-inch by three sheet and went into a sleeve, and the finding is that the sheet said the counts were to be taken going and coming and did not say of what, and nobody asked, and the two are different numbers and neither is struck.** The tenth hand counted stalls: **ten going, ten coming in the morning, and eleven coming** - because the twelfth is **about ninety yards of embankment between the section post and the ninth where the cess has gone soft and the ballast has come up through it in three places**, invisible from the six-foot at ten to eleven with the light on the river and visible at ten past four off the shoulder with the light behind him and the water gone out of the hollow. **And the reason it was never in the line book is that the line book is a book of signals, and a stall with a signal in it gets entered and a stall with no signal in it does not, because there has been nobody standing at one since the fourth year - so the count has been wrong for eight years and it has been wrong in one direction only, downwards, and the answer is that somebody walks every yard of it and not the fourth place to the ninth.** Stenn's count: six marks going, seven coming, and the seventh is the section post with a number in it that is the same number the line book has. **Rutt reads it all out, and a woman of sixty with a stick asks what is going to happen at the west end, and is read the twelfth part, and the answer is that the entry says what two people are doing and not what is not going to happen - and a woman who cannot read a sentence has just located the hole in this district's constitution more accurately than anybody in a yard has in six weeks.** **The key: nine people say no in nine minutes for ordinary printed reasons, and the tenth is Adrian Vale, who says yes before the asking is finished, and is made to say the difference out loud** - on the twenty-sixth of February he had put one thousand five hundred and thirty-six coppers of his own inventory into a steward's hands, **and if he had taken it that afternoon then he had taken it back, and there is no version of taking it back that is not that** - and he asks in advance for one term only, **because the standing objection to everything he has done in six weeks is one man's limit checked by one man, and the only answer anybody has found to it is a notice nailed up in eleven places**, and says plainly that he is not a Binder and cannot close anything and cannot post a security and that anyone who came for a road has come to the wrong yard. **Orren Pike: nobody thanked him and nobody is going to and he would not want it to.** **Fen Ambrose: the fifth rule stopped a thing, it was written for that, it worked, it cost the district a boundary for about a day and a half, and there are five things in the public record that are all wrong and none of them may be taken down and she wrote two of the five.** Then the lease, surrendered in about eleven minutes, **headed surrender of a lease and not assignment and not transfer, because you cannot assign a lease to a village and a village cannot hold paper, and if you assign it to the ward market the market is a road house with a different name - so it is assigned to nobody and goes on the rate sheet as HELD BY NOBODY, the only line on that sheet that is not about money, and the same line as the first one, and the sheet has two now.** Three figures given unasked: **eleven vehicles a week on that siding, four spaces in the Alder Reach yards, seven a week with nowhere to stand, four hundred and ten coppers of salvage worth that in Alder Reach and nothing at the ninth place, and the difference is the Road House and I have been living in the difference for nineteen years**; and the third is not a figure: **before a wall went and not after, and not after a train was late.** **He is not let off and does not ask to be let off. The woman of fifty asks him what the water cost - not the money - and he fails, and the failure is in the book: nobody cost it, and he drew it off twice a week for nine years through men he paid and never once asked her a question about anything except the water, and he was the person who owned the meter and not the person who asked what the meter was for.** And her answer: **then pay for it, not the water - I know what water costs, I have had it eleven years for nothing and have never minded, and I am saying it now so that there is one thing about this morning that is not a mess; what I have got is eleven years of filling two pails a day off a thing a man in Alder Reach decided I should have, and I was never asked, and I would have said yes, and you have never once been in a room with me.** Nessa asks whether she may write it down, and says a boy of fifteen handed her a sheet with four questions on it a fortnight ago and she refused to put a question in the book, **and it is the only piece of paper on her desk that anybody in this city is waiting for.** The name of the payer is not in the entry and is not going to be.

## Chapter 0069 - Three Reasons, and the Word Close
The thirtieth and the first of April, opened on a sheet squared with a thumb. **The idea came off the walk: four bearers, one lot, the use split among four places, so that no single place holds the whole of it and when the origin is taken it is taken four ways and a village is not alone with it** - the correct response to everything Adrian admitted in a yard of five hundred on the eighteenth, written in his own book in his own hand, **the best thing he has written since the four lines at a kitchen table.** **The draft is in the book in full and cannot be closed by anybody who exists, and the board publishes it on the second because a document nobody can close should not be in a drawer.** **The one System panel in the batch** is the object asked about a split: a use may be split among bearers; **a split is drafted by a reader and closed by a binder**; a Stage 1 reader may draft and may not close, transfer, renew, or post security; **standing is given to a bearer and not to a slot, and a pair is a place, an approach and the person able to hold them, and a slot is not a pair**; a board cannot name a person and a board asked by a crowd is not a board; **and none of this is an answer, it is the shape of the thing you are trying to do.** Then **the three reasons in his own words, in the order they hurt.** One: **I may draft a split and I may not close one, and there is no version of me that closes this.** Two: **I cannot post a security, and a four-bearer split with no security is four people on a hook with nothing behind them** - and the Chapter 19 undertaking is named for what it is, a real obligation nobody else can inspect - **and I have been here seven weeks telling this district that a thing you did not consent to is a breach, and it turns out that is a rule about roads I built and not a law, and this draft is a road I am building and I am doing it again, and this time it is worse because this time there are four people in it.** Three: **a transferable slot is not a pair** - the fourth entry is a slot because he could name three people and did not ask the three, and **standing is per bearer, so the standing of this split is the standing of three men and the approach at the fourth place is covered by nobody, and a slot nobody stands in is not a bearer at all, it is a name I have not got.** **Rutt reads the fourth entry aloud and it is the only line in a public document in this city that takes a second and a half to say**, and her finding is that **a mark, a slot and a holding entry are three words for a person who is not there, and the job of all three is so that a person does not have to be a person to be counted**; and **Orren Pike names the three signal hands nobody has ever asked and says he would want to be asked again in a year.** Then the **fourth reason off the floor from Tarin Kest in forty seconds: the sixth place is a drain, and the eighth place is a curve with a shoulder four feet wide and a nine-foot drop, and a man who has to hold a pair at the eighth place has to stand somewhere and there is nowhere at the eighth place to stand - so it is a paper with two holes in it and one vacancy, and the holes are not holes you made, they are because a railway is a drain and a line is an order and not a length and every one of the twelve places was put where somebody wanted to put it and not where a man could stand.** **Mara Quill asks him to close it, in the ordinary way, with the book open, knowing the answer, because you have spent six weeks telling this district that a refusal nobody asks for is not a refusal. No - in about a second and a half, the shortest refusal in the volume** - and he does not say it long to her because she has known since the twelfth and because he squared a sheet with a thumb and read it out in daylight and neither thing was necessary and he did them because somebody would be disappointed in about an hour; she publishes the unclosable draft and says it is the best thing in this district's public record in a month. **Tarin asks again, on the first, in the Road House yard, in front of nine of his own men, and gives a reason, and the second no is four times as long**: the three reasons will not be said a second time in a yard with his men in it, because **a reason that is in your ear and mine is not a reason, it is a thing somebody has learned**; and the word is close, **because a draft is a man holding a door open and asking a room to fill it, and closing it is putting his name on it, and his name on it is standing, and standing is what a man gives when he is replaceable, and he is not replaceable yet and Selik Marne is right about that in about a year and not in a yard** - and Tarin says he will have to live in his foreman knowing the omission is deliberate. Closes in a kitchen under the Ward Nine tram line at a quarter to seven, **where a slate passed between them and there is no account of what was on it in any file in this district, and neither of them said one word about anything that happened in the yard.**

## Chapter 0070 - Fourteen Coppers, and a Sheet
Ivo Stenn, the second to the fifth of April, and it turns on a conversation and a vote rather than on a record for its first half. **He opens by refusing to draw a new sheet: I have never once put a figure on a wall that I did not intend to defend line by line, and I am not going to start on a day when I would have to defend a figure I did not write; the paper is the paper of the twenty-first of the month before last in five columns at fifty coppers over four thousand and one households, the fifth column says nine, and I am not proposing anything. Ask somebody else.** The haulier takes it up: **the nine coppers buys a lamp, and a lamp is for a man who is going somewhere, and I have got nine vehicles on that siding and a stock in a field and a line on the ground and two women holding it and a mechanic who gave up a lease, and a lamp is not a line; I move that the nine stands, that a lamp and a bushel of chalk is a service, that the service is finished, and that the line is not a service and this market can find out by paying for it.** The chandler moves twenty-two and says the reason is not generosity: **this market has funded a lamp on a line for three weeks and has not paid for one single one of the four things that happened on that line.** **Seven to three, one abstaining; the three who voted for the office on the twenty-fifth all voted for the line and nobody asked them why.** **The abstaining trade is the same trade that abstained on the thirteenth and gives the same reason, and the reason is in the minute for the second time: we are not a trade for the people the road is for, and the fact that the reason has not changed is the most useful thing anybody has said in this room in a fortnight.** **The rate on the line is fourteen coppers and nobody proposed fourteen**, and Stenn's entry is the finding of the day: **I proposed nine, in a column, in writing, on the twenty-first of the month before last, and it stands as nine in the paper on the wall beside me, and a rate that comes back by itself is a rate that was never my decision, and a man who spends six years saying no and then signs a figure he did not put up for is a man who has learned something, and I am not sure it is the right something, and my hand is on the minute and that is the whole of what I have.** The four items costed: a lamp, a bushel of chalk, fourteen pints of white paint, oil, and a sheet - **and a sheet of five hundred is nine hundred coppers, which is two hundred and twenty-five a quarter spread over a year, and the sheet is the part of the fourteen that does not fit in it, and the market knows it.** A woman of sixty with a stick, in off a held door, not a trade and not on a roll: **you are not voting for a lamp, you have not been voting for a lamp since the thirteenth, you are voting for a railway, and the reason is a date - a market which cannot say yes to a road can say yes to a day, and I do not think any of you worked out what you were doing.** And the **first timetable in the district's history**: a day against every train, and **the toll column is the widest column on the page and is the only column this market has ever printed that is mostly empty, and what is in it is PASSENGERS PAY NOTHING; THE CHARGE FALLS ON THE ORIGIN, WHICH IS A PLACE; THIS MARKET CANNOT PRINT THE AMOUNT OF THE ORIGIN BECAUSE NOBODY IN THIS DISTRICT CAN STATE IT, AND THE COLUMN IS PRINTED ANYWAY, AT FULL WIDTH, IN THE MIDDLE OF THE SHEET, BECAUSE A COLUMN THAT IS PRINTED NARROW LOOKS LIKE A COLUMN THAT HAS BEEN KEPT QUIET** - and at the bottom, in the same type, **the train at the west end has stood fifty-one days and cannot be moved east, and a timetable is not a promise and this one knows it.** **Halloway is beaten by a column and says so, and has one more: your timetable has days on it and I did not think of that, because a day is a promise and I have nothing to promise with** - and he prints his own sheet on Monday with the market's empty column set beside his figure at full width, so a stranger can take both cold and work out which of the two of them was afraid of the size of the hole. **Orren Pike is paid four coppers a quarter and it is a standing item and not a service**, on the haulier's argument: **if the index had been bought it would have had to have been bought in the ninth year, and it was not, and the ninth year is the year a mechanic signed a lease in about eleven minutes and nobody in this market had heard of the place, and there was no sheet for it because nobody had walked onto it, and nobody walked onto it because there was nothing to price - so it is the first item this market has ever bought for the ninth reason, which is that somebody might want to know something.** He is still not Adrian's apprentice and his mother's name is still not in the book. **Bett Ruhl's fifth line is written in a hand big enough to be read from a doorway and is the longest thing on that wall: a refusal is not free, it is a day, and somebody pays the day; the first time this district priced one it was one hour and eleven minutes of daylight and four people standing in a drain, and that is not the cost; the figures are the cost of the room; the cost of the thing is what the room was doing before the word, and nobody has costed that yet.** She was read the whole of it twice, has never read it herself, and will not be told what it says because she would want to correct it. **Selik at the rail on the fifth reads the timetable in full and will not call either sheet wrong: the two timetables are not safety instruments and he is the only man in this basin whose entire profession is safety instruments, and he will not say what is instead, because what it is instead is the office, and he will not vary that in the ninth year of it.** The key turns on the second, Adrian's one term is honoured exactly, and **a man of about fifty-six off the lower terrace who has kept a chandler's second counter for thirty-one years takes it, and asks in writing that he does not read figures and that a reader at a counter is the only person who may read anything to him, and asks for four days, and gets all three** - and Bett Ruhl did not come and the reason she gave is in the book in a hand that is not her writing.

## Chapter 0071 - Nine Minutes
The sixth of April, opened on a key put on a crate at a quarter to four. **A man of about fifty-six off the lower terrace has kept a chandler's second counter for thirty-one years and asked the question nobody had thought to ask: HOW DO YOU ASK A PERSON WHETHER THEY WILL DO A JOB.** He came a day early on purpose. **The yard works out the method in nine minutes and the method is the district's, not Adrian's and not the tenth hand's and not the board's**: a job is asked in five things - what it is in sentences a person who has never done it can understand, how long with an hour on it, how you say no and that no reason is owed and nobody will ask afterwards, who answers for you while you are saying it, and where you stand when you answer. **Five people say no inside four minutes and a sixth says yes**, and the reasons are in the book in their own words and none is argued with. **Twenty-two names on the sheet, nineteen noes, three people who said they would ask somebody at home, and asking somebody is not a no.** The new term begins at midnight the same night and runs four days, and **for the first time in six weeks there is no hour in which nobody holds the key and no hour in which two people hold it.** A reader reads the nineteen refusals out at the ward market counter the next morning in the ordinary voice, and the finding is that a refusal made in a crowd is not heard by a crowd. **Nessa Pell supplies the sixth thing, which has no name and is not a power: a person in the room who is not the one being asked and is not the one asking, whose job is to say what the question was**, and she has done it for nineteen years and has never been thanked and does not want to be. Adrian says the method is not his and would not have found it in nine weeks. At ten past six that evening **a boy of fifteen walks nine miles with a drawing of a signal frame, because the keeper at the ninth place has found that the Harrow Tongue is not in it**, and she last had it in her hand on the eighteenth of last month, when she took it out because the wedge end was catching, said *that'll do till the end of the month* and put it back, and it is in the book as her own account of her own hand and not as a fact about a hut; she looked for eleven hours and told nobody, and she is not sorry about the eleven and is sorry about the eighteenth, and Teo brings the drawing and says so in that order. The thirteenth part of the results is nine words: **the lot is not in the custody, the custody had no case, no counter and no key, and nobody in this district has the power to move a lot or the power to find one.** The last line of the entry is that somebody has to walk to the fourth place, four hours on foot, nine hours there and back in that weather.

## Chapter 0072 - A Hut Four
The seventh, opened on a boy of nineteen woken on a bench with four pounds of cast iron beside his head. **The Harrow Tongue went eleven yards from the ballast at the ninth place to a hut at the fourth place, in the rain, on the evening of the sixth, carried by a boy called Nevin Roke who thought it was a bar, is nineteen, is not on a roll in any book in Alder Reach, and has slept in that hut since the flood year, and nobody in this district knew that until the eighth of this month.** Three people walk up at half past four in the morning and take five and a quarter hours with all four changeovers, and they stay two nights. **Rutt's rule goes into the entry: found on the seventh, taken on the sixth by a person who is not on a roll, and the person is not struck and is not asked to be a bearer and is not a witness and is not entered as anything.** Petrus Nye, a bridge clerk of thirty-four, writes the finding in the water office's own book and adds that the office has no use for it and he is writing it down because the book is copied twice a quarter and goes where the boy is not, and adds a fourth thing: **there is an empty room over the counting office on the second floor with a bed in it and nobody in it for two years, and he has the keys.** The line's loss is then worked out on the six-foot in about four minutes: **the ninth place is the only frame on the line with a bracket the iron fits, and the bracket is empty, and therefore the only place on the line that can turn a train is a place that does not have the thing in it, and the train cannot go east and cannot go west and has stood fifty-five days.** The boundary's word is a word about a train and there is no train, and Rutt's entry is that it is stopping people and that is what it does and not what it was for. **The woman at the column tells them what the word has actually been doing for ten days: said five times, stopped the haulier twice for about a minute and a half, stopped a man with a dog, and stopped a boy of eleven who cried.** The published entry is in her words: **a word written about a train is now a word about people, and a word does not need its authors to agree with it.** The fourteenth part of the results is published on the eleventh and says the west end is not a place, it is an order, and a district that can only count things is going to find that out in about a week, and the finding will not be a figure. Nye reads it out at the rail at the fourth place at half past four and asks to go back.

## Chapter 0073 - Clean
The fourteenth, opened on a guild inspector's case on a crate at ten past two, and it is the first guild inspector in Volume 02. **Garnet Sill, forty-one, twelve years licensed, about two hundred and forty lots certified and nine of them wrong, and eight of the nine were something somebody had added.** He came twelve miles on his own fee, off his own book, on two days' notice, and nobody asked him, and he reads out the third column of the fee schedule, which prices a lot in a custody with no case, no counter or no key at **eleven coppers, examined in the person of the keeper**, and announces that he is the most expensive thing in that yard this month and earned it. **The certificate says CLEAN** and it is clean, and he says so first and at length, in the guild sense, exactly. **And then the second sentence: it cannot be sold to a village** - because a sale is to a person and a village is not a person, and because nineteen takings of one object have charged the origin, which is a place, and no instrument in this basin can name what is being sold. **Sef Anyard is asked whether she would know if the lot were gone and whether anybody would, and answers no twice, and asks him to write the hole in the custody on the back, and gives the reason she never wrote it down: there was not one person in nine miles who could have asked her about it and not made her into something.** The woman at the column asks whether he is going to write down whether her sister is in there, and he says no, and gives the reason, and she calls it the first time a man has told her no in nine years and given her the reason he could not. He explains himself at the counter on the seventeenth: **a man from the Guild goes into a village to look at a thing, and the place asks him a question, and the place is the only party in the room with nothing to sell, and in every one of two hundred and forty lots the thing that was wrong was in a column, and not one column in this basin is a place.** A ward market secretary finds the price difference on the wrong fee schedule and refunds four coppers and cannot give back the nine, and the minute enters the first figure anybody in Alder Reach has ever entered against a Guild. Selik says **that is not enough** from the rail and then says the certificate is the only good news anybody has had about that railway in four weeks, and that the man is the first in nine years to put a document on a wall that says where the toll lands, and he did it by accident.

## Chapter 0074 - Two Sheets, Upside Down
The fifteenth, opened on a drover of sixty-one holding two printed sheets upside down for about four minutes. **Halloway Reed's Monday sheet comes off the press at about eleven on the fifteenth with four coppers in the market's empty column and the finding printed at the foot that he cannot print what a place costs and has put four coppers in anyway, and a column with four coppers in it is a different thing from a column with nothing in it and a stranger can tell the difference, and that is the only reason he printed it.** **The district cannot tell what day the fifteenth is.** A sexton with a calendar has got it right for six weeks; a chandler makes it Monday; a haulier says it is the day before the day the haulier comes up the bank and takes it back. **Abner Skell's question is the finding: which of these two is telling the truth, and the answer is that both men have said the same thing in different voices and the only difference is that one of them has decided what to charge you for not knowing.** A woman of sixty with a stick asks what happens to a person who reads it, and points out that the only sentence either man printed that is about a person is the market's line at the foot saying the train has stood fifty-one days and the west end is held by two named people until the twenty-ninth of this month. **Four strangers come to the ward market counter in one afternoon and ask one question each, and Rutt says no four times, and the entry says three of the four were disappointed and the one who was not is the one she would have given her own name to.** Skell goes and looks at the west end on the sixteenth on his own, and cannot tell the first, second, third and fourth places apart, and is told the fourth by a boy asleep in it, and four of the nine lines he gives in the yard are about lamps. **Halloway comes into the yard on the seventeenth and sells nothing, and says he has been beaten by a column, that the empty column was worth more than his full one until about the fourth day and that after the fourth day his is worth more, and that he has both columns on one sheet and has not worked out which of them is right and has had nothing but fourteen days.** The finding printed on the seventeenth: **two sheets, both true on their faces, add up to a price for a thing that has not been decided, and a stranger cannot tell a hole from a small price, and the answer is not a better sheet.** The answer is two lines of type under a column that is still empty, with a date and an hour on them, printed by the market and reprinted unpaid by the arch.

## Chapter 0075 - Nine Feet and Not Fourteen
The eighteenth, opened on a man of sixty-eight coming up out of a wet chamber with water to his chest and a bucket. **There is a pumping engine at the Harrow end, on a brick plinth in a brick house eleven feet above the flood, and it has drawn the Harrow interchange down every day since the flood year, and the man who keeps it is called Gilman Trew, is sixty-eight, is not paid, and is given what is left in a tin.** Four things have gone wrong there in one week and **not one of them was reported by anything, because there is no longer anything at the Harrow end of this railway that reports** - the ground signal went out of the same frame in the fourth year when the Tongue was salvaged and has never been put back. **Ivor Kell, a foreman of twenty-two years, shores a washout in a foot of water for two hours and then says the sentence that has been sitting in him since the ninth of April: the vehicle overflow has been standing in the rain against a wall for nineteen days and he has priced it in two figures and one of them is four hundred and ten coppers and the other one is nothing.** His figure is in the public book with his name on it, entered at his own request, because **a method that is not written down is a man being angry and a method that is written down is a rate that has not been argued yet.** Oswin Tace, the lamp-man, asks Gilman Trew what his job is, having said first that he is a lamp-man and not a signal hand, and the answer is six in the morning and six at night and a chamber that silts and a bank that has gone once already. **Adrian Vale stands in that house for two hours with a pencil and takes no figure at all, and says so.** He refuses to read the engine, and the refusal is entered with the hour: **he is in the wrong room, and a man who has kept an hour for nineteen days will spend it here on a machine and come home able to say he read a pump.** Then the arithmetic, from nine years of Road House oil receipts found on a shelf behind the tools: **nine cans in a fortnight at seven coppers is sixty-three a fortnight, one thousand six hundred and thirty-eight a year, fourteen thousand seven hundred and forty-two coppers in nine years, in a tradesman's hand on the back of a bill, on a shelf in a brick box.** He writes it out and says it is not a price, and the reason is the same reason as Bett Ruhl's wall. At the top of the bank he reads eleven lines with no number in them to Tarin Kest, and Tarin says one sentence: somebody has to go and look in the room. The last thing in the chapter is the lamp-man's pocket book, which is the first piece of paper in nine years that came out of a job and not out of a building, and whose last line is about an empty room over a counting office with a bed in it.

## Chapter 0076 - The Name on the Lease
The twentieth and the twenty-second, opened on a lease clerk turning a book round on a table and taking her hand off it. **Junia Crale has been in that room twenty-two years and has read out four thousand and something leases, and nobody has ever stood in it and asked her who holds the paper.** The hook has eleven leases on it: **nine name a firm, two name people, and both of the two that name people have been renewed.** The lease from the ninth year names the siding, the water column, **a shed sixteen feet by thirty on the north side**, and the ground between, and the lessee is **THE NEEDLE ROAD HOUSE, A FIRM**, and the signatory is Tarin Kest. **THE PERSON WHO HELD THE PAPER IS NOT THE PERSON THE PAPER NAMES. THE PAPER NAMES A ROAD HOUSE. THE ROAD HOUSE IS NOT THERE ANY MORE** - and the answer to who pays for the engine is nobody, and the answer to who holds the toll is nobody, and both are correct. The shed was taken down in the flood year and is on the ninth-year survey in ink with a water butt added in a different ink, and four feet off its east wall there is a second mark on the survey: **a line of stones.** Tarin lays it out himself. His father put them down on the morning of the day he signed, across the overflow, nine to a foot, and that is the whole of the work. **The woman at the column has drawn two pails off that overflow for eleven years and the water arrives where the stones send it, and she is not going to be told that by anybody in that room.** Orren Pike, unasked, stops Tarin from going up the bank to tell her: **because if you ask her, the true thing will come back to the wrong man and in four years the story will be about you finding out.** Put it on a sheet, ask it in a room, ask her twice, enter both. On the twenty-second a quarterly return from the water office comes up a bank in a satchel: **the Harrow works maintenance has been one hundred and eighty coppers a quarter for nine years, paid, not arrears, not deferred, and the market has read that return every quarter for nine years, and the oil was never in it because the office did not know the engine was running.** Ivo Stenn does the arithmetic nobody asked for: **six thousand four hundred and eighty coppers in nine years, paid, against fourteen thousand seven hundred and forty-two in oil; twenty-one thousand two hundred and twenty-two in total, in two books in two rooms in two hands, and it is the first time the two have been in the same place** - and under it nine words: **the market refused eighty-eight coppers a quarter for eight posts. A river office paid six thousand four hundred and eighty. Neither asked the other if the thing existed.** The last thing on the twenty-second is a printed half-sheet from the bridge administration: **a signalman will be in the hut at the ninth place from the twenty-fifth, for one month, renewed by the office, nobody asked, and the administration considers that place unsafe.**

## Chapter 0077 - The Safest and the Most Hated
The twenty-fifth and the twenty-sixth, opened on a signalman of thirty-nine putting a folding rule under the left-hand end of a bench and saying the floor is eleven inches out of level and that he is going to sleep on it. **Corvin Sallow is a signalman of the bridge administration and he is the man in that hut because of a four-link chain nobody designed**: Petrus Nye's book on the seventh, the office's second quarterly copy on the nineteenth, an empty room over a counting office with a bed in it and nobody in it for two years, and a lamp-man's pocket book in a sleeve in a yard - **and he asked to be sent, and nobody sent him, and the piece of paper with his name on it says so.** About ninety people go up the bank to be angry and stay to look and go back still angry, and all three are in the book. **A woman of forty-nine who voted for the office in March says the whole of it at a doorway: the most hated office in this district has put a man in the one hut on this line where the ground stops being good ground, and nobody in this district would have, and the district spent a month refusing to pay for exactly him.** Sallow says four things, including that he will find out what the boundary is **when somebody asks him and not before**, and that he is entered as not asked. Then Orren Pike sees in four minutes that a signalman with a place, an approach and a stated week is three of the four things the eighth place's slot needs, **and Sallow refuses before the sentence is finished and gives the reason: if the standing of that lot is the standing of an office with a room and a bed in it, then there is nobody standing there at all, there is an office, and he has never known a public thing that survived an office finding out about it.** The fourth shape on the tenth hand's page: **a bearer is a person. An office is not a person. A slot is not a person. A line of stones is not a person.** In the last hour of light two women teach the newcomer the two things he has not got, and both attach something that is not a condition and has no end on it: **Sef Anyard will not hold a second word alone, and the woman of the column will not have an officer say her word, and he must say it in front of her once and she will decide whether he said it like a person or like an office, and there is no third time.** On the twenty-sixth the district finds out, in a count read out at a counter by a reader who does not read figures, **that the safest office in this basin is the one nobody would vote for, that it has been paying for a pump on a dead line since the flood year out of a river, and that it has sent a better railwayman than the district has to a place nobody would name** - and the only reason the district knows any of it is a seat on a satchel in another office's book. **Selik says that is not enough twice, and then says the rest of it: a man he did not choose out of a room with a door on it asked to be sent, and the same sentence with one word changed is why his man cannot be a bearer, and he noticed the resemblance on a step on his way home.** On the second morning Sallow walks the section because he was told to, and measures the twelfth stall at **ninety-four yards**, which is neither his keeper's figure nor the tenth hand's, and neither number is struck.

## Chapter 0078 - The Same Word
The twenty-seventh and the twenty-eighth, opened on a reader reading eleven lines at the rail out of the page a man wrote about himself. **The asking was done in the yard in nine minutes on the twenty-sixth by the tenth hand, using the five things on the wall, on the reader himself for the first time in seven weeks**, and the sixth thing was done by Rutt, who stated the question so that the entry in a fortnight would be the question and not a memory of one. **Adrian Vale names the person who answers for him before anybody gets to the fourth thing: Bett Ruhl, who cannot read, and the finding is that the district has spent nine weeks treating literacy as a condition of being asked and the reader has appointed somebody who is not eligible.** He asks Sef Anyard on the shoulder whether she wishes to speak before he starts, as she asked for on the eleventh of March, and she has one thing ready: **the iron is not in my hut and you are going to read it on a bench four hours from the place the lot is in, and I want the boy's name on your sheet before the iron's.** **The one System panel of the batch** is the lot, before the hour rather than after: a lot may be read twice; a reading is an hour and a cost and the cost is a part of the toll and it is the same toll every time; **a certificate that says a lot is clean is not a reading and cannot be wrong about the toll, because it has no column for the toll**; a holder who is not at the custody is a holder who is not there, and a custody may be ended only by the person who holds it; you may not read a document nobody has handled, you may not read a person, you may not ask a lot where the toll lands, **and if you ask it for more you will be given what you have already been given**; and none of this is an answer. **The reading is done on the bench in the hut at the fourth place for about an hour and it is the eleven of March word for word, TOLL: THE ORIGIN, and it says ELEVEN USES in March and ELEVEN USES in April.** The cost is entered in his own hand: **he cannot remember which gate he went out of, and there is no west gate, and in March it took a direction off him and today it took a way out of the city, and a departure is what this thing charges.** The departure is dated in the entry he reads out: **the seventeenth of February at six in the morning, to look at a pumping engine, and his own book says the west gate.** The finding: **a toll on a place takes memory of departure; he has paid it twice and got a piece of it each time and the second piece is smaller.** The intervals have gone up on use - seven days, then eleven, then fourteen, and the next permitted hour is not before the eleventh of next month - and Stenn enters that this is not a rule getting stricter and will not defend it, and Adrian says he does not know what it is and is not going to help anybody name it. On the twenty-eighth **Nessa Pell enters a boy's four questions under a heading nobody in this district has ever printed, NOT ANSWERED** - who tells us, by whom, for how long, is anybody obliged to answer - and gives, at last, her own reason for nineteen years of refusing to put a question in that book: **a question is a thing somebody is owed, and if I put one in then I am the clerk who put it in, and a clerk cannot be owed anything, and I would rather it was on a wall where it is nobody's.** Teo reads it first and says the method is on the same wall. And **Nevin Roke asks for his name on the sheet before the iron's and says he is nobody's keeper, does not know what the iron is, and will not carry it anywhere again**, and the finding is that a custody whose keeper is entered as nobody is not a custody, it is a place where a thing is.

## Chapter 0079 - The Two Who Hold It
The twenty-ninth of April, opened on four people and a reader at a table in the front room of a house at the ninth place, and a fifth person in the doorway who was not asked to come in. **The asking took fifty-one minutes and eleven of them were the fifth thing on a slate, and both women said yes, and about twenty-two people from four houses stood at the back.** The tenth hand describes the job in about a hundred and forty words and gets to nine: **there is no train, there has not been one at this end since the eleventh of February, and the word stops people, about nine times a year, and the haulier stood on that bank for a minute and a half in the second week of last month and was not angry.** The term is not the tenth hand's to give and he says so before anything else: **a day is a thing you can hold on to when somebody is cross with you and a month is a thing you cannot.** A month, from the twenty-ninth of April to the twenty-ninth of May. Sef's attachment: **the man in the hut has to be able to say the word, and she will teach him, and he says it in front of her once before anybody else.** Sallow's answer to the fourth thing: **I will answer for her about a pair and I will not answer for her about the word.** The woman of the column's own question, unasked for: **what happens on the twenty-ninth of May - and the answer entered is that somebody has to come up this bank and ask.** Then the thing the morning was for. **The word is a word about people and not about a train, and she liked it and did not tell anybody, and she will not have a book that says her sentence is about a train.** And: **there are stones, four feet of them, laid in the ninth year, and she has known for eleven years and never told anybody and has been waiting eleven years for somebody to ask her a question about water that was not about how much of it is.** She asks to be asked twice - once without the stones in the question and once with them in it - and the two answers are not the same: **somebody should have told us**, and **nobody is to lay a stone.** **The finding: the second answer is the first one with a name in it, and this is the first time this district has costed a question, and it cost two questions and about four minutes and nobody has to pay anything.** Then Sef asks for the word to be held at the fourth place as well, and is refused, and the refusal is the finding of the day: **the line does not know it has stopped working, because a line is not a person and there is nobody standing at the end of it to find out.** Sallow offers a ground signal and is refused in the fastest no of the morning: **a place that reports every morning is a place nobody goes to.** The woman of forty-five was not asked and it is in the count. The sheet goes down the bank at one o'clock, reaches the yard at seven, is entered on the first, and the delay is entered as the board's. And in the last hour of light, going down, a woman carrying a pail says the four words that establish the standing failure: **you cannot see it from down there.**

## Chapter 0080 - The Twenty-Ninth of Last Month
The first to the third of May, opened on a man asking a stranger what a sentence on a wall means now, and **both sheets in this city have a date on it and both of them are now in last month, and one of them is a lie and one of them is a truth and it is not on the paper which is which.** The board publishes its **fifteenth part** on the first, the shortest it has ever published, in two halves: the west end of the line held by two people who can only be asked, and a hut held by an office that renews itself and asks nobody, both for one month, neither seen by a member of the board. **Four standing failures are gathered onto one page for the first time, dated and not struck** - the line of chalk cannot be seen from the end it is for and the fixing is a bushel of white paint against the fourteen pints the rate bought; the word is a word about people; the line does not know it has stopped working; and the hut is held by an office that renews itself. The registrar's nine words under them: **a failure that is entered is not fixed, it is known.** At ten o'clock on the first **Rutt reads the whole of the twenty-ninth of April out loud, answers instead of refusals, and finds that it is harder: giving does not have a shape, and about nine people did not look at the sheet at all and the ninth was a boy who had read it first** - and the finding that a person asked to be read back can name the sentence they need repeated. On the second of this month **Hessa Krell is asked the question she has been waiting a fortnight for, is it a rate, and says that three times is a favour and four times is a rate, and a column is opened in the counting room with one line in it and the fourth carry of eight hundred and ninety-four coppers in it.** The ninth can of oil goes up the bank on the first and the trader puts her own name against the sixty-three coppers and writes nine words: **he has got four weeks of oil. Nobody has asked him about the fifth.** The key turns three times and the sentence under the two lapses gets a third line: **a yard asked and a yard got a yes, and a yard is not a board.** The fifth thing is still unnamed, the toll column is still empty, **the office's name appears once, spelled out in four entries in a column of sixty-one the line book has not got, in a sleeve at 411, and it is not on a wall this month, and that is also entered.** The slate goes past in the kitchen at a quarter to seven on the second and **Rutt writes down that four people have now told her about it and she is not going to ask, because the answer would be in a room and not in a book, and this district has stopped insisting and that is a change in her and is not entered in a column.** Adrian noticed she had written it and did not ask, and not asked is in the count. And the last thing is at about six in the morning on the third in a hut nine miles west, where a man of thirty-nine says a nine-word sentence five times in front of a woman holding a lamp, and on the fifth he says it the way she says it, and she tells the reader of this, and the reader of this is anybody who comes up that bank, and she has decided to allow it.

## Rolling handoff, after Chapter 0070 — Volume 02 Batch 0002 complete

**The walk happened and it produced one thing the district cannot use.** A boundary of chalk sixty yards past the water column, held by **two named people** - the keeper of the hut and the woman at the column, who is also the water - **from the twenty-ninth of March to the twenty-ninth of April, renewable only by asking both of them again in a room with the asking entered**, on a published entry that says in its own words that it is not the fifth thing. **Its standing failure is entered: it cannot be seen from the end it is for.** Adrian is **still a Stage 1 Reader** and the volume has not promoted him; he read the Tongue once in this volume and **closed nothing**, and was asked twice in a room and in a yard to close a multi-bearer split and said no twice, the second no four times as long as the first, and the draft is **published in full in the public lot book because a document nobody can close should not be in a drawer.** The **rate sheet for ground nobody has claimed is up** and its first line is that **a rate sheet is a document that says a place is a place**, and it has a second line that is not about money: **HELD BY NOBODY.** The **ward market has answered the standing order and lost by seven to three** and knows it is refusing something it could afford more than two thousand times over. **The rate on the line is fourteen coppers and nobody proposed fourteen**, and the market has printed the **first timetable in the district's history with the toll column at full width and empty.** **Nobody has been paid anything.** The key has lapsed twice in a month and is now with a man who cannot read figures. **Tarin Kest has surrendered the lease, the stabling and the standing, and the Road House is what is left of the difference**, and nobody let him off. **Halloway Reed's fare is complete and the district can prove he is wrong in a sentence and cannot prove it in a book**, and his answer prints on a Monday. **The reading ledger is closed at one, with two non-events entered and no interval between them.** And the **fifth thing is still unnamed, printed as an empty column in the middle of a printed timetable**, and the answer to the standing objection has still not been given by anybody in Alder Reach.

---

## Rolling handoff, after Chapter 0080 — Volume 02 Batch 0003 complete

**The volume has turned from the cost to the instrument, and the instrument is a method rather than a paper.** A chandler asked how to ask a person whether they would do a job; the yard answered it in nine minutes and put it on a wall; the method was then used on the reader himself, on a signalman in a hut, and on the two women who hold the west end of a railway. **A guild inspector said the truest sentence anybody has said about that iron in thirteen years and it is now the spine of the batch: the lot is clean and cannot be sold to a village.** The **Harrow Tongue is not in a case, is not in a hut it belongs in, and is on a bench four hours up a bank with a boy of nineteen who is nobody's keeper** - the only iron on the line that can turn a train is in the only hut on the line with no frame in it, and the west end is an order and not a length. A **bridge administration staffed a hut at a place it says is unsafe and said so on every published day, and the district found out that the safest and the most hated are the same office, and did not take the man out.** The **counterfeit printed a Monday with four coppers in the market's empty column and gave the beating away for nothing and was not thanked and was not converted and is still right about four things.** The **lease turned out to name a firm and not a person and a shed that is not there any more, so the answer to who owns the engine's oil is nobody, in a book at the end of a table in a room with a board over the window that nobody had gone to look at.** A **reading cost a man the memory of which gate he left his own city by**, and the object gave him the same one word it gave him in March. A **fifteen-year-old's four questions are in the public book under a heading that says NOT ANSWERED**, and nobody is obliged to answer, and the boy says the no is an answer. And on **the twenty-ninth of April the boundary was renewed in a room, asked by a signalman who cannot be paid, answered yes by both women, and the word was asked for at the fourth place and refused, because the line does not know it has stopped working.** Adrian is **still a Stage 1 Reader**; the fifth instrument is **still unnamed and still printed as an empty column at full width**; and the twenty-ninth of May is now the next date anybody in this district owns, and **nobody has been given the job of going up that bank to ask.**

## Chapter 0081 - Three Boards
The fourth of May, opened on a clerk chalking a rectangle on a board in the ward market counting room and complaining about the width before she drew it a third time. **Wren Ostler, the market's second clerk, six years at a counter, designs a toll board in about four minutes and Adrian Vale is not asked and contributes nothing and asks for that to be entered.** It is nine feet by three on two posts tarred at the foot, a heading at the top, a four-foot column ruled in pencil at its two ends and empty between them, and along the bottom a line for a keeper's name, a line for a date, and one line anybody may write a single sentence on that nobody will be asked to explain. **It is not the fifth thing and the board says so on its face: a board is a place to write down what a toll is and a place to write down what a toll is cannot state what a toll is, and a board cannot ask the object anything, and the day somebody prints a number in that column the column is a lie and the date on it will be the date the lie went in.** It came out of a reader's account of a fifth stranger at the counter on the second of this month who stood for a minute and a half reading the market's empty column and then said he had come four miles to be told something was missing and had been told so in the correct type, and **the finding is that a sheet can be taken cold by anybody in this basin and about four hundred of them took one cold, and a board cannot be taken cold because a board is in a place, and a place is the only thing in this district that has ever been able to make anybody stand still.** The three places are the east end, the fourth and the ninth; the sixth is a drain going onto the ballast since before the Long Fracture and the eighth is a curve with a four-foot shoulder and a nine-foot drop, and both reasons stand and neither is struck, and a clerk finds the reason a fourth place on a board is wrong in about ninety seconds: a board with a place on it that nobody stands at is a mark, and a mark may not carry a job. The wall's five things are used, not re-derived. **The second keeper is Corvin Sallow, thirty-nine, a signalman of a bridge administration, and he is asked in the method for the first time anybody in this district has asked a question of it, and he says yes and gives the reason in nine words: KEEPING A BOARD IS NOT HOLDING A PAIR. NOTHING IS CHARGED TO THE PERSON WHO KEEPS IT. NOBODY CAN BE SENT TO A BOARD, and if the only thing at the board is a piece of chalk then he is the keeper of a piece of chalk, and a man in a hut who keeps a piece of chalk is not a man in an office, and he refuses before he is asked to be a bearer: a bearer is a person and an office is not a person and a slot is not a person and a line of stones is not a person, and this is none of those four things.** The first keeper is Wren Ostler, who says yes in four seconds and gives three conditions and a disclaimer, including that she keeps it in the hours the counter is shut and that she is the second clerk of this market and not the market. The room then fails to find the third keeper in about eleven minutes and the failure is entered as a failure, and the reason it is a failure is that the three names put forward are a lamp-man, a haulier who goes up the bank twice a week, and a man of about fifty who has stood in a doorway at the west end for four years and has never been asked what he is for, and the tenth hand refuses to go and ask him in a room with thirty people in it because a boy of seventeen worked out in April that if you ask somebody in front of a crowd the true thing comes back to the wrong man. **A woman of about thirty-eight with a hook and a sacking sheet, who buys old paper off four houses once a month and is in no roll in this city, does the sixth thing at about two o'clock, states the question flat, and gives her name into the minute when the clerk asks for it, which is the first time in nine weeks that a person at the back of a room in Alder Reach has given a name to be written down.** The cost is six coppers of lettering from Abel Troth, sixty-one, who charged three a board and took two, and it comes out of the two hundred coppers a quarter the ninth place owes, which is the first money ever spent at that place, and the finding is that the first time a rate has been spent on the thing it was collected for, and that the sheet it came off says on its face that a rate sheet is a document that says a place is a place.

## Chapter 0082 - The Name at the Top
The sixth to the ninth of May, opened on a sign-writer of sixty-one beginning at the top left with a stencil and a twine and a boy of nine not asked to hold the other end. **The first thing anybody writes on a toll board in this district is a name: THE STANDING OFFICE OF CROSS-DISTRICT PASSAGE, ESTABLISHED, AND NOT AT THIS PLACE, in full, in letters three inches high, and it takes about nine minutes because he was given the source and told to copy it exactly.** The governing sentence of the batch is the argument about where it goes: **a name at the top is a heading and a name in the middle is an amount, and this district cannot print an amount**, and it is put at the top because a heading can be a thing nobody can find and a figure in the middle of a board is a promise nobody in this district is in a position to make. The column is four feet wide because a column printed narrow looks like a column that has been kept quiet, said by the sign-writer and credited to the market. The board ends up with eleven lines, four of which are about a place nobody can find: the toll is the origin; the origin is a place and not a person; the line's own book has nine hundred and six movements since the flood year and a hundred and forty-one of them name the ninth place as the origin held; and nobody in this basin can find that office in any register this basin can reach, and the name is spelled out in four entries in a column of sixty-one the line book has not got and in four entries in a keeper's column headed ORIGIN HELD, and the four in one book are not the four in the other, so there is a fifth somewhere. **The middle is ruled at its two ends and empty, and the reason is printed inside it at about the height of a man's eye, and the order of the lines was argued about for an hour and the reason goes inside the column and not underneath it, because a reason underneath is a reason a person standing in front of the board will read second or not at all, and the sheet is printed and not carved and not chalked and will have to be done again in nine years and the clerk entered that with a date on it and no name on it.** The room tries three times in twenty minutes to put the ninth place in the middle of the board and refuses three times: a reading may state what kind of thing is charged and may not state which one, and a board is worse than a reader, because a reading is done by one man in a hut in an hour with two people comparing the hand afterwards, and a board stands in a street for eleven years and gets read by people who will never meet the man who wrote it, **because a reader is the only instrument in this district that takes the whole of a thing away from him before he writes it down, and a board keeps it.** A stranger of the fifth kind asks the question nobody had asked: *if I want to complain about that, who do I complain to*, and the entry is nine words long: **he can complain to nobody, that is the toll.** Then four people in four rooms spend about two hours failing to find the office: a registrar through the only complete run of public offices in the basin, which is a list of things that collected money and about a third of the entries have no money against them; a bridge clerk of thirty-four through his own office's standing instructions, who does not tell the room until the eighth and gives the reason first, that a man who has found a thing in his own office's papers and has not said so for a day is a man who has had a day to decide what it looks like; a licensed valuer of forty-one through the guild's register, which lists offices still collecting fees in the fourth year and cannot hold one that was not; and **a sign-writer of sixty-one through thirty-one years of lettering jobs with the customers' names on the spine, who finds the phrase twice in his own hand on two boards he made in the sixth year for the water office and who has no office, no customer, and nobody who gave him the words.** Two hundred coppers is collected on the seventh from four households out of a pail of change by the market's own collector, who does not read figures and was given the figure and not the reason, and the finding is that the ninth place's rate is the first money this district has spent there and the other half is that **nobody at that place was asked whether they agreed to the rate, and a rate that is not asked for is a rate that has not been agreed to**, written by the registrar for the fourth time and the second time about a document. A plasterer who refused a drawer in April in front of sixty people reads the finished board in a lane for three minutes, finds a notice about a drain by a man dead nine years on the bottom line, and corrects it in chalk, and that is the first sentence on a toll board in this district. The ninth-place board goes up on the eighth on a shoulder about eleven feet above a nine-foot drop, and **the most-read board in this district is on the most dangerous piece of ground anybody in the basin has put a sheet of paper on, and the two facts were true on the same day and have nothing to do with each other.** The fourth-place board goes up on the ninth, kept by a woman of about thirty-eight who says the week on the board is the week she is on that bank for her own trade, and that she is not a signal hand and does not know what a pair is and wants that on the board too.

## Chapter 0083 - The Man Who Cannot Close It
