The tenth of May, opened on a man carrying a table up four flights of stairs above a chandler's shop, and the room cost six coppers for the day out of the two hundred the ninth place paid. **Five people in the room who were not there to listen to Adrian Vale: a signal keeper of fifty-one, the woman of the column who is the water, a mechanic of forty-three, a woman of forty-four who keeps a Road House on the tide, and a clerk of six years who is the ward market and is not a person.** Two of them walked five and a quarter hours down a bank on the previous night with four changeovers, in a cart for the first four miles at eleven coppers each way out of the same two hundred, and slept in an empty room over the counting office with a bed in it and a window that shuts. The woman of the column said on arrival that every time somebody from this city comes up that bank they come with a document, and the railway is four hundred yards from her door and **she is not the railway**, and she came without a document and asked for that to be written down at the point where somebody asked her what she thought of a place being named as the origin of a toll. **The room strikes the sixth and eighth places out of his own March draft in the first hour, on reasons that were in the book already and were not his, and he says he is not striking them and asks for it to say the room struck them.** Three places are named: the fourth, the east end, and the Harrow end, and the fourth place is a place a thing is in and not a place a person is at. Then the rotation is invented in about eleven minutes, after a question from the woman of the column about whose week is the first one, **and the finding is that a rotation is not a rotation until somebody has written down the order of the weeks, and eleven weeks of asking whether a thing could be held produced nobody who had ever written down which week came first** — three bearers hold a week in turn with named substitutes entered in advance, and the order was settled by a mechanic with a pencil on the back of a trade's invoice, and the first week belongs to the keeper because she is the only one of the three at that end of the line every day. **The fourth bearer has no place, no week and no pair, and is the person who ends the use, and it is not a slot: a slot is a thing you can fill in later, and this is a person who has to be found before a thing starts, and if the three who hold the weeks name them then the man who wrote the document does not get a part in it, and that is the whole of the point of having three.** Adrian Vale says the thing he came to say in four seconds, before the room gets to it — **I cannot close it; I may draft a split and I may not close one, and there is no version of me that closes this, and that has been in the book since the second of April and is not going to stop being in the book because five people have been kind to me about it** — and asks for the fact that he said it first to be entered, and the tenth hand enters the count of four times and puts up the answer on the notice board: **you do not have to be the instrument, you built the method that finds the instrument, and those are two different jobs and one of them is yours and one of them is not.** The mechanic then says what he has been not saying since the eighteenth of March, that he has known since the eighteenth of March that the origin is that village, has not said it in April or in May, and that every one of those not-sayings cost somebody something and the somebody was never him. The origin is then named as **a decision and not a reading**, with the answer in the order the woman gave it: **I do not like it, and I say yes**, and her name stays off it and the place goes on it, and the keeper's sentence goes in in her own words — the word is said by a person and not by a board, and if anybody ever puts that word on a board she will stop holding this and say so on the day she stops — and the entry is that a decision and a reading are two different things and only one of them is on the sheet, and the object has not been asked and did not agree. The last hour goes to the Road House on the tide, who had said she would not be there by eleven and was there from half past eight and said nothing until eleven, and who refuses one clause and gives the right one: any of the four may end the use at any time, **and every one of them has to say the reason out loud in a room with two other people in it, and the reason goes in a public book, because a man who can stop a railway by being tired of it is not a limit, and a man who can stop a railway by saying out loud in front of two people why he is stopping it is the only kind of stop anybody in this district has ever had**, and the mechanic takes it off the table and concedes in public that he had been arguing for the wrong version of it since March and that a reason is not a courtesy to the man who is stopped but for the people standing there when he stops it. The tenth hand, who had asked twice whether he wished to speak before the asking started and said no twice, reads the whole morning back at twenty to one before anybody stands up; the keeper stops him and has one word corrected because the board and the street are not the same word; and the clerk of the ward market states the question flat, which is the sixth thing done for the first time by a person who is one of the five being asked. The sheet goes down the bank at one o'clock in a satchel against a boy's chest, is in Alder Reach at seven, is entered on the eleventh at nine, and the delay is entered by the registrar for the fourth time, **with a second block that says the part of the entry about the man who wrote it down is one line: he drafted it and he could not close it, and he is not one of the four and he is not a keeper of a board and he is not on the committee, and this is the first document in this district's history where that is true of him on the face of it.**

## Chapter 0084 - Beneath the Board
The fourteenth to the eighteenth of May, opened on a boy of seventeen taking the Harrow line book off a dry board above a stove in a hut nine miles west and another piece of paper coming away with it. **The lot is on a bench in that hut with a boy of nineteen asleep beside it and the boy of seventeen is not on a roll in any book in Alder Reach and is paid four coppers a quarter for an index and is not Adrian Vale's apprentice and has said so in a yard.** He came up on his own time, five and a quarter hours with four changeovers, slept in the empty room over the counting office, and was the only person who asked to go. The book is on a dry board because a book kept low in that hut gets damp in February, and behind two ledgers in clips, folded once, was a sheet of printed paper that had been behind the book for longer than either ledger had been on the board. **It is a water office notice dated the second of the sixth year: the column at mile one and three quarters is metered, readings are taken quarterly, the water is not to be drawn between six in the morning and six in the evening while the pump is working, and at the foot, a place is a place and a counter is a counter.** Above the fold line, where a man's name or an office's name would go on a printed notice of that date, there is an impression of a circle broken in one place, about the size of a thumbnail, not printed with the text, not in the same ink, pressed into the paper rather than laid on it, and the same brown as the paper around it, and the finding entered is that the impression is old and nobody can say how old, and that the state's condition of paper is not a date. He does not open it until a quarter to nine, when he is looking for something to lay the book on. The keeper comes in at half past nine and is told what has been behind her own wall for eleven years, and says the thing that is the finding of the night: there were four of those in her book in eleven years and she carried every one and never asked anybody about any of them and never wrote them down because she did not know what a person writes when they do not know, **and she would have written the same shape twice, and she has been asked four times in eleven weeks whether she would hold something and has said yes three times and has never once been asked a question about a piece of paper on her own wall, and the reason is that a piece of paper is not a job and a job is the only thing anybody in this district knows how to ask her about.** The boy of nineteen is awake and says one thing, which the keeper writes down herself because there is no minute-taker: that is the one you said you were sorry about, on the eighteenth of March, not the iron, and that he says the iron is iron and that is a thing a person can be sure about, and he is nineteen and has never been sure of a thing in his life. **Three people say what it is on the afternoon of the fifteenth. A paperer of about thirty-eight, in the hut for her own trade, says it is the water office's mark because it is a water office notice and offices have marks, and is wrong. A bridge clerk of thirty-four puts four sheets from his own office on the dry board in ninety seconds and shows a man's name and a man's hand and initials in the corner on all four, and the notice has none of them, and a circle broken in one place where a name would go, and he says the water office has no mark because a mark has to be cut and the office has never had a die, and then says I do not know, and notes that it is the fourth time he has written that sentence in three months and that a man who writes it four times is either the most honest clerk in the basin or the worst, and that he has not worked out which and has not asked anybody.** The boy of seventeen is right about what it is — an impression on a thing, not a name, not a signature, not a letter, not part of the notice — and wrong about what it means, and says so in a room with a door on it: that two impressions of one thing on two unrelated papers are evidence that one body made both, and that they are not, they are evidence that one thing was pressed into two things, and the registrar stops him and enters both halves in two lines and says the reason she gives every time, **and adds the sentence nobody had said: the first line has five impressions in it and the second line has one, and there is no name on the second one in the place where a name goes, and that is on the sheet and there is nothing else to say about it tonight.** He says I do not agree and she says I know, that is why it is in the book in two lines and not in one, and that is the entire function of a book. On the morning of the seventeenth she lays the five existing impressions out on a table herself — a Warden fuel return, a school ration ledger, a pre-Long-Fracture salt permit with the regional heading scraped away and paper not from this basin, a bus manifest, and a counterfeit claim — and refuses to take the counterfeit out, **because a man who prints a false claim is entitled to press a false thing into a sheet of paper, and if we leave it out then we have decided in advance what kind of person presses these, and that is a decision about people again**, and the boy says they are the same mark and is made to say the second one, and gets the better sentence: that it is the same shape, and he does not know why the words came out in that order and would like to know. **The water office's own form list is complete to the sixth year and does not contain the number of that column, and the notice is dated the second of the sixth year, and the finding is that a clerk was wrong about his own office in the direction of believing the office, which is the direction that made his own sentence harder for him to say, and the registrar entered that she would rather have the harder sentence.** He asks a second time in a yard on the evening of the sixteenth, standing up, and is refused in about four seconds, and the answer he had in the first week of March is in the book and has not changed. **What the district did with a second impression was nothing, and the nothing is on a page with six impressions on it, and the page says A SECOND IMPRESSION IS NOT A SECOND ANSWER — I have been asked three times in two days whether this changes anything and the answer is no and I am going to write the no down instead of the argument, because the argument is sixty words every time and the no is two — and that what it changes is one thing: it changes what we know about where the first one is not.** The boy reads that page standing and says it is the first page in this district that says we were wrong about where it is not and does not apologise for the eleven years. The ninth-place board has eleven sentences on the bottom line and one of them is a question nobody has answered, and the binding of the second copy of the line book came back down the bank on the eighteenth and is in the hut on a shelf beside a stove, and the finding is that a book kept nine miles from the only office that keeps a copy of it is a book that can be lost, and this district has decided that is the correct place for it.

## Chapter 0085 - Thirty-Six Coppers
The seventeenth, eighteenth and nineteenth of May, opened on a bushel of white paint going up the bank on a handcart after standing behind a cooper's shop for eleven days. **The standing failure that the line of chalk cannot be seen from the west end of the siding, which is the end it is for, is entered as fixed, at four coppers, and the finding is that somebody asked a chandler, which is the fourth time this district has had it.** **Pell Anselm, a chandler of about thirty-four with a counter of oil on Ash Row and a stall in front of it**, lost a day of his stall on the walk and nobody paid either man. Then the batch's instrument: **a security is a thing somebody else can go and look at**, nine words, the only definition of a security this district has ever worked with, against a draft that has carried *security none offered and none posted* since the second of April and has been true for seven weeks because the only man who could have posted one is a reader and a reader may not. **He says in four seconds that he cannot post a security, for the fourth time, and the mechanic says then somebody else has to, and then describes the three days he spent thinking about it, which was that a security on a railway is worth what a railway can be made to cost, and a man with a yard and eleven vehicles a week has a figure in him that would frighten anybody, and a figure like that in a public book is a weapon and not a security, and the woman of the Road House confirms she arrived at the same place in four seconds and has been insulting him for three days for taking three.** Four things are then posted in about two hours. **The Needle Road House posts four hundred and ten coppers of standing salvage, his own figure entered at his own request in April by a foreman of twenty-two years, and answers the objection that it cannot be inspected with the reason that the inspection is not the looking, the inspection is that a man can come, and he will pay for the cart and put it back out of his own stock before the man gets home.** The ward market posts eleven coppers a quarter for the bench, the lamp, the chalk and the oil, which is the figure a river office has been paying out of its Harrow works rate for one month, and the entry says the money and the man came apart in the ninth year and have been apart ever since because an administration does not put a man's wage in a rate, **so there are two numbers in a public book about one man, eleven coppers a quarter for the bench and nothing for the man, and not a nil, because nobody in this district has ever costed a signalman and this district has costed a pump.** The ninth place posts the siding, the column, the hut and the ground between them, which a lease named a firm, which was surrendered on the thirtieth of March, and which has stood on the rate sheet since as HELD BY NOBODY. And the fourth is **thirty-six coppers of metered water, four a quarter for nine years, owed to nobody since the thirtieth of March, posted by the people they are owed to, to nobody, entered with the word *hostage* written after it in brackets, and the finding is that this is the only security in the district that cannot be taken away because there is nobody entitled to take it, and that this district now has a security that cannot be taken and a book that has admitted in print that it does not know what to call it**, and the woman of the column's condition is entered alongside it: if anybody ever finds out who was paying for that water she wants it off the sheet in a week and does not care whose name it is. **A Road House on the tide refuses to post anything at all, on the ground that the day a Road House posts a security on a line is the day every haulier through its yard is told what that line costs by the firm that is the door, and then offers her yard once a month for as long as this is in being, and insists it be entered as not a security, because in about four years somebody is going to write that her firm stood behind a public charter, and it did not, and it lent a yard — and a rehearsal is a walk done before the day it is for, and there has been one on this line in nine years and it took six hours each way in the rain.** The four people who are posting are then asked in the wall's method in about twenty minutes, the first time it has been used on people who are all in one room and all know each other's answers; two of the four say no inside eleven minutes and give no reason and give the reasons afterwards, and the minute enters that the rule and the practice are not the same thing. The mechanic — **who is Tarin Kest, and who is named in this chapter in the sentence about the four hundred and ten coppers and again in the minute about the lease** — answers the fourth question for Pell Anselm before it is asked, naming a paperer who was not in the room, and the woman of the column answers the fifth before it is asked, in the same eleven words as April. **A man of thirty-nine then writes one line in the margin in his own hand and nothing else: AN OFFICE IS NOT A PERSON AND I AM NOT ONE OF THE FOUR AND NOBODY HAS ASKED ME TO BE, and two days later adds two more lines asking for it to be read out at a counter and not at a rail, and the clerk reads it out at about ten o'clock on the nineteenth in front of about thirty people, and the finding is that a man in an office asked to be read out and not spoken at, and that this is the first request of its kind anybody in this district has made, and that it was made by the man nobody wanted standing in that hut in the first place.** The sheet goes down the bank at eleven at night and up it at six the next evening and is entered on the eighteenth at nine, and the thing in the book is not a sum, it is four things and a yard that is not a security, **and not one of the four can be turned into a sum without a rate meeting, and a rate meeting cannot amend a document it has already refused.** And the fourth bearer is still not named, and the minute says he is not a slot and is not vacant and will be named by three people who are not in this room, and that Mr Vale has no part in naming him.

## Chapter 0086 - Who Goes Up the Bank
The twentieth to the twenty-third of May, opened on the clerk writing a time on a sheet, covering it, and crossing it out three times in about four minutes, because the second thing on a wall is how long with an hour on it and nobody in this district knows how long a question takes when the asking is five and a quarter hours away. **Fourteen people say no in nine days and the reasons are in a book in their own words and four of the fourteen gave the same reason and the reason is the walk**, and the finding is that this district has asked forty-one people a question in nine weeks and got two yeses and both came out of a room and neither off a board. The reasons include a haulier who is on that bank twice a week and who has been up it about four hundred times in nine years and has never once been asked what for and says he will probably say no and that being asked is worth a fortnight of the walk; a plasterer who is the eleventh name on a sheet of nineteen refusals from the sixth of April and who says a woman read his no out at the counter with the word NO in front of it and that he has not stopped thinking about it; a woman with a counter of fish who told them in April what it is like to hold a thing for four days and come back and find it was open the whole time; a man whose hand will not hold a pen in the cold; a man at a burial on the twenty-ninth; and a woman of thirty-five who would go if somebody else went and be told what to ask, and was told there is no script, and withdrew the offer in writing, and the entry has the word *understood* left out on purpose. Nine more said they would go if the walk were shorter. **The lot offers him the hour on the morning of the twentieth, in the batch's one System panel: a reading may be spent on one lot you have touched, it costs about an hour and a cost, the cost is a part of that lot's toll, the toll on this iron is the origin, an origin takes memory of departure, he has paid it twice, the second time took less than the first, the iron does not hold places and asked for more gives you what you have been given, a reading of a board is not a reading, a document nobody has handled may not be read, a person may not be read, you may not ask a lot where the toll lands, and a reading moves nothing on that line — you have been told this and you wrote it down, and you may take the hour or not take it and neither is a favour to anybody.** He does not take it. The non-event is entered with no cost and no time. Ivo Stenn asks whether it is cowardice or discipline and gets nine words, and asks for the clerk to enter that he noticed the sentence has got shorter each of the four times. **Nessa Pell enters that nobody is going to write it as cowardice and nobody is going to write it as discipline, and that if either word is ever written against the entry she will take it out, and that she has never taken a line out of anything in nineteen years and is not starting with this one.** Her finding is the batch's: **the cost of a reading is not a figure and is not a duration and is a countable number of the specific thing it takes, and nobody in this district had been counting it, and a man who counts it can say no with a number in his mouth instead of a principle** — and the number is departures, and he has two left. He enters in advance that he is not coming up the bank on the twenty-ninth, in eleven words, and is asked for a reason once, and gives the reason of the whole batch: he has been the man on every page of this since the eighth of March, and if he is on the bank then the twenty-ninth of May is a thing he did, and the way to stop that sentence being true in four years is for him not to be on the bank. The tenth hand enters the half of the reason Adrian did not give, that he does not know how long the asking takes and would be guessing in front of a person about the length of a room, and that a man guessing about the length of a room in front of the two people who live in it is a man deciding something about them. **Wren Ostler then says yes before she is asked, for the fifth time in nine weeks that somebody has answered a question about themselves before it was put**, and says the reason is that she held the key twice in March, on the second and again on the twelfth, four days each time, and the first was a lapse and nobody came and the second was a turn, and that a rotation that turns once is a rotation that has been shown it can, and that if she goes up there then this district has a rotation that has turned twice, and the second turn will be a clerk, and a clerk is a person, and a person can be replaced. She names the sixth thing to a paperer who was not in the room and says she chose her and that the paperer did not agree to be chosen and said yes afterwards. A fourth yes and then a written withdrawal, and the finding is that a yes is not a hold and a hold is not a term. **A rehearsal is walked on the twenty-first by four people who left at half past four in the morning, and it took five and a quarter hours going and five and a half coming and eleven and three-quarters in the day, **which is ten and three-quarters of walking and an hour of standing still**, and the number on the sheet the clerk is going to carry up on the twenty-ninth is her own figure and not the tenth hand's and neither of them is going to argue about it in front of two people**, and the boy of nineteen came unasked to see the hut from the outside and said again that he is nobody's keeper. The entry is that the entry was also made by a bridge clerk of thirty-four in the water office's own book, because that book is copied twice a quarter and goes where a sheet does not go. **And under the heading on a wall that says NOT ANSWERED, under the first line anybody has entered under it in nineteen years, the second line went in on the twentieth: THE PERSON IS A CLERK OF A MARKET AND SHE IS NOT ASKED YET.**

## Chapter 0087 - The Twenty-Fifth
The twenty-fourth, twenty-fifth and twenty-sixth of May, opened on a man of thirty-nine in a signalman's coat picking up a folded sheet from under the leg of a bench in a hut nine miles west, which is his own month, stamped by his own office, dated the twenty-fifth of last month, in the hut and not in a book, and which is under the leg of the bench because the desk is in use and a man who has been sleeping on it for twenty-nine days puts things under the low end so they do not slide. **He says it aloud to nobody: it is my own term, it runs out tomorrow, there is no clerk's letter about it because there is no need of one because it renews itself, and I have not once asked anybody whether that was a good arrangement, because an office does not ask an office whether it is a good arrangement.** On the twenty-fourth, before anything else, a copy clerk of the ward market walks into a river administration on his own time with the question written down first, and is told the date in about ninety seconds, and is told in the same ninety seconds that there is nobody to ask, and that if you want to know whether it is renewed you are asking a person and there is not a person; and **the finding is that a public office will tell you when and will not tell you who, and a public board will tell you who and cannot tell you when.** On the twenty-fifth the batch's governing sentence arrives: **an office that lets a term lapse has not ended anything; it has stopped saying when.** Nothing happens. The hut is there, the man is there, the oil went in at six, the chamber silts in a fortnight and he cleared it with a shovel that belongs to the office and that he is allowed to use, and at six in the evening he wrote the date on the paper on the desk for the nineteenth time and under it he wrote one word, *held*, and entered that he does not know whether it is the correct word. **The same day somebody painted a second line on the ninth-place board in the same size of type as the name at the top: THE HUT AT THIS PLACE IS HELD BY AN OFFICE UNTIL A DATE. THE DATE IS WRITTEN HERE. THE OFFICE HAS NOT ASKED. THE OFFICE CONSIDERS THIS PLACE UNSAFE AND HAS SAID SO IN A PUBLIC YARD ELEVEN TIMES IN EIGHT YEARS — the first line on a public board in this district written by somebody who is not the office it is about**, and he says afterwards that the sentence was true on the twenty-fifth of April as well and that he did not write it on the eighth because there were four people behind him asking about a barrow and a week, and that this is the first thing to have happened in that hut in nine years that nobody had a meeting about and he has not decided whether that is good. The district then asks itself, in the counting room with about thirty people in it, whether it can make an office ask, and answers no in eleven minutes through four questions, and the finding is that it has spent nine weeks building a way to ask a person and has not built one, **and that the two things on this line that cannot ask each other were standing four hours apart on the same morning and neither of them noticed the other**; and that a bridge administrator has said on published days for nine years that an office that has to explain itself is an office that can be stopped, and this district has agreed with him eleven times and never once given him a reason why he is wrong. **What the district can do it does in four minutes: it can stop paying for the hut**, because the market can vote on eleven coppers a quarter and the office cannot be asked, and so there are two numbers in a public book about one man. The vote is set for the twenty-seventh. On the afternoon of the twenty-fifth, **Selik Marne says three things from a rail in Alder Reach on a published day, and nobody believed he would stop and he did not stop: that he sent the man and the man is not paid and he has never pretended otherwise; that it is not enough because a hut is not a line and a train on that siding has stood a hundred and three days and cannot go either way; and that an office that has to ask is an office that can be refused, and there is a bridge in this city with a shut span and a suspended certificate and a public review of its own order since the eighth of March across which he has carried four boxes a day because nobody could refuse him, and if he can be refused today he can be refused in April and the river does not care.** Asked what he wants, he says he wants the river office to be able to staff a hut at a place it says is unsafe without putting the name of a man who can be asked into a book, and he knows what he is asking for, because he is asking for the one thing in this district that is cheaper for a man in an office than for a woman in a doorway. **The tenth hand answers him in front of about twenty people, in the ordinary voice: the reason he has never given is that on the ninth of April a boy of seventeen stopped a mechanic from going up a bank to ask a woman a question and was right and the mechanic accepted it and named a date, and that boy is not going to stop a bridge administrator, and so the district's one working instrument against a thing like Selik Marne is a boy who happens to be in the room** — and Selik Marne reads the entry and says that was correct. A woman of about fifty who is the water is then asked whether anybody in this district wants its own offices to be askable, and says yes, and that the room is talking about the wrong thing: **the man in that hut is not the problem, because if you take him out tomorrow the bench is still there and the lamp is still there and the oil is still there and the word is still hers, and in eleven years the only thing anybody has given her that she could not hold with her hands is a piece of paper with a date on it, and a man in a hut is a piece of paper with a date on it that renews itself, and she did not ask for it and does not want it, and she wants the office to be askable because then the piece of paper has somebody's name on the other side of it and not a stamp.** She refuses the word *shop* in the finding, and the tenth hand is told he may not say the word shop in her doorway, and says he was going to say *water* and that she has been holding a wall for eleven years calling it a stream, and asks to be the person who says so in a room on the twenty-ninth with a clerk in it and not at a rail in a street.

## Chapter 0088 - The Count Each Trade Carried
The twenty-sixth to the twenty-eighth of May, opened on a sheet going onto the counting-room table with eleven rows and a column at the right-hand end that has never been read out in that room, because on the twenty-fifth of March this market voted on a document that is not a rate and there is no count of trades in a minute about a document, **and the omission was entered that day in eleven words: the count each trade carried was not read out and the omission is entered because a stranger is owed the count.** Nobody draws a new sheet, on the standing refusal of a freight clerk of six years from the second of April: I have never once put a figure on a wall that I did not intend to defend line by line. The counts are read out at a quarter past ten by the reader at the ward market counter, who said yes before she was asked, **and the reason is that a refusal made in a crowd is not heard by a crowd and an omission made in a minute is not seen by a stranger, and both of those are her trade** — bakers four hundred and twelve, butchers and fishmongers three hundred and sixty-six, cordwainers two hundred and eighty-nine, smiths, wheelwrights and fitters one hundred and seventy-four, weavers, tailors and stayers four hundred and thirty-one, chandlers and oilmen two hundred and eight, haulers and carters six hundred and twenty-two, brewers and victuallers five hundred and seventeen, undertakers, limners and scriveners ninety-six, lampers and ostlers three hundred and thirty-six, public carriers five hundred and fifty; **four thousand and one, and about nine hundred households in no row and not counted anywhere including on the sheet that collects the four thousand and one**, and there is one interruption, from a man who says the nine hundred are his trade, and the reader reads the last four lines again, and he says thank you and nothing else. About ninety people are in the room, eleven of them trades, four of them in no trade and not on a roll and in because somebody held a door, one of them a woman of about sixty with a stick who has located a hole in this district's constitution three times since the eighth of March and has never once been wrong about where it was, and the minute names none of the four because naming them would make them a category. **The standing order of the ninth is voted on four arguments, three of them correct**: that eighty-eight coppers a quarter is nothing, answered in eleven words that the market refused eighty-eight a quarter for eight posts and a river office paid six thousand four hundred and eighty in nine years and neither asked the other if the thing existed; that the market cannot refuse a standing order because the last page says it is not subject to revocation by any body existing at the date of it and the body which may revoke it is created by it and consists of the administrator and two persons named by him who hold no office and may be dissolved only by the body which may revoke it, **a document that cannot be ended by anybody and that creates its own ending**; the technical objection nobody had made in nine years, made in nine words by a man of about thirty-four who mends fencing and who came to the meeting for the first time in his life — the eighth of the eight places on page eleven is Harrow, the market cannot amend it because a body that amends a document which may be dissolved only by itself is the body that revokes it, and **both of those are correct and this is a vote about which of two correct things we would rather be**; and the fourth from the two women who hold the west end, who did not apply for a post and do not intend to. **The office loses five to five, one thousand six hundred and seventeen households against one thousand eight hundred and thirty-four, on the market's own rule which is by trade and not by household, and the reason the rule is by trade is read out and is the reason the count is on the sheet: a trade is a thing that can be asked and a household is a thing that is counted** — and the man who mends fencing says out loud that a market which had read the counts in March would have known the office was going to lose on a tie, and that this district spent two months finding out by the slow method, and is not thanked and did not expect to be. The public carriers abstain for the third time on the same question with the same reason, *we are not a trade for the people the road is for*, and the reader says the fact that the reason has not changed is the most useful thing anybody has said in that room in two months, and the carriers ask for it to be entered a third time and for somebody to do something about it, because a trade that abstains three times is not a trade exercising a right, it is a hole with a name on it, and the minute says nothing was done and that the five hundred and fifty are not represented in either question. **The timetable then carries six to four, twenty-four hundred and fifty-six households against nine hundred and ninety-five, the smallest majority this market has carried on anything that is not a rate, and the market's answer is the answer it gave in March: a rate buys a service and does not buy a seat, and a seat that may be filled and cannot be emptied is a condition and not a service, and this market has spent eleven years paying for a condition and calling it a service** — and it is still true and the market still cannot amend the standing order and both halves remain correct and cannot both be acted on. The vote was for a sheet and not for a wage, and the entry says the difference between a sheet and a wage is a person, and that the sheet will therefore go out of date, and that the market has entered that it will and has not voted a date. **The money came last: the rate is fifty coppers a quarter and thirty of the fifty has never been assigned to anything by anybody since the thirteenth of March, and the market votes to take a nine-hundred-and-six-copper run of five hundred sheets out of the unassigned thirty, and **the sum that makes the gap is nine hundred and six less the twelve coppers a quarter the printed-matter column buys, which is eight hundred and ninety-four, and not nine hundred and six less the hundred and twenty the unassigned thirty comes to in a year**, so the market has voted to spend eight hundred and ninety-four coppers it does not have, on the same gap that has been carried on a trade on the lower terrace four times.** The woman who has carried it says she is not carrying it a fifth time, that three times is a favour and four times is a rate, and that a rate is a thing that is on you and a debt is a thing that is on somebody else, and that she will carry a rate for the rest of her life and not this market's arithmetic, and asks for one thing: print the sheet and put the difference on it, because a sheet that says what it cost and what it has not got is worth more than a sheet that says four coppers. The market prints it, from the only press in the district that can do a run in a day, belonging to a man who is right about four things and wrong about one, and he asked for it entered that he was paid and was paid, and nobody thanked him in a street and he said he would not have thanked them either. And a woman of sixty with a stick asks after both votes that every one of the four rows on the market's sheet was made by somebody walking onto a piece of ground and counting what was standing on it, and not one of them was made by asking anybody, and nobody argues with her. **The finding of the twenty-seventh, in the secretary's hand, is the fourth time this district has had it in a different shape and the first time it has cost an office a seat: the district could not agree about an office and agreed exactly and inversely about a sheet, five to five both times, the same five on opposite sides, and the difference between the two questions is not money and is not principle, it is that one of them can be ended and the other one cannot.**

## Chapter 0089 - Asked a Second Time
The twenty-ninth of May, opened on a woman of about thirty-one moving a pail out of the way of a table before she puts her coat down, which nobody mentions, because in April the pail was by the door and in the way of a chair and the person who asked the question then did not move it because it was not his. **The asking was done by a clerk of the ward market and entered by a bridge clerk of thirty-four in the water office's own book, and neither of them is Adrian, and the entry in that book says the person who wrote it down in the room was a clerk and she asked for a reader and was given a paperer.** In the room: a keeper of fifty-one, the woman of the column, a man of thirty-nine in the hut entered by himself as present and not asked for the second time in two months, a paperer of about thirty-eight who came up the bank for her own trade and is here because she was asked to be, and about twenty-two people from four houses at the back who were not asked to come in and were not asked to leave. **Nobody from a bridge administration is in that room and nobody from the ward market except the clerk, and Adrian Vale is nine miles east and entered himself in advance as not coming.** The asking took thirty-nine minutes and nine of them were the fifth thing, and the clerk got a word shorter at it than the signalman did in April, **and the finding is that a method gets cheaper the second time anybody uses it, and this is the first thing in this district that has ever got better by being done twice.** The keeper stops her in the first thing, because she has said *railway* twice and *bank* once, and is told that she read what the signalman wrote down before she came up, that it is his bank and not hers, that she has never walked it, that she walked it on the twenty-first as a rehearsal, and that she is not going to be able to say what is at the fourth changeover except that a man told her and he was standing in the water when he said it — and the keeper says that is the right answer and wants it in the book, because in April the answer was that he had been up a bank four times in three weeks to learn the difference between a word and a place. The second thing, the hour, took nine minutes and the clerk says before she asks it that she crossed a time out three times on the twentieth because she did not know how long the job was, and gives **a day and a night, leaving at half past four in the morning and back at about ten at night, and not doing that twice in a year for anybody** — and the woman of the column makes her own arithmetic public: she wrote the walking hours down wrong on the twenty-first, it is in the book, and the number on the sheet she is carrying is her own figure and not the tenth hand's and neither of them is going to argue about it in front of you. The third thing is answered before it is finished, in the same words as April, and the woman of the column says the keeper has now said *nobody asks afterwards* twice and that the second time it was not a rehearsed thing, it was a habit, **and a habit is the only instrument I have ever trusted.** The fourth thing is answered by the man of thirty-nine before it is asked, in front of about twenty-two people, and asks for both halves of it to go in together: I will answer for her about a pair and I will not answer for her about the word, and he said that in April and nothing has changed except that he has been there thirty-four days instead of four, **and a man who has been somewhere thirty-four days instead of four is a different man.** The fifth thing is answered before it is asked, in the same eleven words as April, and about nine people at the back do not look at the sheet at all. **Both say yes, to a month, and the second asking is better than the first in three ways and all three are in the book.** First, the person asking: a rotation is only real when it turns, it turned in March on a key and in April on a boundary and on the twenty-ninth of this month it turned on a bank, **and it was turned by a clerk, and a clerk is a person, and a person can be replaced, and the whole reason this district put a counter between itself and the public in the first place is that a counter can be replaced and a man cannot.** Second, **a woman on that bank who said no in March was asked again, in the same room, in front of the same twenty-two people, and said no again, and is not in the book and was not named, and a no that is asked once is a no a person can come back from in a bad afternoon, and a no that is asked twice is a standing answer, and a standing answer is not a refusal, it is a column.** Third, **the renewal carries a renewed refusal, dated: the word is not held at the fourth place**, said by the keeper before anything else and unasked, because a refusal with a date on it is a thing somebody can come back to when the ground at that end stops being ground; and she will hold a third month, and the second month is the one she will not hold alone, and there is a man in her hut who cannot say her sentence like a person yet and gets it right about one time in five, and on the fifth of this month he said it the way she says it and she has told nobody until now. The woman of the column says yes and does not like it, in that order, and not a year, because she is fifty and has not promised anybody a year since she was thirty-four — **and then asks a market for the first time in eleven years for the thing she has been waiting eleven years for somebody to ask her about water that is not about how much of it there is: the next time this market sends anybody up this bank it sends them with the sheet, not with a bag, with somebody who can read it out loud in this room, and the reading-back, and the reason, which is that a stranger is owed the reading.** She is not asking for the money back, because two hundred coppers is six coppers of lettering and a bound copy of a book and she has had eleven years of not having anything. **The whole of the morning is read back in the room before anybody stands up by the paperer, who has never read anything out loud in her life, in four minutes, and she reads the request twice, and the finding is that a paperer read a woman's request for a market to read its own sheet aloud back to a room, and that the fourth instrument this district does not have is a column that is a place, and this is the first time in nine weeks that a person who handles other people's documents for a living has read a public entry out loud.** And the ninth place holds a line for a month that somebody will have to come up this bank and ask about on the twenty-ninth of next month, and the clerk who has just done it said in nine words at the end that she is not going to be the one who does it again, and a clerk can be replaced, and that is the point of her.

## Chapter 0090 - The First to the Third of Next Month
The first, second and third of June, opened on five hundred sheets on a trestle table in a yard still tied, and the first thing anybody does is untie it and turn a sheet over and look at the back, and the back is where the count is. **The month turned between yesterday and the day before, and the new month is June, and last month was May, and this is the first document in this district's history that has been printed in one month and read in the next.** A hundred people in the yard by eleven and eleven sheets went off the table in an hour to be put on walls four, nine and eleven miles away. The sheet carries the name at the top in letters three inches high and the district has printed it five hundred times on purpose, with the market's own words under it: the toll on this line is the origin, the origin is a place and not a person, and nobody in this basin can find that office in any register this basin can reach. **The days are days of the month and not days of the week, and the reason is printed under the column: this district does not have a calendar, a day of the week is a thing nobody here can check against anybody else, a sheet with a weekday on it is a sheet that says something that cannot be found out, **and one was printed in this city in April by a man who is not in this market, who put the day on it on purpose and was not wrong about it being worth more than the blank** — the counterfeit's Monday of Chapter 74, which the repair corrected the panel to attribute to its own author rather than to this market and to an accident.** The toll column is four feet wide, ruled at its two ends, empty, with the reason printed inside it for the fourth time. At the foot, below a rule, the count each trade carried, eleven rows and four thousand and one, and about nine hundred households in no row, and **the eight hundred and ninety-four the market does not have printed in the same type as everything else with a stall's name under it**, because the woman who carries it was asked in a room in May and refused in those words. **The fifth instrument is then tested on a trestle table and it passes, and the finding is that a sheet with an empty column in the middle of it is worth more than a sheet with a price in it for about four days, and the fourth day is the day the keeper starts, which is nine words and is the first time in this district that a person has been paid to leave a space alone** — and there are three such people and none of them is paid out of a rate for it: a clerk at the east end, a paperer once a month at the fourth place, and a man of thirty-nine in a hut at the ninth place who is not paid by anything and says so every time he is asked. A printer's argument about a column is answered honestly and not refuted: a stranger takes the sheet cold on the ninth and the empty column is the most honest thing on the page, and on the thirteenth it is a thing somebody could have written in and did not, and on the seventeenth it is a thing somebody is hiding, and he cannot tell which and neither can we, and that is four days, and a price in that column is a lie, and a lie in a public book that has been believed for four days has been believed for four days. **The oil is then asked about for the first time in nine years**, by a slip in a satchel on a cart belonging to a firm that gave up a lease in March, and the trader on the lower terrace answers in four lines: she has been drawing it against a card that names a road house that is not there any more, she did not know that for three weeks, she has been paying out of her own till since the first of May and is not complaining and would like it said that nobody asked her either, and the card wants a firm's name and she is not a firm and she is fifty-one. **The card is rewritten in a person's name on the third of June, and a man of sixty-eight who puts his hand on the pipe at six every evening is asked and says no for nine minutes and then says yes, and the no is the best thing anybody has said about that engine in nine years: you are asking me to put my name on a card at a chandler's, and a man who is named can be sent for, and in nine years nobody has ever sent for Gilman Trew, and you are asking me to become findable, and findable is what a person is when somebody wants something from him, and I have wanted nothing from anybody in nine years except oil — and he said yes at about the second hour on the grounds that he has four weeks of oil and a trader who has been paying out of her own till, and one of those two things has to stop and it is not going to be the oil.** The notice board under the two lapses has a fourth line nobody can account for, in chalk, nine words long, and it will be there in a year, and **both lapses are in March — the seventh to the eleventh and the twenty-first and the twenty-second — and the key turned twice in the first half of March.** The two books that carry the nine years of the west end are both read on the same morning, a chandler's second counter on the lower terrace and a room with a door on it, and **between them they carry twenty-one thousand two hundred and twenty-two coppers, and the water is not in that figure and no instrument in this district can put it there.** Three of the four standing failures are on one page and the fourth is not, because it has been given a date in public on a board in a street by somebody who was not asked to enter it, and **a standing failure with a date on it in a public place is not less of a failure and is a different kind of known.** Then a man and a woman stand at opposite ends of a trestle table and agree on one thing in about eleven seconds and say no word about anything else: **she reads the sheet before it is allowed off the table because it makes a public claim, and strikes one sentence out of it, *the service is assured*, on the ground that a timetable is a list of days on which a thing has not happened yet and a sheet that says a service is assured has been wrong before it has been printed; he says he has not written that; and she enters that he said so, that he then read the line again for about four seconds and said nothing, and that she is the registrar and this is her instrument and she is striking it out of the printing and not out of his book, and he says then strike it out of the printing.** The reader at the counter writes down of her own accord that a slate went past in a kitchen under the Ward Nine tram line on the second of May and again on the third of June, that five people told her about the first and one about the second, that she is still not going to ask, and that not asked is in the count. **The last two things in the month's book are a number and a morning: the train arrived on the eleventh of February and on the third of this month it had stood a hundred and twelve days, and beside the figure there are now four dates on one line and nobody has put them in a column** — the twenty-ninth of April, asked; the twenty-fifth of May, when a hut in an office's name came to the end of a month an office renewed by itself; the twenty-ninth of May, when a clerk went up a bank; and the twenty-ninth of next month, when nobody has been given the job and the clerk has said it will not be her. And at six in the morning on the third, in a hut nine miles west, a man who has been there thirty-nine days on a term that ended on the twenty-fifth of May says the nine-word sentence to himself in an empty hut and gets it right the first time and writes the date and the word *held* and under it that he does not know whether it is the correct word. **And the fourth bearer is still not named, and the iron is still on a bench four hours up the bank with a boy of nineteen beside it who is not anybody's keeper, and the sheet has a day on it and an empty column in the middle of it and a name at the top of it that nobody in the basin can find, and the day it names is a day on which nothing is going to happen yet.**

## Rolling handoff, after Chapter 0090 — Volume 02 Batch 0004 complete

**Other people built the instrument and the district wrote the name at the top of a board.** A toll board is nine feet by three with a four-foot column ruled at its two ends and empty between them, three of them went up at three places on the line with a keeper at each, **and the first thing written on the first one was a name nobody in this basin can find in a register, and four people in four rooms spent two hours failing to find it, and the finding is that a toll nobody can name is still a toll and a board that says so in a street is worth more than a book that says it in a room.** The keeper's rule is nine words: **a security is a thing somebody else can go and look at**, and the rule that came out of asking a signalman is that **keeping a board is not holding a pair and nobody can be sent to a board.** The four-bearer charter is drafted by five other people in a room above a chandler's shop, **and the man who drafted it in March said in four seconds that he cannot close it, before the room got to it, and asked for the fact that he said it first to be entered, and the fourth bearer is a person who ends the use and is to be named by the three holders and about whom he has no part.** The **twenty-ninth of May** was asked by a clerk and entered by a bridge clerk in a book that is copied twice a quarter, and it was better than April in three ways: the rotation turned, a woman who said no in March was asked again and said no again, **and a no that is asked twice is not a refusal that expires, it is a column**, and the renewal carries a renewed refusal with a date on it. The **twenty-fifth of May** produced the batch's governing sentence — **an office that lets a term lapse has not ended anything, it has stopped saying when** — and the only lever the district has is that it can stop paying for a hut, which leaves **two numbers in a public book about one man and not a nil, because nobody in this district has ever costed a signalman and it has costed a pump.** The standing order was voted again in a market **with the count each trade carried read out for the first time, paying the debt the twenty-fifth of March entered**, and **the office lost five to five on the market's rule by trade and not by household, and the timetable carried six to four, and the finding is that the district could not agree about an office and agreed exactly and inversely about a sheet, and the difference between the two questions is that one of them can be ended and the other one cannot.** The **second impression of the broken circle** is on a water office notice behind a book in a hut nine miles west, found by a boy of seventeen who was there to copy a line book, **and it is entered in two lines that are not joined, and the reason is the reason the registrar gives every time, and he asked a second time in a yard and was refused in four seconds, and what it changes is one thing: this district now knows where the first impression is not.** Adrian **did not read**, the non-event is entered with no cost and no time, and the batch's finding is that the cost of a reading is a countable number of the specific thing it takes and he has two left. A **timetable is printed** with days of the month and not days of the week, the toll column empty at full width with the reason inside it, the count at the foot, the difference the market does not have in the same type, and about nine hundred households in no row. **Halloway Reed was paid, not thanked, and his fourth-day argument was not beaten and is now the reason three people are paid nothing to leave a space empty. He is named in this chapter, as the man whose press the sheet came off, and named again in Chapter 90 asking the engine-keeper whether he said yes.** The oil order that named a road house was asked about for the first time in nine years and is now in a person's name, and a man of sixty-eight said no for nine minutes and then said yes. **Selik Marne said from a rail that an office that has to ask is an office that can be refused, and the tenth hand answered him in public with the best argument anybody has made against him in nine years, and Selik read the entry and said that was correct, and he is not on the twenty-ninth of June's walk and must not be put on it.**

## Chapter 0091 - The Hole
The fourth, fifth and sixth of June, opened on a man with a shovel beside a hole he has been told twice not to fill. He is Tarin Kest, forty-three, who dug it at half past seven with a barrow he did not pay for and says he is not going to be asked about the barrow. **A hole dug to put a post in is not a post, and a post is a mark, and a mark may not carry a job, and this district has one instrument for a boundary on the ground and it is the one instrument it is not allowed to use.** The rule has not been argued about in nine weeks: a mark is the standing record of a person's refusal of a name, and it may not be used as a bearer, a holder, a witness, a responsible party, or a person who is asked. The west end is held by Sef Anyard, fifty-one, keeper of the only pair, and the woman at the column, about fifty, who has been the water there eleven years, and both have said yes three times to a nine-word sentence that stops about nine people a year. Sef Anyard says she is not going to say the thing she has got to, she is going to ask somebody else and then agree with them. **A line of stones is not a mark, and that is not a new rule and nobody is to make one: a mark is cut and that is laid, and there is no rule about stones and there is nobody who is to lay one**, and the tenth hand agrees that it is not extended and is the standing test case. The answer comes in about eleven seconds from a doorway, from a person who does not read figures and has never been in a room in Alder Reach: **two people, a term, and an entry, and a hole. That is all of it. Leave the hole.** The ground is held by nobody, which is the only reason any of them can mark anything on it: the siding, the column, the hut and the ground between them were demised in the ninth year to a firm, the shed of sixteen feet by thirty the lease describes is not there, the lease was surrendered on the thirtieth of March on a sheet headed surrender assigned to nobody, and the rate sheet has carried the ground since the twenty-eighth of March as HELD BY NOBODY. Kest: **I signed the paper that made this a place a man could put a post in, and the paper is gone, and a mark on ground somebody owns is a claim, and I have been claiming this ground for nine years by paying for water out of a meter standing on it, and I am the only man on this bank who can tell you what a claim costs.** The party walks the rest of the boundary west on the fourth: two hundred yards of footpath, then a drainage cut four feet deep down to the flood line, and beyond it nine hundred feet of embankment with a nine-foot drop. Sef Anyard says the cut is the end of it because in the fourth year a man put a train over the cut and it went in, and four people ask and one person answers. Asked how she knew, she says she did not know, that she was about eleven feet above the water at the time, and that **a person remembers the shape of a hole and not the reason for it, and the reason was in a book in Alder Reach since the fourth year and nobody had ever come up this bank and asked her for it.** On the fifth the district tries four things in about two hours and cannot use one: the stone, the post, the gate, and the number, and the number goes furthest, because **a number is not a mark**. A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing ends it: **a number is not a boundary, it is a measurement of one, and a measurement can be taken by anybody at any time and a boundary can only be held by somebody who is asked — you have got a hole, a number and a distance, and you have not got anybody.** The answer on the sixth is four lines long: two named people, a stated term, a published entry, and a hole, and the entry prints the reason on its face and says nobody in that room enlarged it. A man of thirty-nine has been in that hut forty-two days on a term of thirty and is made to say the nine-word word; **he gets four of the nine right, she supplies the fifth and stops, he says the rest, and a man who has been somewhere forty-two days instead of four is a different man and the difference is in the fifth word.** The two women say yes to a fourth month in about four minutes, standing up, in a doorway, in the rain, to a question that was not asked, and the clerk refuses to have a corrected walk figure entered. **Nobody up that bank asked about the train: eight people went five and three-quarters hours to mark a boundary and came back without once looking at nine vehicles, and the finding is that a district will walk five and a quarter hours to look at a hole in the ground and will not turn its head at nine vehicles, and that this is not a fact about the train.** The train had stood a hundred and fifteen days and there are now five dates on one line with no names against them. The entry closes: **a hole in the ground is the only thing this district has been able to put on a boundary in six weeks, and it is not a mark and it holds nothing and it is not going to hold anything, and a person can stand next to it, and in about four years there will be a stranger standing next to it who will not know what it is for, and that is the correct condition for it to be in.**

## Chapter 0092 - Nine Vehicles
The seventh to the tenth of June, opened on a man of sixty-eight with a lamp walking the length of nine vehicles on a siding counting buffers, for about forty minutes, and nobody had asked him to. He is Gilman Trew, he keeps an oil engine in a brick house eleven feet above the flood at the Harrow end, and he is not a signal hand. **A man who has been keeping a pump for nine years knows that nine vehicles standing in one place for a hundred and sixteen days is nine vehicles leaning**, and he says the count, and then says the fifth vehicle is about half an inch nearer the column than in the fourth year, and that it is not a fault and not a stall and not a mystery, and that he would like whoever is writing the book to write down that a man of sixty-eight said it was not a mystery before anybody asked him whether it was. Nobody asked him. On the eighth four people go and look at the three reasons a train on that siding cannot go east, **and the finding is that the three reasons are not one reason and the district has spent nine weeks treating them as one.** The loop: a train that comes in over the points and goes round the loop comes out facing the way it went in, and a train that has to travel east has to come out of that loop facing east. The frame: **the ninth place is the only frame on the whole line with a bracket cut in it that the iron fits, and the bracket is empty, and it has been empty since the sixth of April.** The toll: putting the iron in the bracket and throwing the points is a reversal, the use is one vehicle back along the section it came from, one movement, one way, the use is not given back, and the toll on that iron is the origin, and the origin is a place, and a place is charged. The woman who keeps the book of departures gives the number in about four seconds: **one hundred and forty-one since the flood year, out of nine hundred and six movements, and she can tell you what a hundred and forty-one is and cannot tell you what one of them is, because she has been in that hut eleven years and has never once been able to say when the last thing left here.** The page that carries the number is four lines and the fourth is that every one of the hundred and forty-one was done by somebody in this district, on a railway, without asking a place. **The hut at the fourth place has no keeper and has not had one in nine years, and that is the only reason the iron is under a coat on a bench with a book on top of it.** Four hours back down the bank, the woman who keeps the only pair on that line is fifty-one, and she said the thing about the bracket before anybody asked her, in nine words, from her own doorway: **the bracket was cut in the ninth year, and I have had it empty for five years, and I have never once asked anybody to fill it, and I want that written down before anybody decides I have been sitting on it.** The boy of nineteen: **I will not carry it again. I will hold it while somebody else ties a rope on it and I will not be the one who goes in the hut, and if anybody tries to make me be the one who goes in the hut they are going to have to do it in front of a keeper and a board.** On the ninth a paperer of about thirty-eight with a hook and a sacking sheet asks the woman at the column a question about a piece of paper, which is her whole trade: **has anybody ever put a platform at this place.** The answer is no, and the rest of the day is spent on what a no means. The keeper of the only pair: **it is a siding and a column and a hut and four houses and thirty-one people and a man of thirty-nine who is not paid, and a woman of about fifty who is the water, and a line of chalk sixty yards west of my door that stops about nine people a year. A platform is a thing you get off a train at.** And: **nobody has, and that is the whole of the answer and it took nine years to find out and it took a woman with a hook to ask it, and I have been asked four times in eleven weeks whether I would hold something and nobody has ever once asked me what this place is for.** On the tenth the whole of what a train needs is worked out in about an hour: a platform of nine feet of face and thirty feet long, a lamp, a board so a person in the dark can find out what the place is, and a place in a line book that says a train stops here, kept by hand at the east end by a clerk who is not the market. **The fourth thing is the only one that cannot be bought with salvage, and nobody said so for about nine minutes, and the reason nobody said so is that the fourth thing is a question and a market has never bought a question in its life.** The money came out of the standing salvage, and **the finding of the tenth is that a security which is spent on the thing it secures is not a security any more, and a spent security is a thing somebody else has looked at, and the district has one fewer security than it had in May and one more platform, and the trade was struck at the right price by a man who did not want the trade.** Kest, in eleven words in the minute: it is salvage, nine years of it, and a man who walks up that bank in August is going to find a platform and not a security, and the only thing I ever had to stand behind was a pile of timber and I have just spent it on a place to stand on. The thing that made the rest possible is found on the tenth at about half past ten in a public book by a woman of about thirty-one who was not looking for it: **the entry of the tenth of May, in which five people who were not there to listen to Adrian Vale struck two of the four places out of his own draft, invented an order of weeks, invented a fourth bearer whose only standing is the ending, and named the origin — the ninth place — as a decision and not a reading, with the woman's answer in the order she gave it: I do not like it, and I say yes.** The clerk reads it out at the counter: **a decision and a reading are two different things, and only one of them is on that sheet, and what it means is that for the first time in nine years the toll of a movement on this line can be charged to a place that was asked, and not one of the hundred and forty-one was.** The tenth hand says then the iron can go back in the bracket, and the registrar says: **the iron could always go back in the bracket. What could not happen is what happens after it does.** The same afternoon a stranger takes a printed timetable cold, and **the finding is that the first timetable this district ever printed is a list of days on which nothing was going to happen, and the market knew there was a train on a siding and did not know the train would move, and the sheet does not say so anywhere, and about four hundred people have taken one cold in a fortnight.** A woman of about sixty with a stick: you have printed a service. The secretary: we have printed a sheet. She: **and a stranger has taken it cold and read a day on it, and a day on a sheet is a promise, and I have been in four rooms in nine weeks finding the holes in this district's constitution and I have not found this one because you have printed it very well.** The registrar struck one sentence out of a printing on the second and did not strike it out of a man's book, and what she could not strike on the tenth was nineteen days of the month: **a document can have a sentence taken out of it in four seconds and a calendar cannot, and this district has learned to say what it cannot do and has not yet learned to print what it cannot do.** On the fourth day of the ninth-place board a stranger writes **4** in chalk in the empty four-foot column, in front of about twenty people, and the keeper of the board — a man of thirty-nine, a signalman of a river administration, not a bearer, not paid — wipes it off in about four seconds and says nine words which the man who wrote the figure wrote down himself before he went: **it is not what it costs to ride. It is what it costs to arrive.** Then he writes the eighteenth sentence on that board's bottom line: **THE COLUMN IS EMPTY BECAUSE NOBODY CAN STATE IT, NOT BECAUSE NOBODY HAS.** The man of about thirty who sells nothing is told, and his finding is that he has been right about four things for four months and has now been shown which one he was wrong about, **that he was wrong about a price and right about a person, and that he printed both in his own type on the same sheet in April and did not notice that one of them was about a different day from the other.** The train had stood a hundred and nineteen days on the tenth.

## Chapter 0093 - Twice Is Not a Rotation
The eleventh, twelfth and thirteenth of June, opened on the reader at the ward market counter reading out a date and then reading out that there was nothing under it, to about thirty people, and she is paid eleven coppers a quarter for four days a week and the term has no clause for a shoulder. **The lot offered an hour on the eleventh, the eleventh was the first day the limit permitted one since the twenty-seventh of April, he was in the yard of Lot 17 at about ten in the morning, he was asked by a man who does not sell anything, and he said no in one word, and everybody in that yard had heard him say a longer version of it four times in nine weeks.** Nessa Pell, a clerk of nineteen years and the sitting steward of the public lot: **entered. The question is entered. The hour is entered. The non-event is entered with no cost and no time. And nobody in this book is going to write it as cowardice and nobody is going to write it as discipline. And if either word is written against it I will take it out, and I have never taken a line out of anything in nineteen years and I am not starting with this one.** The finding of the eleventh is that the reason he gave is a number. **The cost of a reading is not a figure and is not a duration. It is a countable number of the specific thing it takes, and the specific thing this iron takes is memory of departure, and he has paid it twice.** In March it took his sense of which way a river went, for about fifty minutes, on a bank nine miles from the book. In April it took the memory of which gate he went out of his own city by, and there is no west gate, and the departure it took is dated on the seventeenth of February of this year at six in the morning, to look at a pumping engine, and twenty-three days after the reading it had not come back. He says **two left** out loud in a yard to about sixty people, and asks for it entered that he has said that sentence four times, that it has got shorter every time, and that today it was a number instead of a sentence and that is new and he has noticed it. The intervals have gone up on use — seven days, then eleven, then fourteen — and Stenn entered in April that this is not a rule getting stricter and that he would not defend it. **A number is not a plan. He has turned a principle into a figure and that is progress, and a figure is not a plan, because a plan says what the hour is for and he has not got one, and I have been waiting nine weeks for a man with a reading to tell this district what an hour is for, and he has not done it, and I do not think he is going to.** The next permitted hour is not before the twenty-fifth of this month, and there is a fortnight in which somebody in this district has to do something that is not reading. **The other thing that happened on the eleventh is the reason there is going to be a third asking on the twenty-ninth, and it is that the woman at the column asked a market for something in May and the market has entered that it will be done and has not decided when — and the thing it was waiting for was a walk.** Her request is in the book in her own words: the next time this market sends anybody up this bank, it sends them up with the sheet, not with a bag, with somebody who can read it out loud in this room, and the reading-back, and the reason, which is that a stranger is owed the reading. The first half is the sheet. The second half is that reading a sheet aloud in a room nine miles and five and a quarter hours away takes a whole day, and the reader at the counter cannot do it, and nobody had asked her. The room finds the person on the twelfth in about eleven minutes, **and the person is a woman of about thirty-eight with a hook and a sacking sheet who buys old paper off four houses once a month, and nobody in that room had thought of her until somebody said her trade out loud.** The clerk of the ward market, who has said in a room in May that it would not be her again: **whose trade is it to read a sheet to a room. Not mine. Mine is figures. Not hers, and hers is figures in other people's writing, which is not the same thing, and there is exactly one person in this district who gets paid for reading other people's documents and it is not a market and it is not a guild and it is not in the count of trades in our room.** She is Jessa Olwin, about thirty-eight, and she has been doing it for nine years, and she said yes before she was asked, the sixth time in nine weeks, and gave two things with it. **One: I go up on the day I go up, and the day I go up in this month is the twenty-ninth, and that is not a coincidence and it is not a favour, because the four houses and I have been doing this on the twenty-ninth since the flood year and it is the day the second house clears its back room. Two: you are not buying a day. I am going up there on the twenty-ninth whatever this is, and I am going to be four houses deep, and the fifth thing about where you stand is not going to happen at a house, and it is going to happen at about eleven o'clock at night at the end of the second house, and if the two of you are standing there then it happens, and if you are not then you have come four hours for nothing and I am not sorry for you.** The market paid her eleven coppers and the entry says **that the eleven coppers are for reading a sheet aloud and for the reading-back, and are not for the day, and that the day was hers.** They came out of the unassigned thirty, and this is the first thing in eleven weeks the unassigned thirty has been asked to do. On the twelfth the salvage went up the bank in eleven loads on a cart a firm that gave up a lease in March no longer owns, **and the finding is that four hundred and ten coppers' worth of old sleepers has been sitting in a yard in Alder Reach for nine years in the rain because nobody could work out what a security was for.** A mechanic of forty-three who founded the Needle Road House loaded every one of them himself. The third man of the party was a boy of nineteen who asked to come and was not asked, said on the bank that he was nobody's keeper, and **the entry records that he was on the bank for no reason that can be entered, and that a person present for no reason is present, and that this district has entered that sentence four times in eleven weeks and has never once been able to say what it is for.** The sheet for the twenty-ninth went into the public lot book on the thirteenth at nine in the morning, and it is the first sheet this district has published that begins with a person who is not an officer, a clerk or a reader.
