It names the paperer and the eleven coppers and says the eleven is not for the day; it carries her own hours, **six and a quarter going and five and a half coming and eleven and three-quarters in the day, which is ten and three-quarters of walking and an hour of standing still, and that the figure is hers and not the tenth hand's and neither of them is going to argue about it in front of two people**; **it says the rate sheet goes up first and is read aloud before any question is asked, because a stranger is owed the reading, and the reading-back is done by the tenth hand, who is nobody's employee and cannot be paid and has said he will do it; and it says Mr Vale is nine miles east and is not asked and has said so in advance, and the woman of forty-five is not asked and is in the count as not asked and is not named, and nobody from a river administration is in that room.** The registrar entered the delay for it herself, the sixth time, on a new clause: **a sheet about a place nine miles away is a sheet about a place a stranger cannot see, and that is the second time this district has published a delay and the first time it has published a delay for a document that was not late.** And the finding of the thirteenth is the one this batch turns on, four sentences long, said by a man who cannot be paid. **A rotation turned in April on a key and turned in May on a boundary. Both of them cost somebody a day. The first cost a man a day he was not paid for. The second cost a woman a day and a night and eleven and three-quarters hours, and she told this room the figure herself, and it is her own figure and it is not mine. That is a subsidy and not a rotation. A subsidy is a thing you stop. A rotation is a thing you do not have to pay for, and the difference between those two things is the whole of what a boundary is and the whole of what an office is. The third one is the first one nobody has had to be given a day for. And I want that written down, and I want it written down by a person who is not me, because I have been the man in the room for nine weeks and I have noticed that the things I say get entered and the things other people say get read out, and both of those are wrong and only one of them is my fault.** A boy of seventeen: **twice is not a rotation. Twice is a market deciding what a day costs. I have been paid four coppers a quarter for an index since the fourteenth of last month and I have never once been asked whether I wanted the index, and nobody in this district asks anybody anything before they pay them, and that is not a small thing and it is the same shape as the walk and everybody here can see the shape.** Nobody answered him and the minute enters that nobody answered him and that he was not thanked and that he had said the same class of thing four times in eleven weeks and been wrong about the meaning three of them. And the sixth thing, which has no name and is not on the wall, is a person in the room who is not the one asking and is not the one being asked, whose job is to say what the question was; it belongs to a clerk of nineteen years, and on the twenty-ninth it is going to be done by a paperer in a front room nine miles from Alder Reach. **Under the heading on a wall that says NOT ANSWERED, the third line anybody has entered in nineteen years went in on the thirteenth: THE PERSON IS A PAPERER AND SHE IS NOT ASKED YET.** And the last line on the sheet is the secretary's: **the sheet goes up with the sheet. The reading happens before the question, because a stranger is owed the reading. And the fifth thing about where you stand has not been moved to suit anybody, and the room is a front room with a board over one pane of the window, and if the two of you cannot stand in it then this is not the asking.**

## Chapter 0094 - The Fourth Bearer Is Not a Slot
The fourteenth to the eighteenth of June, opened on three people in a signal cabin at the east end at about ten past eight in the morning with the sheet of the tenth of May on the table, five weeks after it went into the public lot book, and the fourth line of it the only one that had never been filled in. The fourth line says the fourth bearer is a person who ends the use, is not a slot, is to be named by the three people who hold the weeks, and that Mr Vale has no part in naming them. The three are a woman of fifty-one who is the keeper of the only pair on that line, a woman of about fifty-one who keeps a board in this cabin and is not a signal hand, and a man of sixty-eight at a pumping engine eleven miles west who put his name on a card at a chandler's on the third of this month after saying no for nine minutes. Two hours and forty minutes, and no fourth line, **and the reason no fourth line is that the first thing they did was find a mistake in their own document that had been in it since the tenth of May, and none of the three had found it because none of the three had ever asked whether a board keeper can hold a pair.** **A keeper is somebody who is there. I said in May that I am not a signal hand and I never will be and I do not know what a pair is, and that sentence is on a board at the fourth place in my own words. I put my name in the fourth line of a charter in May and nobody in that room asked me whether I could hold a pair, and the standing of the iron is a person who can hold a signal.** And the woman of fifty-one: **write down that we found it ourselves and that we found it in two hours and forty minutes and that it had been in the public book for five weeks and that four hundred people could have taken that sheet cold and read it.** The finding of the fourteenth is that **a document can be wrong in a way that everybody in the room agreed with, and that agreement is the reason it survives, and that this district has now had this twice.** The first time was the count rule, which turned a village of thirty-one into four households in the week it was written. The second is a board keeper in the fourth line of a rotating charter, and the mistake survived five weeks and two votes and a print, **and the reason nobody said it is that a person in a room who has said yes to something is very hard to look at properly, and this district has learned that four times in eleven weeks in four different rooms and has not learned it in any of them.** The line was struck at about ten past ten and the strike is in the minute with her name on it, because she wanted it on: **I am striking myself in front of three people and I am going to be the one who says why, because if I wait for somebody else to say it then in about four years the story is that a man at a pumping engine found a woman's mistake, and he did not, and I did, and I said yes to it in a room in May because I had a queue at a counter and I wanted it to be finished.** They were three and then two, because a board keeper is not a bearer and the east end of that line is a cabin with a lever frame in it and a man in the district's own signal crew who has a pair in his hand on a Tuesday, or would have, if anybody had ever asked him. The finding on the fifteenth is four lines long: **the east end is eleven minutes' walk from the ward market counter, and nobody had ever asked who holds the frame at the east end because a board went up in front of the cabin in May and a board is a thing people look at, and the frame is behind the door, and the district built an instrument for looking at a thing and forgot that there is a thing behind it.** He is a man of about thirty-one, a signalman of the district's own crew, and he walked up the bank four times between the fourteenth and the seventeenth and was not paid. **Say the whole thing again, and then say it to the two of them, and then let me say no, because I am going to say no, and I have thought about it for two days, and I would like it written down that I said no before you read it to me rather than after.** He said no for about eleven seconds, and the reason is that a week at the east end ends on the twenty-ninth and he has said yes to a boundary on the twenty-ninth twice, and if the third asking is on the twenty-ninth then a week at the east end is a week that ends on the same day he is two and a half miles up a bank reading somebody else's asking back. **Ask me for any other week.** And the entry on the seventeenth says the three holders took the week he named, and that **a rotation is only real when it turns for a reason, and that the first reason anybody in this district has ever given for not holding a week is that the week was the wrong shape.** The fourth bearer was found on the fifteenth, and not by anybody who was looking for one: **the finding attached to it is that the district has spent nine weeks unable to name a person and has had a person eleven steps away from it for the whole of that time.** It is the twelfth place, two miles west of the ninth place, on the embankment on the far side of the drainage cut, a ground frame in a hut about eight feet by nine with a lamp and a stool and a lever, and **the frame has no bracket and never had one and the iron does not fit it.** There is nothing at the twelfth place and about forty people in nine weeks have said that a place with nothing at it is not a place anybody can be sent to. **There is a man there. He is there twice a month because a frame that is not used has to be turned by hand or it binds, and he has been going for nineteen years, and he has never been asked anything by anybody, and he is not on a roll, and he is not in the count of trades, and he is not in anybody's rate sheet, and he is not on any board.** The paperer found him, because her fourth of four houses is at a gate two hundred yards from the frame hut. **She did not ask him whether he would end a railway. She asked him what the lever was for**, and gave nine words about it afterwards: I asked him what the lever was for. He said it turns the points at the eleventh and twelfth so that a thing nobody sends does not go into the flood. I asked him who sends the thing. He said nobody has sent one since the fourth year. I asked him what he does about that. He said he comes twice a month. **I did not ask him anything else and I did not tell him anything and I did not know what I had found until the sixteenth.** His name is Elias Rooke and he is sixty-one and he has held a pair on this line for thirty-four years and has kept that frame for nineteen. He is not in an office and has never carried anything for anybody, and the room recorded both those facts before asking him anything. The keeper of the only pair has the twelfth place in her own book, in her own hand, produced on the sixteenth at about half past four, nineteen years old and four words long: **FRAME TURNED. NOTHING SENT.** **And I have copied that line out about nine hundred times since, and I have never once asked what the frame turns, and I have never once asked who turns it, and I have written nothing after those four words in nineteen years, because there was nothing to write and because I did not know that there was a person.** And then: **I knew the frame was there the way I know the drainage cut is there. It is on the line. It is between two things. I have never had a reason to go to it and I have never had a reason to ask and I have been the keeper of the only pair on this line for eleven years and I have never once been curious, and I am putting that in the book in my own hand because I would like somebody in four years to know that it was me and not the water at the column.** What a bearer actually does came out of a conversation about a lever in a hut two miles off a bank. It is nine words on the back of a mechanic's invoice: **YOU STAND WHERE THE USE IS AND YOU STOP IT OUT LOUD.** Nobody was satisfied; the man of sixty-eight asked for about four minutes alone with it, which nobody has ever been granted at a meeting in this district, and came back and said it is correct and useless, and that it had taken a woman of sixty-one two miles off a bank to say it. He was asked on the sixteenth in the five things and gave the first thing in his own words, longer than anybody else's: **The job is that a use stops. Not a train, not a road, not a thing. A use. And nobody in nine years has ever come up this bank and told me that there is a use, and I have been coming up here twice a month for nineteen years to turn a lever for a thing that nobody sends. So I am going to say what I want and I am going to say it once. I will end a use. I will not end a railway, I will not close a line, and I will not stand in a place and stop people. I will put my hand on a lever and reverse whatever is running and I will say the reason out loud in a room with two other people in it, and if the reason is not good enough then somebody can put the points back, and that is their job and not mine. And I have been on my own for nineteen years, and I would like it recorded that this is the first offer anybody has made me, and that I said yes to it in about four seconds, and that I did not want it to be longer.** The sheet of the seventeenth is the fourth line of the tenth of May filled in: he is Elias Rooke, sixty-one, keeper of the frame at the twelfth place, who has held a pair thirty-four years and kept that frame nineteen and has been asked nothing by anybody; he is named by the three people who hold the weeks; **Mr Vale was not in the room and has no part in naming him and was told the name at the same time as everybody else, on the eighteenth, in a counting room, in the ordinary voice, by a clerk**; he is not a slot and not a substitute, holds no week and no place, his only standing is that he ends the use, and he ends it by reversing what is running and saying the reason out loud in a room with two other people in it, and the reason goes in a public book; he is not an officer and not in a room with a door on it, and **he lives eleven miles from the nearest frame that anybody in this district can point at, and he has been there nineteen years, and the finding is that the district could not name him because nobody had ever walked two miles**; and the man who posted the security this charter is for is a person the fourth bearer has never met. Nobody thanked the paperer and she said beforehand that she did not want to be. The finding of the eighteenth took the room an hour: **the person who ends a use is the only one of the four who is not anybody's substitute. This district has spent nine weeks building a thing in which every job could be filled in later, and the one job in it that cannot be filled in later is the one that matters. A person who cannot be replaced is not the same as a person who is permanent, and the difference is that a permanent person has to be paid and a person who cannot be replaced is paid out of nothing and stays because nobody has thought to ask them to stop.** A boy of seventeen: **he is the second person in eleven weeks who has been found by somebody doing a different job; the first was me and I was copying a book. And the finding is not that this district is short of people. It is that this district cannot send anybody anywhere without a document, and the fourth bearer had no document, and the reason he had no document is that nobody has ever written one about him, and a person with no document is a person nobody can be sent to, and that is why he is the right one.** The registrar refused to enter the last half in about four seconds: **because you have joined two things that arrived separately. A person nobody can be sent to and the right person for a job are not the same statement, and the difference between them is the whole of what anybody in this district has ever been able to say about a mark.** On the eighteenth a clerk read the name out at a counter in the ordinary voice and about forty people stood there, and the man it was about was nine miles west and did not hear it. The man of about thirty: **say what you are going to do with it.** He: I am going to write it in my own book, under the date, with the time I heard it and the fact that I was in this room, and then I am going to go and do the only part of this that is mine, which is put four pounds of cast iron in a bracket, **and I would like it read out that I did not ask for this and that I would have said no if I had been asked and that saying no is not in anybody's hands any more.** And then: **the finding is that I cannot close it and that is not why I would have said no. I would have said no because a man who writes the fourth line of a document and gets to name the person who can stop him is a man with a document and a man, and this is a document and a man, and it took a woman with a hook and a question about a lever to find the one person in eleven miles who could not be reached by either of those things.** And the registrar entered that **the phrase *if I had been asked* has been in this district's public books eleven times in nine weeks and that this is the first time it has been entered as a thing he would have done and not a thing somebody else did.** And then, on the evening of the eighteenth, a mechanic of forty-three who had not been in the room said the thing that four people wrote down, because it is the reason a security gets spent in the next two days: **The ground. The ground is the best one and you are all about to spend it. I posted it so that a man could walk up that bank and stand on it, and on the twenty-seventh of this month there is going to be a platform on it. A security is a thing somebody else can go and look at, and a platform is the easiest thing in this district to look at, and I posted mine for exactly one purpose, and the purpose is about to be used up in public, and nobody is going to be able to tell in August whether the ground was ever anybody's to look at.** So what do you want. **I want it entered now that the fourth security is going to be spent, and I want it entered before it is spent, and I want the entry to say it was spent and not lost, and I do not want it back.**

## Chapter 0095 - Thirty Feet of Platform
The nineteenth to the twenty-third of June, opened on the first thing that went onto the ninth place's platform, which was not a sleeper but a chalk line, drawn by a woman of about thirty-eight with a hook and a sacking sheet who had come up on the fifteenth for her own trade, had been asked to stay two extra days, had said yes, and had asked for the extra two days to be entered as hers. She drew it thirty feet long and nine feet back from the ballast, and she drew it twice, and the first one was wrong, and the entry says so in the keeper's hand: **I have been buying paper off four houses for nine years, and I have never once been the person who says where a thing goes, and a line is not a paper and I do not know why I am doing this, and I have been asked and I said yes, and I would like the yes and the not-knowing in the same line in whatever book this goes in.** And the thirty-one people on that bank are asked about a platform on the nineteenth, and it took two days instead of an afternoon, **and the reason is the count, and the count is the reason the whole of this volume exists.** There are four households, thirty-one people, nineteen of them not in a household and not in no row and not among the about nine hundred that are not counted anywhere including on the sheet that collects the four thousand and one. **The count rule says a person is accounted for by a count and not by a household. So the nineteen are accounted for by the number thirty-one and by nothing else, and there is no list of them and no sheet and no name, and a person who cannot be named cannot be asked, and asking somebody whether they will have a platform is a thing that has to be done to a person.** Four households were asked in about nine minutes; the nineteen took until the evening of the twentieth. The last was a man of about sixty-one, asked in a doorway at about ten at night, who said yes in about two seconds and then asked a question that four people carried back nine miles on foot: **if I say yes to a platform, is it still a place I can stand in.** Nobody in the doorway had an answer, and the four people who walked back had four different guesses and one of them was a man of sixty-eight who said it would be a good thing and did not know why. The answer came on the twenty-first from the woman of about fifty who is the water, in eleven words: **it is a place where a thing stops. That is all a platform is. Come and stand on it any day.** The entry of the twentieth is the first time in nine years that anybody has asked a village of thirty-one what they wanted done to the ground they live on, and the count is nineteen yeses, one yes, and one no, and the no is not against the platform. The no is a woman of about fifty-one who keeps the only pair, and her reason is eleven words: **I will hold a word and I will not hold a thing you can stand on. A platform is a thing a stranger stands on and a stranger is not a person I can ask. So yes, and do not write my name on the platform, and put the name of the place on it, which is what the woman at the column said in May and was right about.** The six figures are entered separately, because entering them as one number would have been a household: nineteen who are not in a household asked separately and nineteen said yes; four households asked and four said yes; one keeper said yes with a condition and the condition is that her name is not on the platform; one keeper said yes and asked for her name not to be on it; **the total is twenty-five yeses, which is not the number of people, and the entry says so.** **And the finding is that the only question that had to be asked twice was asked once, and that the only person who asked for something to be left off was asked for it by himself, and that nobody in this district has ever before asked a village what it wanted done to the ground it stands on, including the man who leased it for nine years.** The platform was built on the twentieth and twenty-first by nine men, one boy of nineteen and the paperer, took about two days, cost nothing, **and the finding is that it is the most expensive thing anybody in this district has done in eleven weeks and it cost nothing.** The eleven coppers a quarter for the bench, the lamp, the chalk and the oil is the whole of the money; nine men lost two days of work and a paperer lost a day of a trade, **and none of that is in a rate sheet, because a rate sheet cannot hold a day.** It is thirty feet long and nine feet of face, about a hundred and forty sleepers on the ballast, boards on top, a kerb of old rail on the front edge, two feet above the shoulder, and at the west end a lamp on a post cut out of a green pipe lying across a cess at the third changeover. **Nobody laid a stone. Nobody put a mark in the ground. The platform has four feet of face and thirty feet of length and no mark on it of any kind, and the finding is that a platform is not a mark and a mark may not carry a job, and a platform is not asked to do any job at all except stop being the place where a thing stops, and that is the whole of what the rule was for.** The man who posted it, in a yard, to about thirty people: **I posted four hundred and ten coppers of standing salvage on the seventeenth of May so that a man could walk up that bank and look at it. There is a platform on it now. In August a man will walk up that bank, see a platform, and write down that there was a security here, and he will be right, and he will be wrong, and both of those are going to be in the same book and I am not going to be able to explain the difference to him. So I want the entry to say it was spent and not lost, and I want it to say it was spent on the thing it was posted for, and I do not want it back, and I would like somebody to write down that I said I do not want it back before anybody offered.** The entry of the twenty-second is one line long: **THE FOURTH SECURITY WAS SPENT ON THE TWENTIETH OF JUNE AND NOT LOST. IT WAS THE GROUND AT THE NINTH PLACE, HELD BY NOBODY, POSTED ON THE SEVENTEENTH OF MAY BY THE NEEDLE ROAD HOUSE IN THE NAME OF TARIN KEST, AND IT IS NOW A PLATFORM, AND A PLATFORM IS THE EASIEST THING IN THIS DISTRICT FOR A STRANGER TO GO AND LOOK AT.** On the twenty-first a woman of forty-four who keeps a Road House on the tide lent her yard for the third time, and **it is the first rehearsal anybody has held that was not on the ground the thing was for**: a rehearsal is a walk done before the day it is for, on the same ground, by some or all of the people who will do it, for the purpose of finding out whether the walk can be done. The first was on the twenty-second of March in the rain; the second on the twenty-first of last month up a bank; the third in a yard on the tide, on cobbles, and took about an hour and a half. She is not paid, not thanked, and insisted it be entered as not a security. **And the finding of the twenty-first is that a yard cannot rehearse a loop, and that this district has now held three rehearsals and has discovered the limit of the instrument for the first time, and the limit is that a rehearsal can find out whether a walk can be done and cannot find out whether a ground will do what a person says it will do.** A foreman of twenty-two years whose name is on the four hundred and ten coppers: **in a yard the ground is flat and it is dry and there is no flood and no cut and no shoulder and no nine-foot drop and no eleven feet of bank. In a yard I can tell you four men can walk a rope out of a barrow in about a minute. In a yard I cannot tell you anything at all about what nine vehicles do on a loop.** So rehearse the walking. **I will rehearse the walking and I have rehearsed the walking and the walking is not the thing that has not been done.** On the afternoon of the twenty-second, at about four o'clock, four pounds of cast iron came up a bank for the second time in this year in a rope, and the boy of nineteen held one end and did not go in the hut; **he held it for about four hours and forty minutes and did not go in.** The keeper of that hut carried it in herself with two men on a board and set it down on the bench under the window in a bag. **She was asked that night whether she wanted it in the bracket, and said she would put it in on a night, and asked her what night, and nobody in that hut could name one, because the fourth bearer had not been named until the seventeenth and there was nothing on any sheet in this district that said which night anything was going to happen on.** The trip took six hours and a quarter in the rain, and the figure is in the boy's index beside the eleven hours the party came back in in May and the five and a quarter the tenth hand wrote down in March and the four and three-quarters four people believed for a fortnight, and none of the five has been struck, **and a district which has been wrong five times about a walk and right once is not a district that should be asked for a correction.** The train had stood a hundred and thirty-one days on the twenty-second, and the bracket in the frame was still empty, and it stayed empty for the seven days that followed it. On the evening of the twenty-first, at a rail in Alder Reach, on a published day, a bridge administrator of nine years said three things about thirty feet of platform: that it is not a station, the second was right, and the third is the sentence he says on published days. He is Selik Marne, argued with and never defeated and never converted and not wrong; he is not on any walk and has not been up that bank in nine years. **That is not a station. It is thirty feet of board and a lamp cut out of a green pipe, and there is no office, no keeper's wage, no relief, no record of who is on it, and if a train stands there in the dark at four in the morning then a person gets off it onto a shoulder with a nine-foot drop and a path four hundred yards long.** The tenth hand: that is all true. Selik: **and it is not the finding, and I have had it for a fortnight, and I have been waiting for somebody to build something so that I could say it about a real thing instead of a plan. The finding is that a platform is a place a thing stops and a person is not a thing, and this district has spent nine weeks being careful about that difference and has in two days built thirty feet of board that a person has to get off. The reason nobody stopped it is that the woman of the column said come and stand on it any day, and she was asked whether she wanted thirty feet of board and she was not asked whether a man of sixty-one could find it in the dark.** Nobody argued with him, and the minute enters that nobody argued, and that a man who has been laughed at once for a correct thing is not a man who can be hurried. The third thing: **that is not enough, and I am going to keep saying that on published days for as long as I hold this post, and nobody in this district believes I will stop and I am not going to stop, and I would like it entered that the man who posted the security on that platform has lost something that cannot be got back and that the man at the rail has not lost anything at all, and that the difference between those two facts is the whole of what I have been trying to say to this district for nine years.** The finding under the third thing is that he did not say whose security, that he had been told on the nineteenth, and that the woman of the column has said in public that a river office cannot be asked and has never once said that it is not listening. And on the twenty-second, in the hours the counter was shut, a clerk of the ward market who is not the market wrote a place into the line book, and **it is the first place this line has ever had in that column that was not a signal, and the entry is four lines long**: WRITE THE PLACE. WRITE THE DATE. WRITE THAT IT IS NOT A SIGNAL AND THAT A SIGNAL IS WHAT A PLACE IS WHEN SOMEBODY STOPS A THING. THE NINTH PLACE. THE TWENTY-SECOND OF JUNE. A PLACE A THING STOPS AT. NOT A SIGNAL. THE COLUMN FOR IT IS THE SAME COLUMN AND THE HAND IS THE SAME HAND AND THE ENTRY IS A LINE LONG AND IT IS THE FIRST LINE IN NINE HUNDRED AND SIX THAT A PLACE HAS BEEN WRITTEN IN IT ON ITS OWN. She said out loud, twice, in the empty room, what the six things on a wall say a person should say before they do a job, because the sixth thing has no name and belongs to a clerk of nineteen years and she was not going to do it on a page where nobody could hear her. The finding on the twenty-third is that a line book kept by hand for nine years has no column for a station and has had a column for a signal for nine hundred and six movements, **and that the whole difference between a railway and a line is four letters in one column and nobody in this district had ever needed to write them until a woman of thirty-one sat down in a shut counting room on the twenty-second of June and could not find anywhere else to put it.** And on the twenty-second a fourth toll board went up at the ninth place, on the platform and not on the shoulder, because the first three went where a person could stand and this one goes where a person gets off a thing. The two posts are the two ends of the platform's own kerb rail, cut and bolted, and the man of sixty-one who wrote signs for two coppers a board was not paid for this one because nobody had lettering money. So the fourth board is the only one of the four with no heading on it, and it carries the same four-foot column ruled in pencil at its two ends and empty between them, the reason printed inside the column at the height of a man's eye, the same three lines along the bottom, and one bottom line anybody may write a sentence on. The finding is that **this district has four toll boards and three of them have a name at the top in letters three inches high and the fourth one has nothing at the top, because there was no money for letters, and that the first board in this district with an empty place where a heading goes is the one a person stands in front of after a thing has stopped.** And a keeper was asked on the twenty-second, in five things, in a yard, in about four minutes: a man of about fifty who has kept a board at the ninth place since the eighth of last month, a signalman of a river administration, not a bearer, who said yes and gave one condition and it is on the board. **I will wipe the bottom line when it is wet and I will keep the date and I will not decide anything. And I am not the keeper of that platform. I have said since the eighth of last month that keeping a board is not holding a pair and I am not going to take a different sentence now that there is something to stand on.**

## Chapter 0096 - Thirty Feet of Dark
The twenty-sixth, twenty-seventh and twenty-eighth of June, opened on a sign-writer of sixty-one on his knees at the west end of a platform at about two in the morning with a rag and a can of white paint, painting four words at the height of a man's hand — **THE NINTH PLACE** — by the light of a lamp a lamp-man had brought up the bank and would not light. He is Abel Troth, who wrote the heading on the first toll board in this district on the sixth of last month in letters three inches high in nine minutes, and who has written the phrase at the top of three boards and five hundred printed sheets twice in his own hand in thirty-one years of lettering jobs and has no office and no customer and nobody who gave him the words. **I have put it at the right height for the hand and the wrong height for the eye, and the difference between those two things is this board, and there is no money in this district for letters, and the answer to a board with no letters on it is not to paint it at the top like the other three. It is to put it where a person who is frightened in the dark can find it without having been able to see in the first place.** The lamp-man's reason is nine words and is the only calendar anybody in this district has: a lamp-man does not start a lamp on a Saturday. Nobody in that hut could say what day of the week the twenty-sixth of this month was. **The finding of the twenty-sixth is that this district has a man who will not light a lamp on a day of the week and cannot tell anybody which day of the week it is, and he is right not to, and the answer took thirteen minutes and is four words long and it is on the board. THE DAY IS THE PERSON. And that is not a joke and I would like it entered that it is not a joke. A lamp that needs a day of the week is not a lamp. It is a thing a man has to be right about.** So the lamp is lit by whoever is standing on the platform, and the oil is the fourth of the eleven coppers a quarter the ward market pays, paid since the seventeenth of last month, and nobody had ever gone and lit it. **The oil itself took nine minutes and is the shape of the whole of this month**: a card at a chandler's counter on Ash Row named a road house until the third of this month and then named a man, and a trader of fifty-one on the lower terrace has been paying out of her own till since the first of last month and has asked twice that it be said nobody asked her either. **Nine gallons of lamp oil went up that bank on the twenty-sixth against a card with a person's name on it, and it is the fourth of the eleven coppers, and it is the only line in this district's accounts that has been paid correctly in the whole of the last two months.** And then the twenty-seventh was spent on the ground, for the first time: the fourth rehearsal, the first one not on ground that could not do what a person said it would do. Four hours, eleven men, a coil of rope and a barrow. **The point of it was the eleven yards that a string of people cannot walk in a straight line on a shoulder with a drop, and the finding is that the loop at this place is eleven yards shorter than the standing train, and that the ninth vehicle therefore stands across the mouth of the loop and not in it.** The man of sixty-eight found it in about half an hour with a piece of chalk and a borrowed tape: **she fits. She has fitted for nine years and nobody has ever stood at the mouth and watched the ninth one come round. And the reason nobody has ever stood at the mouth is that the mouth is a place a train is in, and this district has never had a rule about trains. The rule it has is about people, and a train is not one.** And the fourth rehearsal found the thing the third one could not: **the mouth of that loop is nine feet wide and the ninth vehicle stands across the shoulder, and the shoulder is the ground the boundary runs across sixty yards west, and a line of people and a line of iron cross on the same bank about nine yards apart and neither of them can see the other.** And **the two of them have been eleven feet apart vertically and four hours apart in walking for nine years**, and a line of stones that is not a mark has been standing on the same bank the whole time and nobody laid it this year and nobody is going to. The woman of the column, to a rope and not to a person: **I have been shut at a door by a river for eleven years and the two of you are about to shut thirty feet of platform between a person and a train, and the difference is that the river did not have a schedule.** And then a firm that gave up a nine-year lease on this ground on the thirtieth of March arrived at about eleven in the morning with seven vehicles a week and nowhere to stand, **the largest practical question anybody has brought up that bank in nine weeks and not in a book.** The answer took four minutes and was given from a doorway: **you are asking me to put seven carts on the ground my water stands on. I have been shut by a river for eleven years and I know what it is to have a thing come and stand on you, and the answer is no, and I would like it entered that you asked me in four minutes and I answered in four seconds and that the two are not the same length and that mine was faster.** He did not argue, and said in a yard that a man who is refused in four seconds learns something a man refused in two hours does not. **The finding is that a Road House with seven vehicles a week and a stabling yard given up in the fourth month has a foreman with nowhere to send them, and that the only offer anybody in this district has made him is a yard on the tide lent once a month for a rehearsal, entered as not a security.** The foreman, of twenty-two years: **I have a yard once a month for a rehearsal and no yard for a cart. And the platform I built is a place a train stops at and not a place a cart stands at. The man who posted the salvage to make that platform knew it on the evening of the twenty-first, and I worked it out at about eleven at night on the twenty-seventh, and I would like both of those entered and I would like it entered that the second one is nine days after the first.** Then the half of Selik Marne's third thing nobody did anything about, which is the half about the dark: the platform is thirty feet long and four feet of face and two feet above a shoulder with a drop of nine feet behind it, and on the twenty-seventh there is chalk at the height of a hand saying THE NINTH PLACE, a lamp anybody can light, a rail along the front edge, and nothing at all along the back. **The back edge is a drop, and there is nothing on that side of the platform at all, and that sentence is the whole of what the twenty-seventh settled.** Nobody argued, and the reason nobody argued is that a man of about thirty-four who mends fencing had been in four rooms in nine weeks about this bank and had said in each of them that he could not get a person off that platform at night, and had been thanked once and had asked for the thanking to be entered. The rail along the back edge was cut on the twenty-seventh out of the ends of the kerb rail, took two men about three hours, cost nothing, and **the finding is that a rail is not a security and is not a mark and is not a job. A rail is the only instrument in this district's history that keeps a person on a thing without carrying anybody's name, and nobody has ever written that down.** The paperer wrote it in nine words on the back of a sheet she was going to throw away and kept the sheet. A woman of forty-four who keeps a Road House on the tide asked for one word to be added: **say that a rail is free, and say it plainly, because in about four years somebody will write that a firm paid for the thing a person holds on a platform, and the firm that did it gave up a lease in March.** And on the evening of the twenty-seventh the fourth bearer came to the ninth place for the first time in nineteen years, at his own cost, which is two days a month in somebody else minding a pumping engine, and the entry says he did not have to and came. He carried his own book: a pocket book with a perished rubber band, nine hundred lines, four words on each, **FRAME TURNED. NOTHING SENT.** He put it on the table and nobody asked him to, and the room read about two hundred lines in six minutes. To the keeper of the only pair: you are the woman who copied that line out nine hundred times. And you never asked what the frame turned. **I never asked what the frame turned and I never once asked who turned it, and I have said that in a room in Alder Reach and I am saying it in my own hut, and the reason is that a person with a lever and a book is a person with a document, and there was nobody in nine miles who would have asked me about a document and not made me into something.** The minute carries that in her words for the second time and enters that a man had heard it in a room in Alder Reach and came two miles to hear it in a hut as well. He was asked in the five things in about nine minutes and gave the first thing in his own words, the same nine words as on the sixteenth, and said he would not say a different thing twice. The second thing got eleven words: **THE RIVER COMES UP OVER THE DRAINAGE CUT AND I CANNOT MAKE IT GO DOWN.** **That is the whole of what I know about the ground. Nineteen years and a lever twice a month and I have never once been able to say what the frame is for, and I know one thing about the water, and it is the only sentence in nine hundred lines that is not four words.** And then the signalman of about thirty-one asked him who the two other people were going to be. I do not know them. **You will be told on the night, or you will not be told at all, and both of those are worse for you than being told in advance, and I am not going to argue for it and I am going to lose, and I would like it entered now that I argued for it and lost.** Nobody argued, and **the finding is the one the tenth hand said out loud in a counting room on the evening of the twenty-seventh: TWO PEOPLE WHO KNOW IN ADVANCE WILL AGREE WITH YOU.** He said that was correct, and that he had argued for the wrong thing on purpose because he had not thought of the right one. And the twenty-eighth is where the night got named, **and it was not named by the man who was going to do it, and the finding is that a reader who says he will read only in an emergency has published the exact minute of the emergency and cannot take it back.** The clerk of the ward market asked him in four seconds, in a counting room with about thirty people in it, whether he intended to read the iron. I do not know. **Then say it in that order in the book, because a man who does not know is a man anybody can ask, and a man who says he will not is a man who has already answered, and the second one of those is a thing this district has been trying to get out of people for nine weeks and you have just given it away for nothing.** On the twenty-eighth, in about four minutes, he gave the other answer, the longest thing he has said in nine weeks: **I will read it once, and only the iron, and only if the movement cannot be made without it, and by movement I mean the throwing of the points of that loop. And I want the hour written down and I want the minute written down, and I want them on a page anybody can read. And the reason is that a man who says he will only use an instrument in an emergency has told this district where the emergency is, and there is no way to say that and then not have it be true. I would rather everybody knew the exact moment this line pays for a reading than have it be a surprise at three in the morning with nine vehicles on a bank.** The hour is the eleventh of this month at night, the minute is the minute the points of the loop are thrown, the page went into the public lot book that evening, and the reader at the counter read it out on the morning of the twenty-ninth to about fifty people. A woman of about sixty with a stick asked whether a page that says when is the same as a page that says why. It is not, **and this one says when, and the why is on a different page, and the why is that a bracket is the only part of this that is inside a locked frame, and nobody in this district knows what the iron says until somebody stands in front of it and reads it, and the reading may be wrong, and nine vehicles will already be on the loop.** Nobody wrote anything under that heading and the minute says nobody wrote anything under it, **and that is the first empty space anybody has left in this district's books on purpose.** The party went up on the afternoon of the twenty-eighth and the last thing that happened was the paperer walking the length of the platform in the dark with a lamp she lit herself, twice, and finding it both times: **that is not a rehearsal and I am not going to call it one, and the reason is that the fourth one was on the ground yesterday and this is one woman with a lamp and a hook, and the only thing it found out is that a person can find thirty feet of board at two in the morning, and I would like that written down before somebody comes up here in September and puts four coppers in that column and tells me a platform is a thing you get off a train at.** The boy who keeps the index is seventeen, is not Adrian Vale's apprentice, and wrote the figure in a hand that is not anybody else's: the train had stood a hundred and thirty-seven days.

## Chapter 0097 - The Twenty-Ninth of June
The twenty-ninth of June, opened on eleven coppers counted onto a table in a counting room in Alder Reach at about eight in the morning, and the woman who was going to be paid them put one of them back and had it counted again. **Eleven is for reading a sheet out loud and for a reading-back. Eleven is not for a day, and I am going up that bank anyway on the twenty-ninth because I have been going up it on the twenty-ninth since the flood year, and if the eleven is for the day then I have been paid for a day a hundred times and nobody has ever told me.** She is Jessa Olwin, about thirty-eight, who buys old paper off four houses once a month, has been going up that bank for nine years, and keeps the toll board at the fourth place on the week she is on that bank for her own trade; she is not a signal hand, she does not know what a pair is, and she has wanted both of those on the board since the ninth of last month. The eleven came out of the unassigned thirty, **and the entry notes that this is the first thing in eleven weeks the unassigned thirty has been asked to do.** The party left at half past four in the morning, in rain that had been falling since the fourth, with the river coming up on the ninth place's column for a week and the column's mark under for six days. The paperer's own figure went into the book before anybody left, in her own hand: **six hours and a quarter going and five and a half coming is eleven and three-quarters in the day, which is ten and three-quarters of walking and an hour of standing still, and the six and a quarter is not a guess. It is what a loaded cart did on the twenty-second of this month and I wrote it down at the time.** The district carries nine hours for the day in a sentence the rehearsal of the twenty-first of last month found wrong, and the figure on the sheet is hers. **The entry for the twenty-ninth is that this is the first time anybody walked that bank for a document and not for a line, and the first time the document was a rate sheet with a figure on it, and the first time the people being asked knew what the line cost before they were asked.** And then the finding that is not about the two women: **a timetable with a train on it cannot be printed until somebody settles what the fourth place is, and four people tried at half past six in the morning and did not.** The first timetable runs from the ninth to the twenty-third of this month and every day on it is a day on which nothing was going to happen; it carries the ninth place and the east end and does not carry the fourth place, **and the reason on the sheet is not a decision: a station needs somebody at it, and there is nobody at the fourth place, and a keeper of a board is not a person anybody can be sent to.** So the fourth place has a toll board and no station, and a train that reaches three stations will put people down on a shoulder two miles west of a platform with nobody standing on it. **The finding of the thirtieth is that a station with nobody at it is the fourth thing this district does not have, and that it has now got a name for the other three.** The three it does not have are all in books: a way of saying that a toll lands on a place, printed as an empty column with nobody's name on it; a column that is a place, which a licensed valuer's certificate does not have in twenty-nine columns; and an office, spelled out on all of the same things and not findable in any register this basin can reach. Nobody put the fourth on the list; the registrar entered that the list has three and the district has four, and **a person attached to it is a boy of nineteen who will be there and who has not been asked and who says he is nobody's keeper.** The same hour produced the other thing nobody had asked about: the oil for the lamp comes off a card with a man's name on it since the third of this month, and a trader of fifty-one on the lower terrace has been paying out of her own till for the Harrow works since the first of last month and has asked twice that it be said nobody asked her either. **Two cards in this district name a person instead of a firm, and both of them were found by accidents, and neither of them was found by anybody going looking for a card.** And at a rail in Alder Reach on the morning of the twenty-eighth, on a published day, a bridge administrator said two things about a rate sheet being read out to a village. He is Selik Marne, not on the walk, not up that bank in nine years, and he refused the sheet in four seconds: **I am the office that has been paying a hundred and eighty coppers a quarter on the Harrow works for nine years, and if a sheet is read out to thirty-one people because somebody finally asked them, then the office that never asked them is not a place a sheet goes to. Put me last. Put me last on every sheet for four years and see whether anybody notices.** And the second thing: **that is not enough. And I am going to keep saying it on published days for as long as I hold this post, and nobody in this district believes I will stop and I am not going to stop, and the only thing that has changed since April is that the sentence has more facts in it and the facts do not make it shorter.** Nobody argued with him, and the minute enters that nobody argued, and that he would rather be last on a sheet than first in a room, and that nobody in the room believed him and that the tenth hand wrote it down as a thing that had been said rather than a thing that was true. And then the rate sheet was read out in the front room of the second house before any question was asked of anybody, in nine minutes, and nobody in that room had ever heard a rate sheet read out in the room it was made for, including the people it was made for. Fifty coppers a quarter over four thousand and one households; fourteen the line, six printed matter, thirty never assigned; two hundred coppers a quarter collected on the seventh of last month out of a pail of change by a man who does not read figures and who was given the figure and not the reason; **six of it on lettering, eleven on a bound copy of the line book, and a hundred and eighty-three on nothing**, which is Chapter 82's sum and the only version of it that adds up; the four coppers for the bushel of white paint are on a line of their own and are not out of the two hundred, and nobody has found out whose they are. **The hundred and eighty-three on nothing was the sentence that stopped the room, and it was not the figure and it was not the two hundred, and the finding of the twenty-ninth is that a district will sit in a front room at eleven at night and be made silent by an amount of money that went nowhere.** The woman at the column asked for the reading-back, the paperer gave it, and then asked one question: who is owed the hundred and eighty-three. Nobody could answer and nobody had ever asked. **A market has entered that it will do a reading when it sends anybody up that bank, and a yes without a date is a yes that can be asked again, and the twenty-ninth of this month is the date, and it went in the book in a clerk's hand.** Getting thirty-one people into one front room has its own entry: four households came as four households and nineteen came as nineteen, and the nineteen were counted at the door by the tenth hand with a pencil on the back of a sheet, and nobody in that room knew that was a count until he said it out loud. **You are all being entered as thirty-one, and there are four of you who are households and there are nineteen of you who are not, and I am entering those as two things and not one, because if I enter them as one then a stranger can take this entry cold and read one number, and the one number is a household, and a household is the thing this whole bank was built not to be.** The trader of fifty-one on the lower terrace said the only thing she said that night, and she said it to him and not to the room: **then the two hundred coppers a quarter is one number and there are four households on it, and I am one of the four and I did not know that until the sixth of this month when a man read it out of a pail of change, and if the sheet had been read out in this room in May then a person would have known in May.** Nobody knew in May. **The sheet was read out in this room on the twenty-ninth at about four in the afternoon, and the woman who asked for it said in May that she had been waiting eleven years for somebody to ask her a question about water that was not about how much of it there is, and what she got instead was a figure and a column and a bushel of white paint.** The asking took forty-four minutes and nineteen of them were the sixth question, **and the finding of the twenty-ninth is that the method did not get cheaper the third time anybody used it. It got dearer.** April: fifty-one minutes with eleven of them, by the tenth hand, who cannot be paid. May: thirty-nine with nine of them, by a clerk of the ward market who said in a room that it would not be her again, and it has not been her, and a clerk is a person and a person can be replaced and that is the point of her. June: forty-four with nineteen of them, by a paperer whose whole trade is being handed other people's documents without being asked for them. The sixth question is read aloud for the first time and it has never been named: that the toll on this line is the origin and the origin is a place and not a person; that one hundred and forty-one movements in nine hundred and six have named this place as the origin held; that a toll on a place takes memory of departure and nobody in this district has ever paid it in copper; that nobody in the room can state what one of those hundred and forty-one is, including the woman who keeps the book, because she was eleven feet above the water at the time; that the word being asked about ends on the twenty-ninth of July and not on a feeling; and that a person may answer no twice and be asked twice, and that a no is a column and not a door. **The reason the sixth question took nineteen minutes is that this is the first of the three askings in which the two people being asked knew what the line costs.** The woman at the column, in the middle of it: **I have always known what the toll is and I have never once known what the amount is, and there are four people standing in this room who know the amount and none of them can state the toll, and the two halves of that sentence have been in this district in two books since the eighth of last month and nobody has joined them.** The registrar was not in the room; a clerk of the ward market, who was not there either, wrote it down afterwards from the paperer's own hand and did not join the two halves, and gave the reason she gives every time. Both said yes, for a fourth month, and the order of the two sentences has not changed since the twenty-ninth of March: **I do not like it, and I say yes.** A yes is not a hold and a hold is not a term, and a term is not a promise, and this district has spent nine weeks learning them one at a time and has not yet learned to write all four on one line. And then the keeper of the hut did the thing she attached in April, and nobody asked her to, and about thirty people watched it. Corvin Sallow is thirty-nine, a signalman of a bridge administration, in that hut on a term of thirty days since the twenty-fifth of April which the office renewed without asking, not paid, keeping a toll board in the hours nobody is on a bank, not a bearer, and has said in three rooms that a keeper of a board is not a person anybody can be sent to. He said the nine-word word out loud at about ten minutes to twelve, got four of the nine right, and the woman of the column said the fifth one for him and then stopped, the same as on the sixth, and he said the rest himself. **The condition she attached in April was that the man in the hut has to be able to say the word and she will teach him and he says it in front of her once before anybody else. If he cannot say it like a person then she will not hold it a second month by itself, and he said it, and the minute records that it took him about a second and a half, and that she then said yes to the fourth month, and that the order of those two things was the word first and the yes second, and that the order was hers and not the room's.** Nobody wrote anything under the heading that says NOT ANSWERED except the two lines there since the thirteenth, and one of them had stopped being true, and the registrar entered the change in the margin with a date and no comment. **THE PERSON IS A PAPERER AND SHE IS NOT ASKED YET.** She was asked on the twenty-ninth and she was asked first and she is a paperer, and the line is struck and the strike has a date on it, and the woman of forty-five is not asked and is in the count as not asked and is not named in that room and was not in the room. And then the tenth hand read the whole of the twenty-ninth out loud in that front room, in the ordinary voice, standing up, in about thirteen minutes: **it is the fifth time anybody has read an entry out on the day it was made, and the first time it has been read out to the people it was made about.** Reading a stranger's sentence back is the paperer's trade and the reading-back in the method is the tenth hand's, and Jessa Olwin read the sheet and the tenth hand read the answer, and the answer was in his own hand and not in hers. **The minute says that the man who wrote the answer and the woman who asked the question were in the same room and that neither of them wrote the other's words down. This is the first time in nine weeks that two people have kept two halves of one entry in two hands in one room, and neither of them has said a word about it, and the reader at the ward market counter wrote down on the thirtieth that six people have now seen that room and that she is still not going to ask.** And the woman at the column asked for one more thing, the first request she has made in eleven years that anybody could have refused: **write the day it ends on the board on the platform. I do not want it in a book nine hours away.** She put her hand flat on the boards. **I want a person standing on thirty feet of board at eleven at night to be able to put a hand on a board and find out that this word of mine runs out on a day.** And somebody chalked the date on the kerb rail at about half past ten that night. **The finding is that a date is the only line anybody has ever put on that board that nobody is allowed to argue with, and it took a woman who has been the water here for eleven years to find that out about a piece of chalk.** And the whole of what the twenty-ninth added is four sentences long, then handed to somebody who was not him to enter, and read back by a woman of about thirty-eight: a rotation that has turned once has been shown that it can turn; **a rotation that has turned twice has been shown it can turn twice, and the two turnings cost a man a day he was not paid for and a woman a day and a night. A day and a night is a wage, and a wage is a thing you stop. The third turning cost a paperer of about thirty-eight nothing at all, because she was going up that bank on the twenty-ninth for four houses and had been going up it on the twenty-ninth for nine years. A thing which turns at no cost is not a rotation, and this district now has three samples of a rotation and no rotation and one subscription, and the difference between those two words is the whole of what a boundary is.** And she proposed the fix, in one sentence, and the room entered it without voting on it: **ask me next month from the counting room and I will come down, and ask the keeper from the yard on the tide, and neither of them has to walk.** She was quiet for about four seconds. **Then write down that a boundary held by two people who are each asked from a different room is a boundary either room can end, because I would rather be ended by a room than by a flood.** And the party walked to the west end of the platform at about ten at night with eleven lamps and about forty people. The train was where it has been for a hundred and thirty-eight days, and the man of sixty-eight got into the cab of the first vehicle and came out in about two minutes and said one word to the foreman of twenty-two years. The word was *ready*. Nobody entered the word and everybody heard it. The iron was on the bench under the window in a bag and it was brought out into the wet and put on the boards of the platform and left there, because the frame is at the east end of that loop and the bracket is in the frame, and the frame has a door and the door has a lock, and the key is in the keeping of the only pair on that line. **And the reading of the twenty-ninth is the first entry in this district's history that was read out three times on three days by three people who are not the reader, and the finding of the thirtieth is that a thing read out three times stops being a record and becomes a habit, and this district does not have a word for the good kind of habit and is not going to get one out of this.**

## Chapter 0098 - What the Iron Said
The twenty-ninth of June into the thirtieth, opened on a man writing nine words on a sheet of paper on a platform thirty feet long in the rain at about half past ten at night, and the nine words are the reason the reading was for, and he read them out afterwards in the ordinary voice to about forty people. A woman of about sixty with a stick had asked for them on the twenty-eighth at a counter: I want the why on a page and not only the when. I can do that. **Then write it, because in about four years somebody is going to find that page and read the when, and if there is no why on it they will decide that you did it on purpose, and you did not, and they will be half right.** The nine words: **A bracket is the only part of this that is locked, and nobody knows what the iron says until a person reads it, and the reading may be wrong, and nine vehicles will already be on the loop.** The reading was done at eleven o'clock at night on the boards of the platform, with about forty people on the shoulder, one lamp at the west end and one at the east end, nobody on the ballast, the frame locked, the key in the keeping of the only pair on that line — and the batch's one System panel says: a reading may be spent on one lot you have touched, it costs about an hour and a cost, the cost is a part of that lot's toll; the toll on this iron is the origin, an origin takes memory of departure, you have paid it twice and you have two left, and two is a number and not a principle; the iron does not hold places and will not name one, and it will not tell you whether the bracket takes it; a reading moves nothing on that line, **and a reading also does not make a man able to do a thing he could not do an hour ago. It is not a reward and it is not a stage.** It came back in one line, in the form of March and the form of April, word for word the form both of them took, in his own hand: **THE BRACKET IS CUT FOR IT AND THE BRACKET WILL TAKE IT.** Nobody had been told what that would sound like and about fourteen people said afterwards that they had expected something longer. **The cost was specific to that lot's toll and was not the cost of the twenty-seventh of April repeated. An origin takes memory of departure.** In March it took a direction. In April it took the memory of which gate he went out of his own city by, on the seventeenth of February at six in the morning, and it had not come back. This time it took the twenty-second of last month at about six in the morning, when he left Alder Reach to walk to the ninth place for the first time in nine weeks, got as far as the second changeover, and turned back and came down the bank again. He wrote it down himself before he could stand up; the woman who keeps the only pair read it back, because that is her job on a term of thirty days and not as a courtesy, and the entry is in her hand and not in his, and **the finding of the thirtieth is that the first time in nine weeks that a cost has been entered in somebody else's hand, the somebody was the keeper of the pair, and a keeper of a pair is not a person anybody can be sent to, and the two halves of that are now on the same line.** He has one left, and the figure is in the book as a figure and not as a principle. The echo is a loss of spatial sense: a man with it cannot tell which way a river is going and cannot hold a pair, and **Corvin Sallow took him by the arm at about ten past midnight and walked him thirty feet to the door of the hut and would not let go of him until he was on the step, and the entry says in both their hands that the man who was walked said the word *which* four times on the way and could not finish any of the sentences.** And then the security was posted and the split was closed, and neither has ever happened in this district, and the entry is four paragraphs long and is in the public lot book and on the fourth toll board in the same words. A security is a thing somebody else can go and look at; that is the definition this district worked out in May and it is the only one it has. His is a term of labour and a term of stock, and not one thing of either that anybody could call a recognition. The labour is one day a week at this place from the first hour of the morning until the hour a train is due to move, from the twenty-ninth of this month to the twenty-ninth of next month, **and the day is written on the fourth toll board in chalk, and anybody may go and stand on that platform on that day and see whether he is on it, and nobody is paid for it and he is not paid for it.** The stock is three bound copies of the line book at eleven coppers each, thirty-three coppers, out of his own hand: one on a shelf in the hut at this place, one in the ward market counting room, one in the Needle Road House, and anybody may go and look at all three, and any of the four people who can revoke this charter may send any of them anywhere. And the entry says in its own words: it is not his recognition and not his mark and not anything he has accumulated in nine weeks; it is one day a week and three bound copies of a line book; it ends on the twenty-ninth of July; **a transferable right is not a neutral right, the week moves, whoever holds it did not ask for it and the people it lands on were not asked; he has gained a responsibility and not an authority, he is not one of the four bearers and not a keeper of a board, and the line on page one saying he is not one of the four bearers is the first line in this district's history that is still true of him four months after it was written; this charter can be revoked by four people who do not like him and none of them needs his permission, and the four are the three who hold the weeks and the man who ends the use; and a person who can be replaced has not been worked out and nobody in this district knows how to do it, and that is entered as not known and not as a delay.** Two fresh witnesses who do not travel accepted it: a boy of seventeen who keeps an index, is paid four coppers a quarter and is not Adrian Vale's apprentice, and a woman of about sixty with a stick who is on no roll and is not in a row. The two who were on the bank were not asked to witness and the two who were not on the bank were, and **the finding of the thirtieth is that a witness is a person who will be somewhere else when the reason is read out, and that this district has been choosing its witnesses out of the people who walk for a living for nine weeks and has never once asked whether they would be there.** He could do it. Nobody made a ceremony and there was no panel, and the minute says in his own words that he noticed the moment and could not say what he had noticed, and the clerk wrote *the stage is not a moment* in the margin and underlined it twice, and he asked for both underlines to stay. And there is a thing about the twenty minutes between the closing and the throw that four people wrote down and none of them put in a book, and it is that he sat down on the boards of his own platform for about two minutes and said nothing, and he has been asked twice since and given the same answer both times, nine words long: **I had been told no four times and I did it once, and I wanted two minutes of not being asked anything, and I have not had that since the eighth of March, and there is a man on that bank who has had it every day of his life for nineteen years and I understand now what it is for.** The iron went into the bracket at about twenty minutes to midnight, the only pair on that line put it in and nobody else was allowed to touch it, and the throw of the points was at about a quarter to twelve. Nine vehicles standing for a hundred and thirty-eight days came out of a siding facing west and went out backwards and round that loop and came out onto the bank facing east, and it took forty minutes, and nobody in this district had ever watched it happen. The engine had not been steamed since the flood year and a man of sixty-eight had it ready at about nine o'clock, and the first thing anybody heard was not the engine; it was eleven men on a shoulder shouting one word at each other over the sound of rain on a platform, and then it was the engine. **The ninth vehicle cleared the points at about twenty minutes to twelve, and the boy of seventeen wrote the time down, and it is the only time in nine hundred and six movements in that line book that the ninth vehicle is the ninth vehicle, and the finding of the thirtieth is that a line book has had a column for the number of a vehicle since the flood year and nobody had ever had a reason to write in it.** The train went east and stopped at the fourth place at about ten past midnight, where there is a toll board and no platform and no station, and four people got off it onto a shoulder in the dark, three of them having asked to be put down there and one not asked and in the count as not asked. One of the three was a man of about thirty-four who mends fencing, and he had said in four rooms in nine weeks that he could not get a person off a place in the dark, and he got off one, and a boy of nineteen who is nobody's keeper walked him two hundred yards to a gate with a lamp he did not have lit and did not light, and the entry says the boy did it because the gate is the fourth of four houses a paperer buys paper off and the boy knows it. It stopped at the east end at about half past midnight, eleven minutes' walk from the ward market counter, and a clerk of that market stood in the rain on a platform she had not asked for and watched nine vehicles go past her and out into the yards where they had been standing for nine years. **And then it came back, and it came back to this place. The run was two movements and the second one was east, and the toll was charged once, and the origin was the ninth place, and the ninth place had been asked, and not one of the hundred and forty-one had ever been asked before that.** And the thing this district has wanted for nine weeks did not happen, and the thing that did happen was written down with a date, the twenty-ninth of June, on the board at the platform in chalk and on the boy of seventeen's index and in the public lot book in Alder Reach and in the water office's own book, and the four copies are not joined. A reversal leaves a stall where the train stood; every one of the hundred and forty-one left one; the index has twelve stalls in it and the line book has eleven, and the difference is a stall with no signal in it, ninety-four yards long, measured on the second morning of May by a signalman who had a tape because that is what a signalman has. The boy of seventeen had the tape on the night of the twenty-ninth and walked the shoulder twice before and twice after: **the difference is no longer a length. The difference is nothing. The index has thirteen stalls in it and the line book has thirteen, and the one that was missing is the one at this place, and it is thirty-one feet long, and the platform is thirty feet long, and nobody in this district had ever put those two lengths on the same line.** And then the part that is not the finding, which he wrote underneath it and the registrar has asked not be joined to the part above: **and the stall did not walk west. It is where the train stood and it is still there, and nobody went and looked for it after the fourth day, and a man with a tape walked the ninety-four yards and the two hundred yards and the nine hundred feet of embankment beyond the drainage cut and found nothing that moved in four hours.** In a yard in Alder Reach at about eleven in the morning on the thirtieth, to about sixty people: **nobody knows why. A district that has measured a thing and does not know why is in a different position from a district that has not measured it, and the difference is about four hours of walking and a tape, and it is not about the ground.** And at about twenty past one in the morning the water came up over the far leg of that loop, and the fourth bearer ended the use. He is Elias Rooke, sixty-one, who has held a pair on this line for thirty-four years and has kept the frame at the twelfth place since the fourth year, and he had been in the hut with two other people in it since about ten o'clock and did not know until the moment who the two other people were going to be. He put his hand on the lever and reversed what was running and said the reason out loud, and the reason went into a public book, and it is nine words and it is not about the water: **A THING THAT HAS STOOD FOR A HUNDRED AND THIRTY-EIGHT DAYS IS NOT ENDED BY A FLOOD.** The man of sixty-eight, from the ballast: that is a good reason. The fourth bearer: **it is the only reason I have got. I have been on my own in a hut for nineteen years and I have never had a reason in writing before tonight, and I have written nine words and I would like somebody to tell me if nine is too many.** Nobody told him, and the minute enters that nobody told him, and that the reason went on the fourth toll board at about half past two in the morning in chalk in a hand that is not anybody else's in this district, and that it is the twenty-first sentence on that board. Nobody on that shoulder knew what they had been standing in the middle of, and the entry says so in a line a clerk of nineteen years wrote and did not soften: about forty people watched nine vehicles come out of a siding and go round a loop in the dark in the rain because a man they had all said no to had said he would read an iron and then had not said no. **Nineteen of the forty were people who are not in a household and are not in no row and are not counted anywhere including on the sheet that collects the four thousand and one, and they were on that shoulder because somebody held a door, and the finding is that the nineteen were the only people there who had not been asked anything about it and were the only ones who did not appear to want to have been.** One of them, a man of about forty-one who has been at this place three years and is not on any roll, said at about ten minutes to midnight the only sentence anybody said that night that was not about a railway: **I do not know what that was. I have been standing on a bank in the rain since about eight o'clock and a thing went round and I could not tell you what it was and I am not going to pretend to anybody in the morning that I could.** The tenth hand wrote it down in his own hand and it went in the book in his own hand, and **the finding of the thirtieth is that this is the first sentence anybody in this district has said about a railway that was not a finding, and it was said by a person who is not in a household, and it went into the public lot book before anything else that night did.** And the toll landed on the ninth place at about a quarter to two in the morning and nobody there felt it, **and the finding of the thirtieth is that the cost of a thing is always paid by somebody who is not the person who caused it and is always the last thing anybody looks for.** The keeper of the book of departures is fifty-one and has been eleven feet above that flood line for a year, and she and the woman of the column went through the book together from about half past two until about four in the morning with a lamp, in the rain, page by page. One hundred and forty-one out of one hundred and forty-one was what this place could produce at eight o'clock the evening before; one hundred and forty out of one hundred and forty-two is what it can produce now. **The one it cannot produce is the one they made, and neither of the two women knows that anything has happened, and the difference between those two facts is the whole of what a toll on a place is.** Forty people were on that platform or in that room or on that shoulder, and not one of them can say afterwards what the nine vehicles looked like going round, and two of them were on the ballast at the far end with their faces turned and were looking straight at it.
