The four things are read out a second time by a different man.

## Chapter 0128 - What She Was Offering
The eleventh to the fourteenth of October. **An agent of a guarantee house comes over the raised plank road with two ledgers and does not hide what she is offering.** **She was on the far bank for the ninth and the tenth, four miles off this flat, and about nine people here heard on those two days that a guarantee house had a person coming and did not go and look.** She gives her name and will not let it be entered; the clerk enters *an agent of a guarantee house*; **Hester Vail is in no register in the west bank.** Her ledger is nineteen rows and four columns and it is a lot, and three of the nineteen have a name in the first column and sixteen do not, and she would rather have a blank column than a name a person cannot take out. She offers to take twenty-one years off the book and **to put a clerk in that room with a wage and a day off**, and the price is **first refusal on the person who stands in the frame**. The third thing on the sheet is free and she made it free on purpose so a room would count three and not one. A woman of forty-four who has done it eleven years answers in four sentences and the fourth is the finding: ours is four things I will not sign, and the difference is that I have said no in public and you have said yes nineteen times in a book and you have called it a ledger. The room does not say yes and does not say no. **She refuses the name of the place where the sixteen came from, says it is not a courtesy, and the heading is in the room and is not read. One hundred and forty-one questions in three days, about nine hours a day of them.** Adrian is thirty-four miles east for all of it and never meets her.

## Chapter 0129 - Nobody Knows What It Is Called
The fifteenth to the eighteenth of October. He reads the sheet in a room over a shop at the fourth place and writes four words under it that are in no book. **There are still five boards, and the number of instruments this district has built and not named is still six and the number of things it does not have is still five, and neither moved.** The two lines ruled and empty since the twentieth of August are full and they are a boy of nineteen's, and he is not a keeper. Three people are asked what the thing is called and give three answers and a clerk enters all three and chooses none, and a haulier of about forty says *bill* and then *confessional* and then says he had said the word once in his life. Six hands, six lines, none of them a keeper. **The board is used four times in eleven days to stop a person who has not been asked anything, and the count of the four was made by a person who is not paid and was not asked to be right.** A man of thirty-eight says a board with four lines on it and no name on it is a notice until somebody can say what a notice is for, and that *used for* is not *what it is for*.

## Chapter 0130 - The Right to Be Neutral
The nineteenth to the twenty-first of October. **The entry goes into the market's own book and the word steward is in it at the request of nine households and not at the request of the man named in it**, and a reader at the counter says the word out loud for the first time in nine years and asks that it be noticed that it is in the entry. Nine households are asked one question each in turn and one did not understand the difference between an address and a remedy and understood it the second time, and it is the ninth household, which is the one that said it would rather have the four hundred coppers. He says the cost of it in a yard: **the right to be neutral, which was worth the ability to be wrong about a thing without anybody noticing, and four people noticed inside nine minutes, and he asks for that to be entered as a credit to them.** A man of fifty-six enters that the other eight are four people who stand in frames, the woman of forty-four, the man of thirty-four, a clerk of nineteen years and himself, and that none of them asked for the name and none of them was asked afterwards. A woman who has done it eleven years says nobody has said what happens when one of the four of them stops, and that it is a thing for the thirty-first of March and not for tonight. The train has stood two hundred and fifty-two days and the boy of seventeen's index still carries five dates and a rule under the fifth.

## Chapter 0131 - The Columns

The 22nd to the 24th of October. A man of about thirty-four who digs loam and does nine days of it in ten, refused a guild form on the twenty-seventh of September, comes back to a room and says he is not saying no to a book. A mason of about fifty comes for loam for the second time in a month and does not find any and says he is not saying a channel took it; a clerk enters that the two occasions are not joined. A man of fifty-six proposes a memory ledger. **Adrian is asked to design it and refuses in four seconds, and the reason is nineteen words and it is his own: a book the man who can be found builds is a book about him and not about the year.** The six column headings are written out; the sixth, whether they were in the room, is argued for two hours, lost twice and saved by the man who refused the form. A man of twenty-six asks twice for his row to carry a year and a half and is refused twice. The guild form's twenty-nine headings are read out: two of the six are on it and twenty-three have nothing about a year, and the clerk entered *counted* and not *left over*. Six people agree to hold the ledger; the fourth of the six is the man who digs loam.

## Chapter 0132 - Before the First of April

The 24th to the 26th of October. The room finds it does not know how many rows the ledger has, and that no count on the flat is a count of people who have stood in a frame. A man of about thirty-eight who deals in second-hand paper writes the notice form, will not sign it, and refuses the words *release* and *consent* because a form is a thing people use and not a thing people read. The council meets on the twenty-sixth; the man who can be found is thirty-four miles west and was not asked to come. **The woman of forty-four who has done it eleven years says the finding: a worker who says no on the last day of a season is a worker who has already paid.** The clause changes four times and carries no date for giving the notice; a man of fifty-six says the interval out loud as a hundred and fifty-one days and twenty-one weeks and four days, and a woman of fifty-eight who carries water and does not read figures calls it five months, and the two are the time it takes the wrong person to find out. A man of twenty-six says do not put the year on the notice. **A woman of thirty-one in the third channel asks for her own name to go in the ledger, and the clerk enters that this and the agent's sixteen blank bearer columns are not joined.**

## Chapter 0133 - Four Hours on Eleven Boards

The 26th to the 29th of October. Six people and a clerk fill eleven rows of a page ruled to thirty-one in four hours; twenty rows are ruled and empty and are not a failure. The fifth column is NOTHING SAID for two of the six, and a clerk enters that nothing said is not nothing happened. The third column is not totalled, in nineteen words: a column of years is not a column of money and a year does not go twice. **The second column, WHICH FRAME, is the only one of the six that cannot be checked at all, because the four who counted on the second of October named the frames instead of numbering them; one cell in it holds two names with a stroke between them and a clerk entered the stroke because a clerk who leaves a mark off the page has entered a mark and not a name.** **The seventh row is not a year: NINE DAYS IN TEN, AND THE TENTH IS UNDER TWO FEET OF WATER AND I HAVE NO BOOTS THAT KEEP IT OUT.** The eleventh row is four empty columns and NOT ASKED in the sixth. The man who prints declines to price a sheet about six things. A stranger takes a copy cold on the twenty-ninth and understands the sixth column without being told.

## Chapter 0134 - A Notice Withdraws a Season

The 29th to the 31st of October. **The batch's public failure.** The first withdrawal notice in this district is filled in by a man of twenty-six on the thirtieth and it names a season that never started. He asks what he is withdrawing from; nobody answers for nine seconds; asked properly in the six things he says yes and then says there is no season and that a certificate is in a drawer thirty-four miles east. A man of thirty-four says a stranger reads a notice with a name and a date on it and reads a man who was told about a season, and that a guild form has one column that is consent. A man of fifty-two is asked whether he wants a notice and says no in four words. The notice is withdrawn on the thirty-first; the entry carries a date, a person, a reason and no name. **A man of about thirty-four who digs loam says a ledger is what happened and a right is what somebody did not let happen, and he does not rule a column for it.**

## Chapter 0135 - A Frame Is Oak

The 1st to the 3rd of November, and the month turns. The second form is written in four hours with a person, a frame and a date on one line in the middle of the sheet and no season on it. **The hole is named: a frame is oak and a frame is the last thing that happens to a person, and the channel is where the year comes out of.** A woman of fifty-eight who carries water tells the room what happened at the third house up from the sluice in the fifth year. The woman of forty-four says that on the thirty-first of March a person is still standing in the thing. A frame measures four feet by three and the channel under it two feet wide and nine inches deep. A stranger asks what the sheet lets him do with the year, nobody answers for about nineteen seconds, and a man of thirty-four who mends fencing says the answer in seven words: **it lets you hold it. Nothing else.**

## Chapter 0136 - The Ninth Refusal

The 3rd to the 6th of November. Adrian is asked in the five things to read a frame so that a notice can say what it withdraws from, and refuses in four seconds: a reading takes about an hour, the cost is a part of the lot's toll, and the toll on this one is a year. The cost of the refusal is nothing. Ivo Stenn reads the ledger out and then his own nineteen words; the intervals have gone up on use for the fourth time in eleven weeks. **A guild inspector of forty-four comes thirty-four miles on his own money and says a withdrawal notice cannot be put on a guild form, and that he has been asked eleven times for a form and every one wanted a column in for one person.** He is then asked in a doorway what a public office was for and declines for the fourth time in eleven weeks.

## Chapter 0137 - The Cell That Is Empty

The 7th to the 9th of November. A man of twenty-six comes west at his own expense, has his row read to him, and finds the third cell empty and nobody having noticed in eleven days. **The batch's one System panel arrives unasked in the yard and is about the instrument: a ledger is a record of what a thing did; a year is entered once and the column does not decide when it went; a right to withdraw is a door in one direction; a person who has not been asked has not withdrawn; the man named is not a row.** He is asked to post a security on the notice and could have, and a man of thirty-four who mends fencing stops him with Adrian's own words from the ninth of October, and a man of fifty-six says a sentence said against one's own advantage in front of nineteen people is the only rule this district has that anybody believed. The man of twenty-six asks for his row to be struck and is refused.

## Chapter 0138 - A Slate in a Kitchen

The 9th to the 12th of November. **The romance moves one sentence and does not resolve, and for the first time it is in a kitchen and not in a room and there is no minute of it.** She says one sentence about four thousand and one households and one person; he answers with a date, the thirtieth of March, and not with an answer. A reader at a counter was told by him on the eleventh and was not asked whether she wanted to be told. **A man of about forty-four who has the second cut sends one line unasked, giving four seasons, and the sixth column cannot hold a person who was not asked and then wrote.** A correction reaches one copy of nine. A stranger reads the sixth column out loud in the yard and reads one cell wrong, and the fault is the page's.

## Chapter 0139 - What a Name Is Worth

The 13th to the 15th of November. **A man who sells on a tram arch and has not come into a yard in nine years walks into the yard of Lot 17, reads the ledger in eleven minutes and the sixth column twice, and prices the name at about a fifth without being able to tell a fifth of what.** He says he has never sold a year and cannot warrant one and cannot strike it out when it comes up short, and nobody can tell him whose it is. Adrian concedes in nine seconds that this is his trade's fault and then says the half that makes it true: a security is what a bidder pays for twice, the price of his is a year, and he cannot give it back. The ninth household answers a question about the name in four words. The man from the tram arch reads the twenty-one years out loud himself, takes no copy, asks for no correction and leaves at about eleven.

## Chapter 0140 - The Line Under the Eleventh Row

The 16th to the 21st of November. A line is ruled under the eleventh row and nothing is written under it. The second half of the word steward is entered as unfinished in nineteen words on the sixteenth of November: the first half is a word in a book, the second half is a season and has not started. The four seasons go into the eleventh row's third column and the sixth column is left saying NOT ASKED. **The board outside the room has one day left on it, the twenty-fourth of November, and nobody has written a fourth.** The man who is the address gives the same answer he gave on the twenty-first of October. The train has stood two hundred and eighty-three days. The last entry is eleven lines long and has no finding in it.
